# Changelog: November 2026

What changed this month for games built on Flint, newest first. How to read
it and how to add an entry: [CHANGELOG.md](../CHANGELOG.md).

## 2026-11-25

### A build with nothing changed no longer rebuilds flint-player and flint (#1338)
**What changed.** flint-player's build script told cargo to watch each package's `schemas/archetypes` and `schemas/scene` folder, and some packages have none (git keeps no empty folder). Cargo takes a missing watched path as changed, so every `cargo build` and every `flint build`, a change or none, recompiled flint-player and flint-cli: about 2 minutes in release, now 0.6 s. A new schema folder in a package is still seen, since the whole `packages/` folder is watched.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game's `build.bat` that bootstraps `flint build` with cargo should run cargo from inside `engine\`, as `flint build` does: the engine's `.cargo/config.toml` adds a linker flag that cargo reads only there, and a build from the game's root (`--manifest-path engine\Cargo.toml`) shares no compiled crate with one from inside, so the second builds everything again.

### List your packages, then build with `flint build` (#1337)
**What changed.** The book has a Packages chapter (concepts/packages.md): what a package is, the seven the engine has, `[packages]` in `flint.toml`, `flint package list`, `flint package used` and `flint build`. Each concepts chapter now names its package on its first line, and the Script API Reference groups its modules by package. A project made by `flint init` explains packages in its `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`, and builds with `flint build` instead of a bare `cargo build`. Nothing in the engine's behaviour changed: a game with no `[packages]` table still has every package.
**What to do.** To give a game only what it uses, run `flint package used --write` in the project. It adds a `[packages]` table to `flint.toml` that lists every package the scenes, prefabs and scripts reach. Check it with `flint check`, then run `flint build` (`--release` for optimized binaries) and use the `flint` and `flint-player` it prints from then on. In a game that carries the engine as a subtree, the first build is `cargo run --manifest-path engine/Cargo.toml -p flint-cli -- build --release`, run from the project root.

### `flint build`, and `unbuilt-package` (#1335)
**What changed.** `flint build [--release] [--target <triple>] [--dry-run]
[--engine-path <dir>]` builds a project's `flint-player` and `flint` with
the packages its `[packages]` lists: each package's `[build] features` on
whichever of flint-player and flint-cli has them, plus each crate's build
options (`audio`, `gamepad`, `frame-history`, `dialogs`), as `cargo build
--no-default-features --features ...` in the engine workspace (the
project's `engine/`, else the engine its schemas come from). It prints the
cargo command and where the binaries landed; `--dry-run` builds nothing. A
project that drops `debug-hud` gets its player built in a cargo run of its
own, since flint-cli turns that feature on. On Windows a binary that is
running is renamed to `<name>.old.exe` first, and each binary deletes its
old copy when it next starts. With a `[packages]` table, `flint play`,
`flint-player` and `flint render` refuse to run when the binary lacks a
listed package (exit 2, naming `flint build`), and `flint check` reports it
as a new `unbuilt-package` error. The error for a command whose package a
build left out now says to list the package and run `flint build`.
**What to do.** Replace a `build.bat` line such as `cargo build --release
--bin flint-player --bin flint` with `flint build --release`. A game with no
`[packages]` table builds every package, as before. The first build, before
any `flint` exists, is `cargo run -p flint-cli -- build` from the engine
folder.

### An examples index entry takes `packages`, and CI builds each package alone (#1336)
**What changed.** An `[[example]]` in an `index.toml` may say `packages = ["living"]`: the packages its scene needs beyond the kernel. `flint examples --check` on a flint built without one of them passes over that example by name (`SKIP <scene>: needs the living package, which this flint was built without`; JSON `skipped`) instead of failing it, and a name no package manifest has is a failure. `flint examples --json` lists each entry's `packages`. The engine's CI has a `package matrix` job (`scripts/package-check.sh --build`): flint-player and flint-cli are checked with no package, the standard set, each package alone and all of them, and a tree that links a left-out package's crate fails.
**What to do.** Nothing. An examples repository that wants `--check` to pass on a slim flint lists each scene's packages in its index.

### `[packages]` in `flint.toml`, `unlisted-package` and `flint package used` (#1334)
**What changed.** A game can list the engine packages it uses under
`[packages]` beside `[project]` (`living = "*"` for a feature package,
`debug-hud = false` to drop a standard one). With the table, only the
listed packages' schemas load and only their script modules are in `flint
script api`; a component, scene table or script function of a package left
out is an `unlisted-package` error in `flint check`, `flint validate` and
`flint script check`, whose hint is the line to add. A name no package has
is a `bad-manifest` naming the line, with the nearest name. `flint package
used [--write]` reads the scenes, prefabs and scripts and lists (or writes)
what they use. `flint init` writes an empty `[packages]` in every template.
**What to do.** Nothing: without a `[packages]` table every package is in,
as before. To list yours, run `flint package used --write` in the project.
A game binary that builds a `flint_core::project::SchemaDirs` field by field
adds `packages: None`.

### `living_prop`, `shallow_wooze`, `music_session`, `[living]` and `[wooze]` live in their packages (#1332)
**What changed.** Their schema files moved from `schemas/` to
`packages/living/schemas/` and `packages/music/schemas/`. A schema directory
with the engine's package manifests beside it (`engine/schemas` in a game
with the engine as a subtree, a `--schemas` path to a Flint checkout) now
brings each package's `schemas/` in right after it, so the same components
and tables load, in the same layer order, for every command, the player, a
bundle, Android and the web. `flint schema living_prop` names the new file.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that lists `engine/schemas` gets them as
before; only a game that copied the engine's `schemas/` folder somewhere
without `packages/` beside it loses them, and copies `packages/` too.

### `flint-cli` has package features: `living`, `music`, `rhythm`, `procgen`, `ai` (#1333)
**What changed.** Each package of ADR 0136 is a Cargo feature of `flint-cli`, on by default and forwarding to `flint-player`'s; `packages` turns on all five. A build without one links none of its crates (music, rhythm and pad capture, living props, the AI generators and cost ledger). Its commands stay in `flint --help` and still parse, but running one, or a flag that needs it (`gen --ai-create`, `render --ai-refine`, `gen --strip`, `music validate --chart`), exits 2 naming the package; under `--json` the error document carries `package`. `flint package list --built` lists the packages a binary was compiled with. The default build is unchanged: every help page but `package list`'s is byte for byte the same.
**What to do.** Nothing, for a default build. A headless build that used `--no-default-features` and still wants every command adds `--features packages` (`cargo build -p flint-cli --no-default-features --features packages`).

## 2026-11-24

### `flint package list`: the engine's packages, each from its `packages/<name>/package.toml` (#1331)
**What changed.** The engine now says what its packages are without compiling anything. Seven manifests sit under `packages/`: `engine` (the kernel, holding for now physics, audio, animation, particles, terrain and the ocean, grass, stylize and panels features), the standard `procgen` and `debug-hud`, and the features `living`, `music`, `rhythm` and `ai` (ADR 0136). `flint package list [--json] [--dir d]` prints each one's tier, version, requirements, build features, and the crates, components, archetypes, scene tables, script modules and commands it owns. A game's `flint package list` reads its `engine/packages`. A manifest that doesn't read is a new `bad-package` diagnostic naming its file and key, and the command exits 1. In Rust, `flint_core::package` reads a manifest (`package::read`, `Packages::read(dir)`, `Packages::owner(kind, item)`). Nothing else changes yet: a game can't list packages in `flint.toml` until `[packages]` arrives (#1334).
**What to do.** Nothing. A game binary that adds its own top-level command named `package` now clashes with the engine's: rename it.

### A player without `living` links no wooze field; five Rust paths moved (#1330)
**What changed.** `flint-render` no longer depends on `flint-wooze`, and
`flint-player` and `flint-viewer` link it only with their `living` feature
(default on), so a build without living props carries none of the field.
Moved, for Rust that names them (the games in Flint's repos have none):
`flint_render::shallow_wooze::{ShallowWooze, GrassWooze, warn_without_a_field,
warn_grass_without_a_field}` are `flint_wooze::shallow::*`, and
`GrassWooze::advance` / `warn_grass_without_a_field` take the grass's
`GrassConfig::wooze_comb()` (new) instead of the config; `BoilFrame`,
`ShallowWoozeDraw` and `WAVELENGTH_MIN` are defined in
`flint_core::field_frame` (still re-exported from `flint_wooze` and
`flint_render`); `flint_core::components::LIVING_PROP` is
`flint_living::components::LIVING_PROP`, and `MUSIC_SESSION` is
`flint_music::components::MUSIC_SESSION`. Scenes, scripts and pictures are
unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game's own Rust names one of those paths:
change the `use` line.

### A user style's `<name>_style` component loads in `flint render` and the player without a schema file (#1320)
**What changed.** A per-entity user style (a `styles/<name>.shader.toml` with `"mask"` in its `inputs`) gives entities a `<name>_style` component, as the book says. Every drawing host (`flint render`, `flint play`, `flint-player`, `flint edit`) used to log it on each load as `error[unknown-component] ... no component named `<name>_style``, asking for a `components/<name>_style.toml` the book never asks for, though `flint check` and `flint validate` accepted it. The loader now leaves a `<name>_style` to the user style the scene finds in `styles/`, and the host checks its fields once the styles load (a misspelt field is still one `unknown-field` warning). A `<name>_style` for a style that reads no mask does nothing, and `flint check` and the hosts now say so as a `bad-style-setting` warning that names the fix, where `flint check` gave an `unknown-component` error. The examples' open `schemas/examples/components/hologram_style.toml` is gone: nothing needs it.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that declared an open schema for its own user style (`[component.<name>_style]` with no fields) can delete the file.

### `flint profile` and the profiler tab read a trace one way, and Compare rings the scope `explain` names (#1287)
**What changed.** `flint profile` and `flint frame open`'s profiler tab now share one trace reader, so they agree on a trace's frames, its parents, where a wrapped ring's scopes begin and which frame `hitch` means. The tab refuses what `flint profile` refused: a trace newer than this flint reads, a file whose `format` isn't `flint-trace`, and a file with no scopes. Compare now holds each bar to its median over the trace's normal frames (the ones `flint profile explain` uses, so hitches and the look-back's cut edges no longer count in it), splits a path by its file detail as `explain` does, and rings in gold the bar that carries the frame's excess, which is the scope `explain` names; the selection line says `carried by <scope>`. The coverage line both say ends `so the N frames before them have no scopes` (it said `are left out` in `flint profile` and `have none` in the tab). A parent scope that closed in a later frame than its children, such as a worker's long load, is now their parent in the tab too. Every `flint profile` view prints the same as before on a trace that didn't wrap.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that matched `are left out` in `flint profile`'s coverage line should match `have no scopes`.

### `--perf` and traces time imported module functions and closures (#1281)
**What changed.** While a callback is timed (`--perf`, F2, `--hitch-log`) or a trace records, an imported module's functions now get their own records, named as the import names them (`menu::is_open()`), and so do closures, named by file and line (`hud.rhai:closure at line 12`). In a trace each is a scope inside its caller's, so a slow `menu::` helper or `sort(|a, b| ..)` is no longer hidden in the callback's own time. With timing off nothing changes, in what runs or in what it costs.
**What to do.** Nothing. One thing to know: timing a module imports a second, timed copy of it, which runs the module's top level once more when timing first starts. A `log()`, sound or `random()` at a module's top level happens again then. See the book's Profiling guide, "Inside a script".

### A hitch the frame after a screenshot names its own cause, not the screenshot (#1274)
**What changed.** A screenshot, trace-ring or F2-overlay note from the frame before no longer counts toward a hitch's cause. A tool waits for its own work, and its frame already gets its own `tooling` hitch. A render stall on the frame after a `--screenshot-every` shot used to read `screenshot .., 74.8 ms (frame before)` in the hitch log, the exit report, `--perf`, `?perf=1` and the F2 graph. It now reads `render +58.0 ms`. A script stall there no longer has `(also: screenshot ..)` after it, and the screenshot stays in the line's `notes`. The overlay's note is still shown beside a stall as `(also: stats overlay .. the frame before)`, because its next frames do draw more HUD.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tally of `causes:` from a run with screenshots may move from `screenshot` to the phase or script that stalled.

### `flint check` reads every look file and judges its user-style tables (#1272)
**What changed.** `flint check` (and `flint bundle`'s check) now reads every `*.look.toml` a project holds, named by a scene or not. One that doesn't read is a `bad-look` error on its file. A look's `[post_process.styles.<name>]` table for a style that isn't built in is now judged against the user styles of the folders that can take the look. A style none of them has (`[styles.hologramm]`, with `did you mean`) or a key that style doesn't take is a `bad-style-setting` warning naming the look file. Before, a typo there showed only as a load warning in a scene that took the look. Checking one scene (`flint check <scene>`, `flint validate`) judges the scene's look the same way.
**What to do.** Nothing. A look table for a game's own Rust style is warned about, since only the game's binary knows that style: allow it in `[project.diagnostics] allow` if it is one.

### One hash and value noise for every style; `flint_vnoise` and the mask readers in the user prelude (#1256)
**What changed.** Pencil, contour, Kuwahara, watercolor and ASCII's dissolve now boil and grain with one integer hash (PCG) and one value noise, `stylize/noise.wgsl`, and every style reads the style mask through one reader. Pencil's looks are unchanged; contour's and Kuwahara's boil, watercolor's paper and blotches, and the order an ASCII dissolve turns its cells in are new patterns of the same character (the dissolve's was a `sin()` hash that read differently GPU to GPU). A user style's `flint_hash(v)` now hashes the vector's bits with the same PCG, so it returns other values (still a stable 0..1, now also far from the origin), and the prelude adds `flint_hash1(x, y, seed)`, `flint_hash3`, `flint_vnoise(p, seed)` and, with `inputs = ["mask"]`, `flint_mask_load`, `flint_mask_blend` and `flint_mask_texel_of`.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a user style defines a function of one of those names: rename yours. A style tuned against `flint_hash`'s exact values will see a different (equally random) pattern.

### A trace's save costs the frame thread a copy, not a conversion: `profile::snapshot_records`, `Records::scopes`, `warm_copies` (#1267)
**What changed.** Saving a trace (`--trace-on-hitch`, the `save_trace` key, a capture's `trace.json`) used to turn every scope in the window into a named, timed copy on the frame thread: 3.6 ms for 90 000 scopes in release, a frame of its own. The frame thread now copies the records as they lie in the rings, about 0.35 ms for the same window (0.7 ms the first time, into fresh memory), and the trace's writer thread turns them into scopes. The file is the same. A key's or a capture's trace is now taken two frames after it is asked for, so the copy's memory can be readied on another thread meanwhile. In Rust, `flint_core::profile::snapshot_records(since_ns)` returns a `Records`, whose `scopes()` gives what `snapshot(since_ns)` does, on any thread, and `warm_copies()` readies the next copy's memory. `snapshot` still works, both halves on one thread.
**What to do.** Nothing. A host that saves its own traces on the frame thread can call `snapshot_records` there and `scopes()` on the thread that writes the file.

### Scope traces on the web, measured: `?scope_bench=1`, `run.mjs --save-trace` and `--isolate` (#1266)
**What changed.** A web build with the scope recorder (`trunk build --release --cargo-profile web-release --features trace`) was played in Chrome and Firefox: `?perf=1`'s Save trace downloads a trace Perfetto and `flint profile` open, and its header now says when it was saved (`written_at` was empty on the web). A recorded scope costs about 120 ns in a browser (30 ns natively), nearly all of it `performance.now()`. On a page that isn't cross-origin isolated the clock steps by 100 µs (Chrome) or 1 ms (Firefox), so most scopes read 0 ms; a scope's mean over many frames still reads true, one frame's tree and self times don't (the book's Recording a trace). The page's new `?scope_bench=1` times a scope in the browser it runs in and leaves the numbers in `window.flintScopeCost`; `flint_core::profile::cost::measure` is that bench, for any host. `tools/web-perf/run.mjs` gains `--save-trace` (presses Save trace and keeps the file, summed up by `scopes.mjs`) and `--isolate` (serves COOP/COEP), keeps `?scope_bench`'s numbers, and no longer leaves Firefox running after a run on Windows. A trace's GPU passes no longer overlap by a nanosecond, which Perfetto counted as an import error each frame.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `--hitch-log` marks each hitch on a sampling profiler's timeline (#1250)
**What changed.** With `--hitch-log FILE`, every hitch is also marked where a sampler records it, named `hitch <ms> ms <class>: <cause> (frame N, wall_time T)` after its log line. On Linux it is samply's marker file, `marker-<pid>.txt` beside `FILE`, so `samply record` shows each hitch as a span on the main thread. On Windows it is an ETW event from the `Flint.Player` provider (`89d78a2a-cb1c-507a-4e4b-896621b7b194`), which a trace recorded with xperf carries into WPA and `samply import --unknown-event-markers`. The exit report's `[perf] hitch log:` line ends with where the marks went. A run without `--hitch-log` marks nothing.
**What to do.** Nothing. To line a sample up with a hitch, see the Profiling guide's "Lining a sample up with a hitch". On Linux, delete the `marker-*.txt` files beside your hitch logs when you no longer need them.

### A font face egui can't read is skipped with a warning, not a crash (#1262)
**What changed.** A file in `fonts/` (or one a `fonts.toml` alias names by path) that isn't a font egui can open, such as an empty, truncated or mislabelled `.ttf`, used to panic the player and `flint render --scripts` when the HUD's fonts were installed. Now it is left out with `fonts: skipped fonts/Mono.ttf: not a font face egui can read (...)`. Text a script draws in its family falls back to egui's default face. If another file of that stem exists (`Mono.otf` behind a broken `Mono.ttf`), that file is used instead. Faces that load are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. If the warning names one of your fonts, replace the file.

### A trace's oldest frames keep their scopes' text: each script's file, each pass's label (#1259)
**What changed.** The scope recorder kept every thread's text details (`script::call`'s file, `encode_part`'s label, a stage's name) in a fixed 64 KB, which on the Overgrown Ruins held the last ~90 frames of them while the records reached back ~600: a trace or a hitch saved after the fact had `script::call` with no file in every older frame. The text ring is now 32 bytes for each record the ring holds and grows with it, so a record's text lasts as long as the record. That costs memory, only on a thread that records text: a default ring is 2.6 MB of records and 2.1 MB of text, the player's 5 s ring 6.6 MB and 5.2 MB, and a ring grown to its most 52 MB and 42 MB. A trace's `ring_bytes_per_thread` now counts the text too (11.8 MB for 5 s, was 6.6 MB). `flint_core::profile::TEXT_BYTES_PER_RECORD` and `Ring::text_capacity()` are new.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `flint render --scripts` runs every scene subsystem the player does (#1252)
**What changed.** A capture builds its scene's subsystems from the player's own list, so a subsystem added as one file in `player_app/subsystems/` draws in `flint render --time T --scripts` what `flint-player --frame-step` draws at T; before, only living props, the music and declared fields ran there, and any other was left out without a word. A subsystem that only a window can show declares it (`Subsystem::presentation_only`), and the capture names it once in its warnings. For a game embedding the engine's capture, `HeadlessGame::start_living(field, moment)` is now `start_subsystems(scene_file.scene_tables(), CaptureStart { wooze, moment, scripts })`, called at the same place.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless your own host drives `HeadlessGame`: call `start_subsystems` where it called `start_living`, before `start_scripts`.

### Crossfades and sequence layer fades take an easing: `animator.blend_easing`, `blend_to(.., easing)`, a step's `easing` (#1248)
**What changed.** A crossfade's weight can now move as one of the engine's easings (`linear`, `smooth`, `ease_in`, `ease_out`, `ease_in_out`) instead of only linearly: write `blend_easing` on the `animator`, call `blend_to(entity, clip, seconds, easing)`, or give a `.sequence.toml` `blend` step an `easing`. The easing stays written, so later crossfades ease the same way. A sequence's `layer` step takes `easing` too, written as the layer's `fade_easing`. A step naming an easing that doesn't exist is a load error naming the file and the step. An animator that never writes `blend_easing`, and a step with no `easing`, fade linearly as before.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A style cap that leaves an entity out says so on screen, in the player and the scene viewer (#1246)
**What changed.** When a style's cap leaves something out (ASCII's 8 dissolve bands or 8 snap grids, pencil's and watercolor's paper images, the style mask's ids), the player and `flint edit`'s scene viewer now show a card in the bottom-left corner, above the style error card: `style 'ascii' leaves `prop008` out`, then the cap and how the entity draws instead. It is the warning the log already had (#740), and the card empties when a scene without drops loads. It is part of the debug HUD (`debug-hud`, on by default). `flint_debug_ui::paint_style_errors` now returns the height it drew, as `paint_script_errors` does.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that draws `paint_style_errors` itself can stack `flint_debug_ui::paint_style_drops(ctx, &flint_render::stylize::variant_table::dropped(), inset)` above it.

### A `shallow_wooze` translucent is veiled and fogged where it is drawn, not at its rest pose (#1241)
**What changed.** A blended mesh with `shallow_wooze` (a drifting pane of glass, a woozy soft-edged body) is now redrawn through its woozy vertex stage in the style veil and the fog veil, as the main pass draws it. In front of a styled entity it unmasks the style where the pane is drawn (it unmasked where the pane had been, so glyphs showed through one side of the glass and a dimmed ghost stood on the other), and in fog it is fogged at its own depth (it was fogged as what lay behind it, beside a fogless ghost of itself). A woozy body translucent only at its soft edge, which the main pass blends whole, is now fogged at its own depth too. A scene with no woozy translucent draws as before, byte for byte.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A collider's `layer`, `mask` and scale changed by a script are heard; `set_collision_layer`, `set_collision_mask` (#1221)
**What changed.** A collider's `layer` and `mask` and its entity's world scale used to be read once, when its body was made. Now a change is heard at the next physics step: `set_field(id, "collider", "layer", "ghost")` puts it on the new layer (a body at rest is woken), and `set_scale` on the entity or a parent remakes its collider at the new size, `offset` included. New script functions `set_collision_layer(id, layer)` and `set_collision_mask(id, mask)` write the same fields and refuse a name that is no layer at the call. A step where nothing was written costs less than before (26 us against 81 us for 2000 colliders): `FlintWorld::write_stamp(id)` is new, and a collider whose entity and ancestors weren't written isn't read.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game changed `transform.scale` on a physics entity and relied on its collider keeping its old size: it now follows the drawn size, as one made at that scale would. `PhysicsSync::update_sensor_flags` is now `update_colliders`, and `sync_from_rapier` takes `&mut self`.

### UI documents: `scroll = true` on a `list`, and a `text_input` element (#1229)
**What changed.** A `list` with `scroll = true` clips its items to its box and scrolls: it keeps the focused item in view as the arrows or a pad move focus, a mouse wheel over it moves it a row a notch (focus follows onto a row in view), and a finger dragged along it scrolls it. An overflowing list draws a thumb (`scrollbar_color`, `scrollbar_width`). A new `type = "text_input"` takes typing while it has focus (`placeholder`, `max_length`; style `caret_color`, `placeholder_color`): characters, Backspace, Delete, Left, Right, Home, End, and Enter, which sends `on_ui(id, "activate")`; each edit sends `change`. New script functions `ui_get_text(id)` and `ui_editing()`. `ui_set_text` on a text input puts its caret after the text. Draw items made by UI documents now carry the clip of the scrolling lists they are in. A list without `scroll` lays out and draws as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. To scroll a long list, add `scroll = true` and give its class a `height` (not `height_auto`). A game that reads movement from letter keys can skip it while `ui_editing()` is true.

### `flint check` and `flint validate` know the collision layers: an undeclared `collider.layer` or `mask` name is found before the game runs (#1220)
**What changed.** A `collider.layer`, or an entry of `collider.mask`, that is neither `default` nor one of the project's `[project.physics] layers` (written on the entity or given by its archetype) is now `bad-enum-value` in `flint check`, `flint validate`, `flint edit`'s panel and the scene's load summary, naming the nearest layer: `"playr" is not a collision layer`, hint ``did you mean `player`?; it is read as `default` ``. A project that declares no layers is told the line to add, `[project.physics] layers = ["player"]`. The player says the same words when the body is made, where it used to say `"playr" is not a value of `layer`` for a layer and repeat itself for a mask entry; what a bad name does is unchanged (a layer reads as `default`, a mask entry is left out).
**What to do.** Nothing, unless `flint check` now lists a layer: declare it under `[project.physics] layers` in `flint.toml`, or fix the spelling.

### A `music_session` plays in the browser's worklet; `flint_music::clocked` replaces kira types in the suite's API (#1216)
**What changed.** On the web, a scene with a `music_session` no longer moves the whole mix to the main thread: its suite plays in the `AudioWorklet` with every other sound, and its beat lands on the same samples as on the desktop. If the worklet fails, the suite starts again on the main-thread fallback. In Rust, `AudioEngine::host` now hands its closure a `&mut dyn flint_audio::clocked::ClockedMixer` instead of kira's `AudioManager`, and `Unhosted::Worklet` is gone. `SuiteSession::start`, `BusMixer::build` and `ChartSession::open_shared` take that mixer, and an `AudioManager<B>` still coerces to it. `StemBus::set_gain`, `set_lpf`, `set_detune`, `set_sound_volume`, `set_alternate_mix` and `BusMixer::stop_all` take a `flint_music::clocked::Ramp` (`Ramp::now(secs)`, `Ramp::at(start, secs)`) instead of a kira `Tween`. A start time is a `clocked::Start` (`Now` or `Tick(n)`), so `SuiteSession::clock_time_at_raw` is now `start_at_raw`. `StemBus::play_cue` and `sound()` give a `clocked::StemVoice` (`set_volume(db, ramp)`, `set_rate(rate, ramp)`, `stop(ramp)`), and `SuiteSession::clock` is a `clocked::MixClock` (`ticks()`). `AudioEngine::mixer_epoch()` says when the mix has moved.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless your Rust drives a suite's buses with kira types. In that case, replace `Tween { start_time: StartTime::ClockTime(session.clock_time_at_raw(raw)), duration, .. }` with `Ramp::at(session.start_at_raw(raw), secs)`, and a `match` on `Unhosted::Worklet` with nothing.

### `shape = "mesh"` and `"convex"` colliders take procgen and runtime models' triangles (#1222)
**What changed.** A `mesh` or `convex` collider on a model a `*.procgen.toml` spec makes is the mesh the spec generated, the one drawn, where it was a box of `size` with a warning; one the deferred queue makes later becomes its hull on the frame after. On a runtime mesh (`model.runtime = true`) uploaded with `upload_mesh_with(.., MeshOptions { ray_target: true })`, the collider is the ray target's triangles from the frame after each upload, so `raycast()` meets what draws; before the first upload it is a box of `size`, now without a warning. A runtime mesh uploaded without `ray_target` keeps no triangles, and its collider says so once.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a scene leaned on the old box: a procgen or runtime model's `mesh`/`convex` collider is now its drawn shape, so its `size` no longer matters. To give a runtime mesh a collider, upload it as a ray target.

## 2026-11-23

### `flint init` projects have a pause menu; a click no longer catches the cursor while paused (#1228)
**What changed.** Every template (`walk`, `blank`, `2d`) writes `ui/pause.ui.toml` and `ui/pause.style.toml`, binds `pause` to Escape and a pad's Start in `config/input.toml`, and its `scripts/hud.rhai` opens the menu with `import "menu"` (Resume, Restart, Quit; a pause button on a touch screen). With `--engine path` or `none` the project carries `scripts/lib/menu.rhai`, a copy of the engine's, since `import` doesn't search a checkout named by path. In the player, a left click in a first-person scene no longer catches the cursor while the game state pauses physics (the `paused` state `menu::toggle_pause` pushes), so a click in a pause menu stays the menu's.
**What to do.** Nothing for an existing game. A first-person pause menu that relied on a click to catch the cursor again calls `set_cursor_captured(true)` when it closes. A project made by an older `flint init` can take the menu from a fresh `flint init x` beside it.

### A player's own bus volumes, saved between runs: `set_user_bus_volume` / `user_bus_volume` (#1217)
**What changed.** Each bus has a second volume, the player's, which the mixer multiplies with the game's: `set_user_bus_volume("music", 0.4)` (0..1, clamped) under a script's `set_bus_volume("music", 0.5)` fade is heard at 0.2, and neither call overwrites the other. `user_bus_volume(bus)` reads it back on the same frame (1 when never set). The player saves it for the game, half a second after the last change, at a scene transition and on exit, in `<config>/<game id>/audio.toml` (`[buses] music = 0.4`; `<config>/flint/audio.toml` with no `[project] id`; `localStorage` `flint:/config/<id>/audio.toml` on the web; `audio.user.toml` in a `--save-dir`), and reads it at the next start before the first sound and `on_init`. `flint playtest` keeps it in memory. Nothing changes for a game that doesn't call them.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that saved its own music and sfx levels through `persist_set` and re-applied them with `set_bus_volume` can move its settings menu to `set_user_bus_volume`, which frees `set_bus_volume` for fades.

### The terrain's collider is a heightfield (#1219)
**What changed.** A `terrain` now collides as one Rapier heightfield of its chunks' vertex grid instead of their triangles as a trimesh. The surface is the same, triangle for triangle (rays answer within 0.01 mm), but the edges between cells and the seams between chunks are smooth: a ball rolling across them no longer hops, and the collider holds one height a vertex. `get_collider_extents` on a terrain now answers `shape: "heightfield"` (it was `"mesh"`), with the same box.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a script compares a terrain's `get_collider_extents(..).shape` with `"mesh"`: compare with `"heightfield"`.

### A bus ducks under another, and every bus has its own low-pass: `[audio.buses.<name>] duck` and `lowpass`, Rhai `set_bus_lowpass` (#1218)
**What changed.** A bus in `flint.toml` takes `duck = { under = "voice", by_db = -9, attack = 0.08, release = 0.6 }` (only `under` is required): while anything plays on `voice` (or a bus under it) the bus dips by `by_db` and comes back after, on the desktop and in the browser's worklet alike. Every bus, `sfx` and `music` included, has its own low-pass: `lowpass = 3000` starts one muffled, and scripts call `set_bus_lowpass("sfx", 800)` (`20000` opens it; extensions `ctx.audio.set_bus_lowpass(bus, hz)`). An open bus low-pass costs nothing. The worklet's `Command::BusNew` carries the bus's cutoff, and there is a new `Command::BusLowpass`; `MixerBackend::add_bus` takes the cutoff. Games without the new keys sound as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that faded its music by hand under every line of dialogue can declare a `duck` instead.

### `flint frame replay --draw-times` and `--overdraw`: what each draw costs, and how often a pixel is drawn (#1203)
**What changed.** A replay can now time each draw of the scene pass on the GPU, `--draw-times`, which puts a `gpu_ms` on every scene draw in the replay's `manifest.json` and lists the costliest draws and the cost by kind. `flint frame show --draws --sort gpu` and the frame debugger's draws table (a `GPU ms` column) sort by it. `--overdraw` adds an `Overdraw` pass after the scene's: `overdraw_count` counts the draws that shade each pixel, and `overdraw` shows that count as a heat map, each colour twice the draws of the one before (1, 3, 7, 15, 31+). Neither flag changes a pixel of the frame, and a frame drawn without them is drawn as before. Draw times need an adapter with timestamps inside passes (Vulkan, DX12, Metal); elsewhere the replay says so. A capture's `DrawDescription` gained an optional `gpu_ms`; older readers ignore it.
**What to do.** Nothing. To find a costly draw: `flint frame replay <capture> --draw-times --overdraw`, then `flint frame show <replay> --draws --sort gpu`.

### `character_controller`: `radius` and `camera_mode` are gone, a second controller is `second-player`, and a schema can retire a field (#1212)
**What changed.** `character_controller.radius` and `camera_mode` are no longer fields. Nothing ever read them: the capsule is the entity's `collider`, and a character's camera is always first person. A scene that still writes either gets `unknown-field` at load and in `flint check`, reading ``character_controller` has no field `camera_mode` any more``, with why in the hint. The `player` archetype no longer sets them. A scene with two entities carrying a `character_controller` (written or from an archetype) now says so: `second-player` on each after the first by name, naming the first as the player. Only the player's controller is read, as before. A schema can name the fields it took out under `[component.<name>.retired]` (`field = "why, and what to do"`), and a scene writing one is told that reason in place of the nearest field's name. An extension's `retired` adds to the earlier layer's. The controller's six action names (`move_forward`, `move_backward`, `move_left`, `move_right`, `jump`, `sprint`) stay fixed and are now constants in `flint_physics::character` (`ACTIONS`). ADR 0134 records why they, and the controller itself, stay as they are.
**What to do.** Delete `radius` and `camera_mode` from your scenes' and prefabs' `character_controller` tables; `flint check` lists each one. Give a second character no `character_controller`, and move it from a script with `move_character`.

### `flint frame replay` draws the floor grid when the capture did (#1211)
**What changed.** A still `flint render --frame-capture` draws the floor grid unless `--no-grid`, and its replay always drew without it, so the scene pass replayed off by the grid's pixels (1,893 of 14,400 on `stylize_props` at 160 x 90). A capture's `replay/inputs.json` now records `renderer.grid`, and the replay draws the grid when it is `true`. A capture written before this reads `false` and replays as it always did.
**What to do.** Nothing. To replay an older still capture that drew the grid exactly, capture it again.

### The player says when the OS held its frames back, so the hitch tools found nothing (#1200)
**What changed.** Under vsync, Windows throttles the presents of a window it doesn't show: one behind others, or a background job's opened with `--no-activate`. Every frame then stands 250 to 580 ms while the game's own work is a few ms, and a 100 ms stall can't stand out against that median, so `--capture-on-hitch`, `--hitch-log` and `--max-hitches` found nothing and didn't say why. Now a run whose frames after the first 2 s were mostly held by the OS (at least 100 ms and four refreshes each, under a quarter of it the player's work, under half the GPU's) says so once on the console as it happens, `[perf] frames held by the OS at ~412 ms (the player's work 3.1 ms): ...; FLINT_VSYNC=off measures real frame times`, and `--exit-after`'s report adds `[perf] frames held by the OS: N of M after t=2 s, ...` under its hitch count. A run whose window shows says nothing new.
**What to do.** Nothing. To look for hitches in a run whose window won't show, set `FLINT_VSYNC=off` (or pass `--vsync off`).

### `flint playtest --save-from <file>` starts a playtest from a save (#1193)
**What changed.** A playtest can begin from a save file instead of an empty store: the file is loaded as the player loads a save (its `[meta] version`, the scripts' `on_persist_migrate(from)`, `persist_corrupt()` for a file that doesn't parse), and the run still writes nothing, so the file keeps its bytes. The report gains a `save` section (what the load found, the versions, `untouched`). A playtest's slots are now kept in memory, so `persist_save()` and `persist_slot(name)` work in one where before they had no save location.
**What to do.** Nothing. To test a returning player, a migration or a corrupt save, pass `--save-from` with a copy of that save.

### `flint capture` refuses a run that won't fit on the disk; `--no-disk-check` (#1196)
**What changed.** Before the first take, `flint capture` asks the disk under `out` for its free space and stops when the run's estimate (8 MB a 4K frame, scaled by area; with `--verify`, the takes plus the largest one's second run) passes 90% of it, plus whatever old takes the run replaces. The error names the estimate, the free space and the ways out: a `--scale` that fits, the largest `--shot`, `--out` and `--no-disk-check`. The header line now says the free space too, and `--dry-run` prints the same warning without refusing (`--json`: a `disk` block with `need_bytes`, `free_bytes`, `reclaimed_bytes` and `fits`).
**What to do.** Nothing. A run you know will fit (a disk that frees space as it goes) takes `--no-disk-check`.

### A frame capture says what its copies cost on the GPU: `capture.gpu_copy_ms` (#1160)
**What changed.** A capture's `manifest.json` has `capture.gpu_copy_ms` (every copy together) and `capture.gpu_copies_timed`, and each image `gpu_copy_ms`, timed on the capture's own timestamp queries, so the frame's GPU timer still times every pass. `flint render --frame-capture` and the player's `wrote ...` line end with `copied in N ms on the GPU`. On an adapter without timestamp queries the fields are `null` and the line says nothing more. Older captures read as before.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A hitch's `cause` names a script that stalled, not the frame before's slow note (#1194)
**What changed.** The hitch log's `cause`, `--perf`'s hitch line, F2's graph and the exit report's `causes:` named a frame's notes whenever, with the frame before's, they covered half its excess. On a loaded machine a 100 ms reload poll on the frame before outvoted a 150 ms script stall on the frame itself, which read `reload poll 1 scripts watched, 111.7 ms (frame before)`. When the hitch's own script took half its excess, the frame before's notes no longer count toward it: the line reads `script stall.rhai:on_update 150.0 ms (also: reload poll 111.7 ms the frame before)`. Notes on the hitch's own frame count as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A `--max-hitches` gate counts the same hitches; only their causes are named differently.

### `flint playtest --test-script <file>`: a playtest's own Rhai checks (#1192)
**What changed.** A playtest can run Rhai checks that live beside its input script instead of in the game's scripts. Each `--test-script` (repeatable) is attached to an entity the run spawns in every scene it enters (`playtest`, then `playtest_2` ...), loaded beside the scene's scripts, with the whole script API and the `test_*` functions. Assertions and verdicts name its file and entity, `--strict` holds it to the script API before the run, and a file that isn't there fails the run at its start. A scene with an entity named `playtest` of its own gets a script error when a test script needs that name.
**What to do.** Nothing. A check that went into a game script only to test it can move to a `--test-script` file.

### `flint asset generate model --provider meshy` makes a preview, then refines it (#1187)
**What changed.** A Meshy model was one request, a refine with a prompt and no `preview_task_id`, which Meshy's v2 API refuses. It is now the two the API asks for: a preview of the prompt, polled until it succeeds, then a refine of it with `preview_task_id`, polled and downloaded. Each is its own line in `.flint/ai_costs.jsonl` (`openapi/v2/text-to-3d preview`, 20 credits; `... refine`, 10), and the built-in price is `[calls.meshy] unit = "credit"`, `usd = 0.02`, so a model still costs $0.60. A job record names both tasks: `remote_id` is the preview, the new `refine_id` the refine once submitted; `flint asset job resume` goes on from either, and a job stopped in its preview pays for the refine alone (its ledger line says `"operation": "resume"`). A style guide's negative prompt is no longer sent to Meshy (the v2 API takes none); the refine asks for PBR maps (`enable_pbr`).
**What to do.** Nothing, if `.flint/prices.toml` doesn't price Meshy. One that does as `unit = "task"` keeps working (the price is split 2:1 between the two requests); to price by the credit, write `unit = "credit"` and your plan's rate a credit.

### `flint playtest --render`: screenshots and `frame` checks with no window (#1191)
**What changed.** `flint playtest --render` draws every frame into an offscreen texture through the player's own renderer and HUD, so models load, `--screenshot` / `--screenshot-at` / `--screenshot-every` write PNGs at game times, and the report gains `render` (frame size, frames drawn, draw calls) and `screenshots`. Input scripts take `screenshot <file>` (the next frame drawn, in `flint play` too) and `frame` checks: `expect frame hud contains "<text>"` / `lacks`, `frame style <name> on|off`, `frame draws [no] <entity>` (the entity or its model's nodes). Without `--render`, `frame hud` still reads the HUD's draw list and the other two fail saying no frame was drawn. A playtest's warning count now leaves out a library's warnings (the Vulkan loader's), as the load summary already did.
**What to do.** Nothing. To check what a frame shows, add `--render` and `frame` lines to a playtest.

### `flint asset generate texture` follows fal's queue (#1186)
**What changed.** Flux's default endpoint, `queue.fal.run`, answers a POST
with a receipt, not an image. Flint read the image straight from that answer,
so every generate failed with `Unexpected Flux response format` after fal had
queued the request (and billed it). Now Flint polls the request's
`status_url`, fetches the output from its `response_url` and then downloads
the image. A queued texture is a job in `.flint/jobs/`, as a Meshy model is,
so `flint asset job resume <id>` finishes one whose run stopped without
paying again. An `api_url` pointing at the synchronous `fal.run` still works,
and makes no job.
**What to do.** Nothing. Jobs from earlier runs read as before.

### A query field steps into an array by index: `transform.position[2]`, `path.points[3].x` (#1185)
**What changed.** A field path in `flint query`, a constraint's `query`, `--attach`, `--dump-query` and a playtest's `expect` / `wait` can step into an array by its index from 0, `[n]`, as well as by the axes `x`, `y`, `z`, `w`, and on through a list of vectors or tables (`path.stops[1].name`). `entities where transform.position.z > 5` selects by the third element, of a file and of a running game. A path that finds nothing (past an array's end, into a number or string, `[n]` of a table) matches nothing, as a missing field always has, with `!=` as well, and is not an error. Queries that parsed before mean what they meant.
**What to do.** Nothing.

## 2026-11-21

### `flint play --json` is flint's global flag again (#1184)
**What changed.** `flint play <scene> --list-keys --json` prints the debug-key table as JSON, and so does `flint --json play <scene> --list-keys`. Before this, both were refused as a window command, so the line the book gave never worked. A plain `flint play --json` is now refused with flint's window-command error (exit 1, an `error` document). It used to fail with a clap error asking for `--list-keys` (exit 2). `flint-player --list-keys --json` is unchanged. A game binary that set `PlayerArgs::json` from clap now sets it itself: the field is still there, but it is no longer a flag.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game's own binary flattens `PlayerArgs`: then give it a `--json` of its own and copy that into `args.json`, the way `PlayerCli` does.

### `draw_world_text` with `facing` warns when its size reads as points (#1182)
**What changed.** With `facing`, `draw_world_text`'s `size` is the em in world units, and without it points. `size: 15.0` with `facing` asks for a 15 m em, which a camera a few metres away sees as a corner of one glyph or nothing. The player now warns once a label when a plane's size is 4 units or more and longer than the text's distance from the camera (`draw_world_text `> _` ... has `facing`, so its size 15 is the em in world units`). Drawing is unchanged. `flint script api` and the script API reference now say what `size` means in each case, and that faced text reads only from the side `facing` points to.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless the player warns: then give that label's size in metres (about 0.03 for a line of text on a screen, 0.15 to 0.3 for a shop sign), or leave out `facing`.

### A frame capture writes behind the game, and a hitch while it writes says so (#1178)
**What changed.** The player's frame-capture writer (`--capture-frame-at`, `--capture-on-hitch`, Shift+F2) and its encoder threads now run at background priority: the scheduler's background mode on Windows (CPU, disk and memory priority), nice 10 on Linux, the background QoS class on macOS. Each frame while a capture is being written carries a new frame note, `capture write <folder>`. A hitch on such a frame names the write as its likely cause in the hitch log, the exit report, `--perf` and the F2 graph, unless a script took the time itself (`script stall.rhai:on_update 150.2 ms (also: capture write)`). `--capture-on-hitch` passes over a hitch while a capture is being written, because it may be that capture's own cost, and says so once. A capture taken while `--trace` records no longer fails with `holds more than a frame capture (trace.json)` when the trace's writer reaches the folder first.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `tron` keeps a ground seen at a grazing angle as wire, not solid glow (#1181)
**What changed.** The `tron` style's depth edges now fire where the depth bends (the second difference of 1 / distance), not wherever it changes, so a flat road, floor or sea running to the horizon no longer fills with glow from a few tens of metres out; objects keep their outlines, now visible against it. The world grid's lines thin and fade toward the horizon instead of breaking into dashes. The `edges`, `grid` and other settings keep their meaning: a step of 5% of the view distance is still a full edge. A frame under `tron` that shows a ground plane looks different.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene that only blended `tron` in while the camera faced a wall to hide the glow can show it over open ground now.

### `reveal_invert = true` keeps a style outside its reveal region, so the plain picture can open from a point (#1180)
**What changed.** Every style's table (and `--style`, `set_post_style_param`, F4's Reveal group) takes `reveal_invert`. Inverted, the region still grows with `reveal` and keeps its shape, edge, ink, rim and shadow, but the style is kept outside it and the frame it read shows through it: `--style ascii:reveal=0.3,reveal_shape=iris,reveal_invert=true` is the rendered picture in a circle with glyphs round it. Inverted, `reveal = 0` is the style everywhere and `reveal = 1` the live frame, byte for byte the frames of a plain reveal at 1 and 0; easing `reveal` 0 to 1 opens the plain picture from `reveal_x`, `reveal_y`. The rim of bare paper stays on the style's side and the shadow falls into the hole. `reveal_invert` is a reserved key, so a user style can no longer name a parameter or option `reveal_invert`. A reveal without the key draws exactly as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A film that wiped the plain picture in because an iris shrank the wrong way can use `reveal_shape = "iris"` with `reveal_invert = true`.

### `font: "monospace"` draws fixed-pitch text, and a `fonts.toml` alias may name a face by path (#1179)
**What changed.** `draw_text`, `measure_text`, `draw_world_text` and a UI document's `font` take `"monospace"` and `"proportional"`, egui's two built-in families: one `draw_text` call draws a line of code or a column of numbers in a fixed pitch with no font file of the game's, and `measure_text` with the same `font` is the width it draws. Before, both names fell back to the proportional face with a warning. A project file or alias of either name still comes first, and with `[egui] monospace` set the name draws the game's own face. A `fonts/fonts.toml` alias's `file` may now be a relative path from the manifest's folder (`file = "ofl/VT323/VT323-Regular.ttf"`, or `../vendor/Mono.ttf`), so a face kept in a subfolder for a glyph set is a HUD font without a second copy; it registers under the alias only. A manifest is read even when `fonts/` holds no font file of its own.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that drew fixed cells one character at a time can draw the line with `font: "monospace"`.

### `flint gen --describe` lists the keys of nested tables (#1173)
**What changed.** `--describe` (text and `--markdown`) and the flint-procgen README catalog now print a nested table's keys under the key that holds it: `creature_v1`'s `bones[].name`, `body_parts[].shape`, `limb_chains[].segments[].length` and `materials.<name>.roughness`, and `sweep_v1`'s `control_points[].twist`, each with its type, its default and what it does. A key a table must have reads `required`. The creature schema now also lists keys its parser always read but the schema never named (`body_parts[].offset`, `limb_chains[].attach_offset` and `.material`, `segments[].rest_angles`), and holds a segment to `length`, `radius_start` and `radius_end`, which generation already refused to go without.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A frame capture names its camera, and `flint frame image` shows depth in metres (#1176)
**What changed.** A capture's `manifest.json` has a `camera` block: the camera the frame drew with (position, target, fov, near, far, orthographic and its height), and a comic panel's under its panel. `flint frame image <capture> Scene --target depth` and the frame debugger's `Auto` view turn the scene's depth into metres through it, on a log scale from the nearest drawn (white) to the farthest (dark), and the `view` line names the range (`depth 2.67..86.0 m, log scale, near white`). The debugger has a `Metres` mode beside `Depth`. `flint frame show` prints a `Camera` line. `replay/inputs.json` no longer carries its own camera: the replay reads the manifest's. A capture written before this has no `camera`: its depth keeps the old stretch over the stored values, and its replay reads the camera from `inputs.json` as before. Shadow map layers keep the stretch too.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### Entities sharing a skinned model draw their first frame the same every run (#1177)
**What changed.** Several entities on one skinned asset (a bartender and two patrons on one `.glb`) share a fallback palette that an entity draws with until it writes its own bones. It was whichever entity wrote last, which wasn't the same one every run, so a stepped capture or screenshot of that frame could differ between two runs. It is now always the first entity by name's pose; the style scissor's bounds fall back to it too.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### F1 lists the debug keys in every `flint edit` editor; the procgen previewer's debug-mode cycle moves to F5 (#1170)
**What changed.** The editors' function keys are a debug key table like the player's: F1 shows the same card in the scene viewer, the track editor, the procgen previewer and every other editor, listing the host's F1 and the editor's own keys. In the procgen previewer F1 used to cycle the debug shading mode; that is F5 now (F2 wireframe, F3 normal arrows and F4 skeleton keep their keys). A held F-key no longer repeats its toggle. A game binary that paints the card itself calls `flint_runtime::debug_key_card::paint` (the runtime's new `egui` feature); `flint_viewer::host::Editor` gained `debug_keys`, `debug_keys_live` and `on_debug_key`.
**What to do.** Press F5, not F1, for the previewer's debug modes. An `Editor` of a game's own declares its F-keys in `debug_keys` instead of matching them in `event`.

### A terrain spec is a `terrain_v1` procgen spec: `flint gen x.terrain.toml --register`, `--ai-generator terrain_v1` (#1168)
**What changed.** A new generator, `terrain_v1`, wraps the terrain bake, and `flint gen` reads a `.terrain.toml` as a spec for it, so every procgen flag works on terrain: `--register` stores and reuses both maps, `--batch`, `--validate`, `--print-spec` (the recipe as a `*.procgen.toml`), `--describe terrain_v1`, and the AI steps (`--ai-create "rolling hills" --ai-generator terrain_v1`; words such as hills, mountains and valley pick it). The maps are written as an image set: `-o terrain/meadow.png` makes `terrain/meadow_height.png` (16-bit grey, the same bytes as before) and `terrain/meadow_splat.png`, where #719's route made `meadow.png` and `meadow_splat.png`; `-o dir/` makes `output_height.png` and `output_splat.png`. A value the bake can't use (a splat rule's `layer` past 3, a resolution over 4096) is now an error that stops the run, not a warning, and the `--json` document is the procgen one (`variants[].files`), without #719's `coverage`. The terrain editor lists the spec's warnings under its Save buttons. Procgen's `ImageData` gained `format` (`PixelFormat::Rgba8` or `L16`) and `ChannelSemantics::Splat`; the procgen disk cache's files from before are misses and are regenerated once.
**What to do.** A script or scene that names the heightmap `flint gen` wrote as `<name>.png` names `<name>_height.png` instead. Rust that builds an `ImageData` literal adds `format: PixelFormat::Rgba8`.

### `flint edit` says what it says through the player's log: one load summary, `--log-format`, `--quiet` (#1164)
**What changed.** The editor windows (`flint edit` on a scene, model, procgen spec, terrain, texture pipeline or particle effect) write every line through `tracing` to stderr, as `flint play` does; none prints to stdout any more. A scene, in the viewer or with `--spline`, says its load in one line once its models are in, and again after each hot reload (`Loaded <scene>: N entities, 4 models in 180 ms; no warnings`), in place of `Loaded scene:` / `Entities:` and a line for each file. The window's notices (saves, reloads, undo, F4 toggles) are info lines of target `viewer`. `flint edit` takes `--log-format text|json` and `--quiet`, and the model previewer's per-cue `[sequence] cue` line is debug. A `--render` still still answers `Rendered ...` on stdout.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a script read the viewer's stdout: read stderr, or `--log-format json` and the `load` line's `scene`, `entities`, `warnings`, `errors` and `ms` fields. `RUST_LOG=load=debug` lists each file again.

### `texture_v1`'s colour defaults name the colours it renders (#1165)
**What changed.** The schema, `flint edit`'s previewer and the README showed a colour default as the bytes of its linear floats, which parse to a far darker colour: `base_color`'s `#735A47` renders on average 69 levels (of 255) darker than leaving the key out, and the same went for `mortar_color` (`#40382E`) and for the pipeline ops `cell_color` and `mortar_color`. They now read `#B3A090` and `#898176`, the sRGB hex that parses back to the default (to within one level). `height_variation` no longer shows `0.3` for `tiling_grid`, whose default is 0.2; its description gives both. A spec renders byte for byte as before, whatever it says.
**What to do.** A spec saved from the previewer with `base_color = "#735A47"` or `mortar_color = "#40382E"` and meant to keep the default: delete the key, or write the new hex.

### A graph-mode op can read `voronoi_texture`'s `cell_output` by node id (#1166)
**What changed.** `voronoi_texture` now declares `<cell_output>` as a channel it writes, so in a pipeline whose ops have `id`s, another op's `inputs` can name it (`cell_id = "cells.cell"` or `"cells.<cell_output>"`), and the texture node editor shows it as a second output pin. Before, graph mode never copied it out and the reader got zeros. With `cell_output` unset nothing changes, and specs without ids render byte-identically. The node editor also stops guessing `height` for an optional channel left unset, which wired such a pin to whatever read height.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `flint render --scripts --time` draws a look fade as the player does (#1157)
**What changed.** A capture inside a `set_look(name, seconds)` fade drew the fade from the look the scene file authors when an earlier `set_look` had changed it: it kept only the last look a script asked for. It now lays every look asked since the last capture in order, each fade from the frame at the moment it was asked, so a capture at T has the post values and style blends the player draws at T. In the player a fade is now timed from the game time it was asked: the frame of the request draws its start (it was one frame further on) and it arrives at exactly the asked time plus its seconds.
**What to do.** Nothing. A capture of a scene whose script cuts to one look and later fades to another draws differently mid-fade, which is the fix; re-bless a golden or still taken there.

### `--frame-capture` reads the selection mask and ASCII's edge history (#1161)
**What changed.** A frame capture now holds two kinds of image it used to leave out. With `--select`, the `Selection mask` pass writes `selection_mask`, and the `Selection outline` pass lists it under `reads`. A style can now note the textures it keeps itself (`StylePass::held_targets`). ASCII notes its edge cells, slots, thinned lines and outlines, the past edge cells and slots it read (`ascii.edge_cells.past`, `ascii.edge_slots.past`), its tone statistics, variant cells, snap owners and dissolve state. Each one is on the ASCII pass and in the manifest's `targets`. Frames that nobody captures draw exactly what they drew before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game's Rust style that keeps textures outside `frame.target` can return a `HeldTargets` from `held_targets` and `note` each texture after the pass that writes it.

### `[post_process] preload_looks` and `preload_styles`: a scene pays for its later looks at its entry (#1159)
**What changed.** A scene can name the looks and styles it will show later, and the player builds them when the scene loads, on worker threads, so the frame a script cuts or fades to one builds nothing:

```toml
[post_process]
preload_looks = ["graphite", "ink_wash", "watercolor"]   # for set_look
preload_styles = ["matrix", "tron"]                      # for set_post_style
```

`prepare_look` (#1121) built a look's styles; it now builds the blend, reveal and tear draws they ask for too, which a frame used to build the first time a style's `reveal` lay between 0 and 1 (92 ms in one film) or it tore in. A preloaded look gets every one of those draws, since a fade blends it in; a preloaded style gets every one too, whatever its table says at load. A scene's own styles get their draws built at entry with no key at all, so a sheet waiting at `reveal = 0` tears open without a stop. A preloaded look is read from its file once, at load, so the cut to it asks the disk nothing; and every `set_look` now uses the project found at the scene's entry instead of looking for it again, which on a busy disk had stalled cuts by 130-170 ms. So a look file edited while a scene plays shows at the scene's next load if the scene preloads it. A look the search can't find or a style nobody registered is warned about at load and skipped. Rust hosts: `SceneRenderer::prepare_styles_with(device, &config, &names)`.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a scene switches looks or styles mid-play: list them under `preload_looks` and `preload_styles`.

### A camera rig for scripts: `set_camera_on_path`, `shake_view`, `set_hand_held`, `shot`, `noise` (#1158)
**What changed.** The new `camera_rig` module flies the 3D camera along a smooth path through a list of points (`camera_path`, `camera_path_length`, `set_camera_on_path` with `look_at`, `look_ahead`, `bank`, `max_bank` and `closed`), at an even speed by distance and leaning into its turns from the path's own curvature. `shake_view(amplitude, frequency, decay)` shakes it across its own view (right and up, plus a small turn) from seeded noise, laid over whatever camera the scripts set this frame; `set_hand_held(strength, #{ steps })` adds a hand-held drift and a walk's bob; `shot(t, starts)` says which shot of a list a time is in, how far through, and whether it began this frame. `noise(x)` / `noise(x, seed)` is smooth seeded noise in the math module. All run on game time, so a `--frame-step` capture makes the same frames every run. `shake_camera` and the `camera_2d` module are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that draws its own Catmull-Rom, sums sines for a shake or chains `if t < ...` for its cuts can call these instead ([Scripting → Camera Rig API](docs/book/src/concepts/scripting.md#camera-rig-api)). A script that defines its own `fn noise(x)` keeps it: a script's function wins over the engine's.

### `flint edit <scene> --spline --render` shows the spline (#1154)
**What changed.** The track editor's still now draws the spline, its control polygon and its numbered control points over the scene, as the window does. Before, the still left them out with the panels, so it showed only the scene. A selected or hovered point draws as it does in the window (yellow, cyan); at rest every point is white. Every other editor's still is unchanged byte for byte.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `--vsync`, `--frame-latency` and `--no-pacing` on `flint play` and `flint-player`; `lod_scale` and `draw_distance_scale` are quality keys (#1149)
**What changed.** The present mode, the swapchain's frame latency and vsync pacing have real flags: `--vsync off|mailbox|fifo`, `--frame-latency 1..3` and `--no-pacing`. `FLINT_VSYNC`, `FLINT_FRAME_LATENCY` and `FLINT_PACING` still work as aliases, read only when the flag isn't given. The distance levers are quality keys: `--quality lod_scale=0.5,draw_distance_scale=2`, the web page's `?quality=`, an Android dev profile's `[quality]` and a script's `set_quality(#{ lod_scale: 0.5 })` all set them, and `get_quality()` lists them. `set_lod_scale` / `set_draw_distance_scale` set the same two keys. `FLINT_LOD_SCALE`, `FLINT_DRAW_DISTANCE_SCALE`, `FLINT_SHADOWS` and `FLINT_SHADOW_RES` are those keys' aliases. `flint env` and each command's `--help` name the flag or key every aliased switch stands for. A game embedding `PlayerApp` has `launch.vsync`, `launch.frame_latency` and `launch.pacing`, all `None` (the variables, then the defaults) unless it sets them. `RenderContext::with_present(window, PresentOptions)` takes the present mode as an argument; `RenderContext::new` still reads the variables.
**What to do.** Nothing. A perf script can say `--vsync off` in place of `FLINT_VSYNC=0`.

### `flint render`'s per-effect flags are gone: write `--set` and `--style` (#1144)
**What changed.** The 67 flags #708 hid for a release (`--exposure`, `--bloom-intensity`, `--fog-density`, `--kuwahara-radius`, `--contour-width`, `--ascii-ramp`, `--ascii-cell`, `--tear`, `--fxaa` and the rest) are no longer flags. A command line, `--variant` or golden case that gives one fails before drawing, with clap's exit code 2 and an error listing every old flag on the line beside what to write, for example `--exposure 1  ->  --set post.exposure=1` and `--fog-density 0.05  ->  --set post.fog_density=0.05 --set post.fog_enabled=true`. The deprecated `--render-mode`, `--mode-mix` and `--mode-params` (#746) still work; #1036 removes them.
**What to do.** Run each render line in your tools, scripts, variant files and docs once, and paste in the spelling the error prints. The rules: a `[post_process]` key is `--set post.<key>=v` (`flint render --list-effects` lists them). A strength that used to switch its effect on also needs the switch: `post.bloom_enabled`, `post.fog_enabled`, `post.fog_height_enabled`, `post.dither_enabled` or `post.volumetric_enabled`. A flag that turned a style on is `--style <style>:<key>=v`, with colours written `r/g/b`. A flag that only tuned a style is `--set <style>.<key>=v`: `--set ascii.cell=12`, `--set kuwahara.boil=0.5`.

### `flint edit <scene>` orbits like every other editor (#1153)
**What changed.** The scene viewer and the track editor (`--spline`) now use the orbit camera the model, procgen, terrain and particle editors use. A drag turns at the same rate (0.005 rad a pixel, half the viewer's old rate), right-drag pans by a share of the distance, and scroll zooms by 10% a notch instead of one unit. Dragging down now tilts the view to look from higher up, as in the other editors, and the view cube's drag turns the same way. A view cube axis click still eases onto that side, orthographic; a drag that orbits no longer picks the entity under the cursor; O and `[` `]` work in the track editor. In every editor an orbit tilts to 1.56 rad (the old previewers stopped at 1.4), and the turntable stops when a drag orbits, not at the first press. `--watch` is the editors' shared watch, with the same folders and the same rule for user styles' WGSL (#429), and a new WGSL file in a style folder no longer reloads the scene: on Windows the folder itself was reported changed right after the file, and that counted.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A frame's description lists the fog veil's and the selection mask's draws, and names some pipelines more exactly (#1151)
**What changed.** `flint render --frame-dump`, a frame capture's manifest, `flint frame show --draws` and the frame debugger now list the `Fog veil` and `Selection mask` passes' draws by entity, each with the pipeline it drew through (`fog veil mesh (covers)`, `selection mask skinned`...); they listed none before. The style mask and albedo have listed theirs since #1204. A woozy blended skinned mesh's scene pipeline is now `wooze skinned alpha (back, then front)` (and `wooze skinned additive`, `wooze skinned multiply`); it was named as a woozy static mesh's. Every pipeline name now comes from the same choice the pass binds by, so a description can't name a pipeline the frame didn't use. Pictures are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script or diff that matched `wooze alpha (back, then front)` on a skinned draw matches the new name.

### `flint_player::PlayOptions`: the web page and Android read their options as the desktop's flags do (#1142)
**What changed.** The desktop's flags, the web page's query string and Android's `dev_profile.toml` each build one `flint_player::PlayOptions` now (`PlayerArgs::options()`, `PlayOptions::from_query(&Query::parse(search))`), and one `PlayOptions::apply(&mut app)` sets the app from it: msaa, render scale, quality, styles, touch controls, frame step and seed, hold, frame rate, perf and the pass knobs. Each value goes through the same reader everywhere, so `?scale=0.5` and `--render-scale 0.5` are one setting. The desktop still refuses a value it can't read before a window opens. The page used to drop a bad `?msaa=`, `?scale=`, `?touch=`, `?fps=`, `?frame_step=`, `?seed=` or `?hold_at=` without a word. It now says each one in the console (`[flint-web] ?scale=huge: a number from 0.25 to 1, or auto`) and plays on with the default. It also checks a `?style=` word's syntax as `--style` does, and leaves out a malformed one with a warning. An unknown style name still gets through, since a user style registers later. A game binary that sets `app.launch.*` itself is unaffected. `LaunchOptions` now implements `Debug`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A Rust host that sets these launch fields by hand can build a `PlayOptions` and call `apply` instead.

### `flint_cli::run_with` and `CliExtension`: a game's own `flint` with commands of its own (#1140)
**What changed.** flint-cli is a library as well as the `flint` binary. A game binary that calls `flint_cli::run_with(extensions)` is the whole `flint` CLI plus the subcommands its `CliExtension`s add, as `PlayerExtension` adds hooks to the player. `flint_cli::extension::subcommands(|cmd: GameCommands| ...)` turns a derived `clap::Subcommand` enum into one, and `.opens_window_when(..)` names the commands that open a window. They parse with the engine's commands in one parser, so `flint --help` lists them and the global `--json` and `--project` reach them, and they answer through `extension::emit`, `extension::document` and `extension::say`. A name the engine already has (`render`, `help`, a hidden command such as `serve`) or another extension's is refused before anything runs. The stock `flint` is `run_with(Vec::new())` and behaves exactly as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. To give a game's tools a command, see the book's Player Extensions guide, "Commands of the Game's Own". A game binary links its own 8 MB stack on Windows, as a player binary does.

### `flint validate-suite`, `play-suite`, `render-suite`, `calibrate`, `play-chart`, `replay-chart` and `spike-rumble` are gone (#1141)
**What changed.** The old music command names, kept for one release after #709 moved them under `flint music`, are unknown commands now (`error: unrecognized subcommand`, exit 2). So is `flint render-suite <x>.goldens.toml`, #327's old spelling of `flint goldens`, and `flint spike-rumble`. The commands themselves are unchanged under their new names.
**What to do.** In scripts, CI and docs, write `flint music validate`, `music play`, `music render`, `music calibrate`, `music chart play` and `music chart replay`; `flint goldens <x>.goldens.toml` for a golden suite; and `cargo run -p flint-input-capture --example spike_rumble` (from the engine checkout) for the rumble spike. Arguments and flags stay as they were.

### A frame capture keeps each style's history, so `flint frame replay` draws a moving ASCII frame exactly (#1136)
**What changed.** A capture (`flint render --frame-capture`, the player's `--capture-frame-at`, Shift+F2) now writes `replay/history/`: ASCII's tone and edge cells as the frame read them, and a user style's `history` targets, named with their keys in `inputs.json`'s `renderer.style_history`. `flint frame replay` hands them back before it draws, so a frame of moving ASCII that replayed with whole glyphs off (205,431 of 921,600 pixels on the motion bench) is byte-identical, and a `history:` line (JSON `history`) says what each style took or why it drew fresh. `replay.fresh_only` no longer lists `ascii: temporal history` or a user style's history targets. A capture from before this has no history and replays as it did. A game's Rust style that keeps state between frames can hand it out too (`StylePass::export_history` / `import_history`, `flint_render::stylize::history`); one that doesn't draws fresh on replay, as before.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A point light casts a shadow with `cast_shadows = true` (#1112)
**What changed.** `cast_shadows = true` on a point light draws a cube of six shadow maps around it, out to its `range`, softened by its `source_radius` as a spot's is. A lantern on a table shadows the floor around the table on every side. One point light casts, the first by entity name that asks; another that asks lights without one and logs `shadow-cap` naming the one that does. That's a budget of its own, so a spot and a point light can both cast. A point light asking no longer logs "a point light casts no shadow yet". Meshes, skinned meshes, terrain, grass and the ocean take the shadow. The shadow map texture holds one more layer, 16 MB at the default 2048 texels, whose six faces are each a third of `shadow_resolution` wide. `point_light_at` in `local_lights.wgsl` takes the receiver's normal and pixel now, as `spot_light_at` does. A scene that doesn't ask renders as it did.
**What to do.** Nothing. To shadow from a lamp, add `cast_shadows = true` to its point light, and `material.cast_shadows = false` to the lamp's own glass or shade, which would otherwise shadow everything around it.

### `prepare_look(name)`: build a look's styles before it is shown (#1121)
**What changed.** The first `set_look` to a look whose styles had never been drawn built them on that frame, one pipeline after another: ASCII's stopped the game for about a second (976-1083 ms measured on Vulkan, 825-1491 ms on DX12). `prepare_look("ascii_noir")` builds a look's styles on worker threads now, while the game goes on drawing, and the `set_look` that shows it later costs its frame nothing. A frame that needs a style still building waits for that build rather than starting another. Rust hosts have the same through `SceneRenderer::prepare_styles(device, &config)` and `PlayerApp::prepare_look(name)`. On the web there are no threads, so the build happens at the call: make it where a stop is hidden (a loading screen, a fade to black). `flint render` captures ignore it (they build what they draw before drawing it).
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game switches looks mid-play: call `prepare_look` for each look it will show, early (`on_init`), and the switches stop hitching.

### The script hot-reload poll no longer touches the disk on the frame (#1122)
**What changed.** Every frame, the script hot-reload poll used to work out the search's `scripts/lib/` folders again, which stat'd the disk: 0.2-0.3 ms a frame, and 1-35 ms when the disk was busy. The folders are now worked out once, when a scene's script search is set. The whole poll is now about 0.02 ms (worst 0.08 ms in a traced showcase run, against 14 ms before). A trace shows its steps as `script::poll_take_finished`, `script::poll_failed_at_load`, `script::poll_scan_libs` and `script::poll_each_script`, under `script::poll_scripts`. For a host calling `ScriptSync::lib_dirs()`, it now returns `&[PathBuf]`, not a `Vec`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A `scripts/` folder that appears mid-run beside the scene is still picked up: the folders listed are the ones the search would read, whether they exist yet or not.

### `--skip-pass` / `--repeat-pass` and `?skip_pass=` / `?repeat_pass=`: cost a part of the frame on any GPU (#1317)
**What changed.** `flint play`, `flint-player` and `flint render` take `--skip-pass <label>` and `--repeat-pass <label>:<N>`, and the web page takes `?skip_pass=` and `?repeat_pass=`. The labels are the ones F2 and `--gpu-time` print (`Scene`, `Style edges`, `Bloom`). A skipped part records no work and its targets keep what they last held. A repeated part draws N times inside its one timer scope. Parts that add into what they drew or keep history (`Grass`, `World lines`, `Selection outline`, `UI`, `ASCII`, a user style with a `history` target) draw once and print a warning. A game's Rust style can answer `StylePass::keeps_history` the same way. With neither knob set, nothing changes.
**What to do.** Nothing. To cost a pass where GPU timestamps read 0 (Mali), see the book's Profiling guide, "Costing a part without timestamps".

### `persist_set` with the value a key already holds no longer rewrites the save file (#1119)
**What changed.** Setting a `persist_*` key to the value it already holds is no change: the store stays clean and the save debounce doesn't start. A script that writes its state every frame (`persist_set("zone_unlocked", true)` in `on_update`) used to rewrite the save file once a second on the frame thread, 8 to 70 ms each, a hitch a second in `sync`. A value of another type (`1` after `1.0`) is still a change. A save that does happen now leaves a `save` frame note (detail: `debounce`, `persist_save`, `scene transition` or `exit`), so a hitch it causes names it in the hitch log, `--perf`, the F2 graph and traces instead of `sync +x ms`.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A scene load behind a fade is a `loading` hitch, not a gameplay one: `--max-hitches-of loading` (#1125)
**What changed.** When a script ends a transition's exiting phase with `complete_transition()`, the load's long frame (and the frame after it) is drawn under the script's cover. A hitch there is now classed `loading`: the hitch log's `class` says so, the exit report gives it a `[perf] loading hitches` line, the F2 counters show `+N loading`, and the perf report has `hitches.loading`.
