The game's counts leave it out (`[perf] hitches`, the perf line, `hitches.after_start`), and so do `--max-hitches` and `--max-hitches-startup` unless `--max-hitches-of loading` names it. A scripted run that walks through a covered door no longer fails `--max-hitches 0`. A transition no script covers (none calls `complete_transition()`, or the 10 s timeout ended its fade) is still a gameplay hitch.
**What to do.** Nothing. To hold loads to a limit, gate them by name: `--max-hitches N --max-hitches-of loading`.

### The perf line's `mean N ms a frame`, marked `GPU-bound` when the GPU holds the frames (#1318)
**What changed.** `--perf`'s line, `?perf=1`'s panel and its copied line carry a `mean` part after the frame times: the window's span over its frames, which doesn't snap to whole refreshes as one frame's time does. When the frames are unpaced (`?fps=native`, or the desktop), the player's own work is under half the frame and, under vsync, the frames miss the refresh, it adds `GPU-bound` and the inputs it judged by: `mean 29.41 ms a frame, GPU-bound (guess: unpaced, player 2.10 ms, refresh 8.33 ms)`. Then the mean is the GPU's time a frame, on adapters whose timestamps read nothing too. `tools/web-perf/perfline.mjs` reads it as `throughput`, and `tools/web-perf/run.mjs` gains `--rounds N` and two table columns.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that splits the line on ` | ` sees one more part.

### `flint validate --fix` fails when a fix doesn't reach the file, and reports the file as written (#1116)
**What changed.** `--fix` reads the scene back after writing it and reports on that file. If a fix didn't make it into the file (for example, a prefab entity that two instances' prefixes could both make), the run ends with `N of M fix(es) not written to <scene>, which still has what they fixed: <entities>` and exits 1, whatever the constraint's severity. Before, it printed `not written` and exited 0. The JSON's `fix` object has a new `dropped` list, the actions the file doesn't hold. The diagnostics and `remaining_violations` now describe the file as written, so a value a fix put back in range is no longer reported as `out-of-range`. Before, that stale error made `--fix` exit 1.
**What to do.** A script that runs `flint validate --fix` should treat exit 1 with a `dropped` or `not_written` entry as "fix by hand". Nothing else.

### A frame's `gpu_ms` is its own GPU time, in the hitch log, captures and the frame history (#1104)
**What changed.** The GPU's time for a frame is read back a frame or two after it, and the frame history used to record it on the frame it arrived in, so a GPU-heavy hitch showed its GPU time on a later frame and a short one beside itself. Now each read-back carries the number of the frame it timed and lands on that frame's record: the hitch log's `gpu_ms`, a frame capture's `cpu.frame.gpu_ms` and `cpu.window[].gpu_ms` and the F2 graph's frames are each frame's own. The newest frame or two have `gpu_ms` `null` until theirs comes back. `--perf`'s smoothed GPU lines are unchanged. A game binary that read `SceneRenderer::take_gpu_frame_ms` reads `take_gpu_frame_times()` (`flint_render::GpuFrameTime { index, ms }`), and gives the timer its frame's number with `GpuTimer::set_frame_index`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tool that compared a hitch line's `gpu_ms` with the frame after it can now read the hitch's own.

### A chunk's models, and a script's `spawn_entity` models, draw (#1107)
**What changed.** The player loaded model files only when a scene was entered, so an entity a `load_chunk` brought in, or a script made with `spawn_entity` and `set_field(id, "model", "asset", ..)`, drew nothing unless the scene already used that model. Now the player loads them: a chunk's on the frame `load_chunk` is carried out (its `model load` frame note inside the `chunk load`), a script's at the end of the frame it was spawned on, through the scene's search (catalog, procgen spec, file), textures and skeletons included. Each file is read once; later spawns of it draw the mesh already loaded. A `model` given to an entity spawned in an earlier frame is still not loaded, and `flint render --scripts` draws only the scene's own models.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that worked around it by naming every streamed model in its scene can drop those placeholder entities.

### F2 and `--perf` count the GPU objects a frame creates, and the memory the renderer holds (#1091)
**What changed.** The renderer counts every buffer, bind group, texture, pipeline and shader module it creates and every buffer and texture write (`flint_render::gpu_counts`). F2's overlay shows `Alloc/frame` (amber when a frame created anything) and GPU memory by class; `--perf` and `?perf=1` add `gpu/frame create ...` and `gpu mem ...` parts; `--exit-after`'s report adds two `[perf]` lines; a frame that creates objects leaves a `gpu objects` frame note. `RenderStats` gains `gpu_calls`, `gpu_memory` and `device_memory`, and `SceneRenderer` gains `frame_gpu_calls()`, `gpu_calls_reading()` and `gpu_memory()`. Inside flint-render the bare `device.create_buffer` and friends are refused by its own `clippy.toml`: use `CountedDevice` / `CountedQueue`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that builds a `RenderStats` literal adds `..Default::default()`; a game's own Rust that calls wgpu directly is not counted.

### `flint edit <scene>`: F2 shows the frame-time graph (#1110)
**What changed.** The scene viewer's F2 overlay now opens with the player's frame-time graph and hitch counters above its rendering stats, each bar split into `update`, `render`, `egui` and `elsewhere`. The viewer times its frames only while F2 is open; closed, it records nothing (it used to keep a second of frame times all the time for its FPS row). An editor built on `flint_viewer::host` can ask for its frames' phases the same way, through `Editor::times_frames` and `Editor::frame_timed(&FrameTimes)`. `GameClock::advance_wall(secs)` paces a wall time the caller measured itself.
**What to do.** Nothing.

## 2026-11-20

### `--perf-report <file.json>` and `scripts/perf-compare.py`: perf numbers as data (#1093)
**What changed.** `flint play` and `flint-player` take `--perf-report FILE` beside `--exit-after`: at exit they write what the `[perf]` lines print as JSON (`format = "flint-perf-report"`, version 1), plus each phase's and script's mean and max over the run, the GPU's passes as spreads, hitch counts, and the machine (adapter, backend, present mode, size, build profile, engine commit). The lines are printed from the same numbers. It records every frame, times scripts and turns GPU timing on, as `--hitch-log` and `--perf` do. `flint render --gpu-time-json` now also writes `format`, `version`, `build_profile` and `engine_commit`; its pass fields are unchanged and shared with the report. `scripts/perf-compare.py a.json b.json` prints each metric's change against a noise floor, refuses reports from different setups, and exits 1 when frame p50 regresses past `--tolerance`. `scripts/perf-run.sh <scene>` runs the player five times with vsync off and keeps the least noisy report.
**What to do.** Nothing. To compare a change: `bash scripts/perf-run.sh <scene> --out before.json`, change, again with `--out after.json`, then `python scripts/perf-compare.py before.json after.json`.

### `flint play` and `flint-player` say what is wrong with a game's schema files (#1064)
**What changed.** A schema file of the game's that spells a type the old way (`type = "float"`), names a type that is none (`type = "integr"`, checked as a string) or writes a key the loader ignores used to be reported only by `flint validate` and `flint check`. The player now says it too, once, when it first reads the schemas: one warning summary a file, as a scene load prints its diagnostics, `schemas/components/lantern.toml: 1 warning (the full list: flint check)` and then `warning[old-type-name] lantern.brightness: ...`. A transition says it again only for a schema directory it had not read before. The engine's own files (an `engine/` subtree) are never reported. The start's `Loaded` line counts these warnings, and so does `flint playtest`, so `flint playtest --strict` fails on one.
**What to do.** Nothing, or fix what it names: `flint check` lists every one with its hint.

### GPU pass times say "unavailable on this adapter" when its timestamps read zero (#1316)
**What changed.** When most of a frame's GPU scopes read exactly zero ticks for a whole 60-frame window (the Pixel 8 Pro's Mali under Chrome), the F2 overlay's GPU section, `?perf=1`'s panel and copied line, and `--perf`'s line say `GPU pass times unavailable on this adapter (N of M scopes read 0 ticks for 60 frames)` instead of listing `0.00`s; a trace's `gpu` track holds one mark of that name and its header a `gpu_pass_times` line; `--exit-after` warns under `[perf] avg GPU`. A game reads it as `RenderStats::gpu_timestamps` or `GpuTimer::timestamps()` (`flint_render::TimestampHealth`). Real pass times are shown as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that parses the perf line's `gpu` part should expect the new wording on such GPUs.

### `--load-report`: where a load's time went, on `flint play`, `flint-player` and `flint render` (#1092)
**What changed.** `flint play` and `flint-player` take `--load-report`, and `--perf` and the web page's `?perf=1` turn it on too. The start-up, every scene transition and every chunk print a table: each stage's ms, share and count (scene read, window, GPU device, audio device, renderer, procgen, models, textures, pipelines, scripts, `on_init`, physics, audio, the first frame...), what no stage named, and the five slowest assets by name. `flint render --load-report` prints the same table up to the first capture, and `--report json` carries it as `load`. Seven new frame-note kinds (`scene read`, `scene teardown`, `physics build`, `audio load`, `animation load`, `particle load`, `on_init`) name a transition's steps in the hitch log and the F2 graph, where that time had no name before.
**What to do.** Nothing. To find a slow load, run once with `--load-report`.

### `flint validate` checks style palettes, fonts, papers and strokes (#1063)
**What changed.** `ascii_style.palette` and `font`, `pencil_style.paper_image` and `stroke_image`, `watercolor_style.paper_image` and `ui_text.font` are `asset` fields now, so a name that is neither a file nor a built-in (`palette = "ambr"`) is a `missing-asset` error whose hint suggests the nearest built-in. Built-in names (`amber`, `cga`, `builtin`) and `"none"` for a paper or stroke image pass with no file. A schema's `asset` field can list such names itself: `builtins = ["none"]`. `palette = "none"` is reported too: the renderer never took it, and drew `cga` with a warning.
**What to do.** Run `flint validate` (or `flint check`) and fix any name it reports; a palette you meant to leave unused can be left out.

### `ocean` waves, `material_overrides`, `water_body` shapes and `camera_tuning` say when a value is the wrong type (#1068)
**What changed.** These components were the last ones read by hand, so a value of the wrong type took its default and nothing said so. They now read through the typed view, as the rest do: the default is still used, and a `bad-type` line names the entity, component and field once (`error[bad-type] entity 'sea' ocean.amplitude: expected a number, got string "high"`, `material_overrides.Glass.emissive`). The same goes for `joint.motor_target` read by `get_joint_target`, a `bone_probe`'s `joint` and the Ocean and Camera panels' starting values. `ocean.seed` and `ocean.num_waves` are integers: `12.0` still reads as 12, but `12.5`, which used to be cut to 12, is now the default and a report. Engine code gains `ComponentView::table(field)`, a table field as a view whose reports name it by key.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless the log now names a mistake in one of your scenes or scripts: fix the value it names.

### The player reads the asset catalog from the project root (#1065)
**What changed.** The player looks up a `model.asset`, `material.texture` or `sprite.texture` name in `<project root>/assets/*.asset.toml` and loads it from `<project root>/.flint/assets`, the catalog `flint validate` and `flint check` ask. It used to read `assets/` and `.flint/assets` in the working directory, so a game played from anywhere but its root loaded its catalogued models as missing (or, from an engine checkout, the engine's catalog), though it validated clean. A transition into another project reads that project's catalog.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game relied on a catalog in the working directory that isn't its project's: move it under the project root (the folder holding `flint.toml`), or pass `--project <dir>`.

### `flint schema --format json-schema` writes `[post_process]` defaults as written; a value at a tuned bound is inside it (#1062)
**What changed.** The scene JSON Schema's `[post_process]` defaults are the decimals the effects declare (`bloom_intensity` `0.04`, not `0.03999999910593033`), so an editor's completion inserts `0.04`. A `[post_process]` value written exactly at a key's tuned bound (`0.7` where the range ends at 0.7) no longer warns that it is outside the range. The book's editor note now says how taplo resolves paths: a `.taplo.toml` rule's `path` and `include` are read against VS Code's workspace folder or `taplo lint`'s working directory, not the folder the file is in; a `#:schema` path is relative to the scene.
**What to do.** Regenerate `scene.schema.json` if you keep one. Open the project folder itself as the VS Code workspace, and run `taplo lint` from the project root.

### A wrong type in `[camera]`, `[environment]` or `[post_process]` is `bad-type` by field, and every scene-table diagnostic has a line (#1061)
**What changed.** `[camera] fov = "wide"` used to be serde's `toml-parse` with a snippet, and stopped at the first such table. `flint validate` and `flint check` now say `error[bad-type] 6:1 [camera] fov: expected f32, got string` with the hint `the scene does not load until the table fits`, once a table, and still check the rest of the scene. Every diagnostic about a scene-level table (a typo in `[living]` or `[wooze]`, an `unknown-table`, a value out of range) now carries the line and column of the key it names, in the text (`5:1 [living] shadowz`) and as `line` / `column` in the JSON; the load's warning summary has it too. A tool that matched `toml-parse` for these tables, or the text `[table] field` at the start of a location, should read the code and fields instead. `scene.version` stays informational: nothing reads it to decide what a scene means (ADR 0115).
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `flint bundle` honours `[project.diagnostics] allow` everywhere (#1060)
**What changed.** The allow list in the packed `flint.toml` now applies to everything the bundle's check finds, the references in scripts, prefabs and other files (`missing-file` and the rest) as well as each scene's diagnostics, so `flint bundle` refuses only what `flint check` reports. Before, a project that allowed a non-scene code passed `flint check` and was refused by `flint bundle`. The bundle says how many it left out (`  check: no problems (1 allowed by flint.toml)`), and its `--json` document has `allowed`.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### A game adds a component to an engine archetype with `extend = true` (#1054)
**What changed.** A schema file's `[archetype.<name>]` may say `extend = true`: the components it lists are then appended to the archetype an earlier schema directory declared (one already listed is not listed twice), each `[archetype.<name>.defaults.<component>]` merges into the earlier one key by key, a key named again taking the new value, and a `visual` replaces the earlier one. An extension of a name nothing earlier declares fails the load, naming the file and the nearest name. Without `extend`, a later layer still replaces an archetype whole, so existing files mean what they did. A key an archetype doesn't hold (a misspelt `extned`), and an extension's `description`, are now `ignored-schema-key` warnings. `flint schema <archetype>` prints `Declared in:` and `Extended by:` and tags each component and default with its file's number; `--format json` adds `declared_in`, `extended_by`, `component_files` and `default_files`. For Rust: `SchemaRegistry::archetype_origin` returns an `ArchetypeOrigin`, and `ArchetypeSchemaDefinition` gained `extend` and `stray`.
**What to do.** A game that copied a whole engine archetype file to add a component or a default can cut it to `extend = true` and what it adds, and stop tracking the engine's copy.

### An inline `particle_emitter`'s `rotation` and `angular_velocity` take effect, and its modern keys load without warnings (#1051)
**What changed.** The `particle_emitter` schema declares every key the emitter reads: `lifetime`, `speed`, `size`, `color`, `alpha`, `size_scale`, `brightness`, `rotation`, `angular_velocity`, `speed_curve`, `bursts`, `forces`, `on_death`, `on_birth`, `trail`, `lighting`, `fog` and `name`, so a scene that writes them no longer logs an `unknown-field` warning per key at every load. The keys that take several forms (a number or `[min, max]`, a curve, `shape` as a name or a `{ kind = .. }` table) use a new schema field type, `any`: the key is known and documented, and the component's reader checks its form. `rotation_min` / `rotation_max`, `angular_velocity_min` / `angular_velocity_max` and `shape_radius` have no schema default any more. The loader wrote those defaults into every emitter, so an inline `rotation = [0, 90]` or `angular_velocity = 180` was replaced by 0 to 360 and 0, and every legacy `shape = "cone"` spawned from a 0.5 m disc instead of a point. A scene that writes the legacy keys plays as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A legacy cone that was meant to spawn from a 0.5 m disc now spawns from a point: write `shape_radius = 0.5`.

### An `ocean` with only `tint` set draws its own sea, not a crimson one (#1041)
**What changed.** `tint_preset` has no default now: unwritten, it names no preset, so the `tint_*` fields fall back on the sea's own colours (`deep_color`, `shallow_color`, `sss_color`, `foam_color`), and everything else (`tint_sky`, `tint_ramp_steps`, `tint_band_wobble`, `tint_pools`, `tint_keep_light`, `tint_below`) falls back on changing nothing. A scene that set `tint` alone used to draw the `blood` preset's sea of blood, because #1035 made `blood` the unwritten preset; now it draws the sea it had. An unknown preset name reads as no preset, and is reported once. In flint-music, `SuiteManifest::reintegration` and `Conductor::previous_re_entry` are gone. The manifest keeps every top-level table it doesn't read in `SuiteManifest::tables`, and flint-rhythm reads its `[reintegration]` from there (`flint_rhythm::Reintegration::of`, `previous_re_entry`). `SuiteSession::schedule_seam` is renamed `schedule_jump`. Suite files are unchanged.
**What to do.** If a scene that set `tint` should still draw the crimson tide, name it: `tint_preset = "blood"`. Float's port (#1035) already names its preset everywhere it sets the tint. Rust that called `schedule_seam` calls `schedule_jump`.

### `flint_living::ResolvedProp` is read by knob: `prop.get(LivingKnob::Softness)` (#1025)
**What changed.** Every Living Props knob is one row of `flint_procgen::living_knob_rows!`, and the enum it makes, `flint_procgen::LivingKnob`, is the one the panel (`flint_debug_ui::Knob`, now a re-export), the runtime and the player all use. `ResolvedProp` lost its field per knob: `prop.get(knob)` returns that knob's `Resolved<KnobValue>` and `prop.iter()` every one. `KnobValue` is `Number`, `Count`, `Flag` or `Name` (a stagger by name) and compares with a plain `f32`, `u32`, `bool` or `Stagger`. The scene-table readers that were `flint_player`'s (`defaults_of`, `species_of`, `species_problems`, `apply_preset` and the per-knob checks) are `flint_living`'s now; `flint_player::apply_living_preset` and `flint_player::species_problems` still work. `clear_living`'s error lists its keys in the panel's order. `flint render --report json` is unchanged, key for key and byte for byte.
**What to do.** A game binary that read `resolved.softness.value` writes `resolved.get(LivingKnob::Softness).value`; one that called `Knob::desc()` or `Knob::in_section` imports `flint_debug_ui::KnobPanel`. Scenes, presets and scripts need nothing.

### Point and spot lights light grass and water (#1048)
**What changed.** A `point` or `spot` light now reaches grass (`grass_ground` and a terrain's grass) and the ocean and water bodies, as it reaches meshes and terrain: a lantern in a meadow lights the blades around it, and a lamp over water lays a streak of cel glints and a little scattered light on the waves. Before, both read only the sun, so a lamp's pool stopped at the grass line and the waterline. The casting spot's shadow (`cast_shadows = true`) falls on both too. A scene with no point or spot light draws exactly as before; one with lamps near grass or water gets brighter there.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene that leaned on the old hole (a lamp placed by water or grass and tuned bright to make up for it) may want its `intensity` turned down.

### `flint edit` keeps its undo history over a reload, F4's Commit is in `.flint/viewer-edits.jsonl`, and `flint validate --fix` writes prefab overrides (#1046)
**What changed.** When the scene file changes on disk while the viewer has it open (an agent editing it), the reload keeps every undo and redo step whose field still holds what the step left there, so Ctrl+Z still undoes your last edit; a field the other writer changed ends that field's history. F4's **Commit to scene** appends a line for each `[post_process]` key it wrote to `.flint/viewer-edits.jsonl`, with `entity: null`, `component: "post_process"` and `table` naming the table (`post_process.styles.ascii`). `flint validate --fix` writes a fix to an entity a prefab instance makes into `[prefabs.<instance>.overrides.<entity>.<component>]`, the same place the viewer's save writes it. Before, it printed `not written`. It still refuses archetype, parent and whole-component changes there.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tool that reads `viewer-edits.jsonl` should expect `entity` to be `null` on a commit's lines.

### A scene that fails to load is named: `Failed to load scene levels/cart.scene.toml` (#1044)
**What changed.** `flint render`, `play`, `playtest`, `edit`, `spline-edit`, `entity` and `query` said only `Failed to load scene` above the cause (a parent cycle, a missing prefab), which names entities but not the file; each now names the scene, as `flint check` does. `flint edit` said nothing of a scene at all, and its hot reload's error names the file too. `flint_scene::load_failed(path)` is the one wording, for a game binary's own commands; `entity create`/`delete` say `Refusing to write <scene>: ...`.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a script matches the bare `Failed to load scene` line: the path now follows it.

### `ocean.tint_preset` picks the sea's tint by name: `blood` or `underside` (#1035)
**What changed.** The `ocean` component has a `tint_preset` key, `"blood"` (the default) or `"underside"`, that the `tint_*` fields fall back on when unwritten, so a script switches the sea between the crimson tide and the surface seen from below with one `set_field` and drives `tint` alone. The `tint_*` fields no longer carry a schema default: a load wrote it into the component, where it would beat the preset. A scene or script that writes them reads as before, and one that writes none draws the crimson tide as before. A script's `get_field` of an unwritten `tint_*` field now returns `()` instead of the crimson tide's value.
**What to do.** Nothing. To show the underside that `set_render_mode(5, ..)` drew, set `tint_preset = "underside"` and `tint` to the dunk's mix (with the `underwater` style on); have one script own the tint fields.

### Living props' runtime is the `flint-living` crate; the resolver lends it a budget (#1024)
**What changed.** `LivingProps` and everything that runs living props over time (`LivingPropRequest`, `LivingPropId`, `LineageDone`, `LivingStats`, `BlendFrame`, `PropBlend`, `LivingDefaults`, `LivingLayer(s)`, `LivingScope`, `LivingShadows`, `Resolved`, `ResolvedProp`, the `*Override`s, `jitter_rate_clamped`, `row_rate_clamped`, `POSE_CACHE_BYTES`) moved from `flint_procgen` to `flint_living`, and `ProcGenResolver` no longer holds the props. Its living methods are `LivingProps` methods that take the resolver: `resolver.add_living_prop(r)` is `props.add_prop(&mut resolver, r)`, `make_living_now()` is `props.make_now(&resolver)`, `process_living(dt)` / `process_living_in(dt, w)` are `props.process(&resolver, dt, w)`, `step_living_now` is `step_now`, `process_living_beside(side, ..)` is `props.process_beside(side, &resolver, ..)`, `settle_living_now(side)` is `props.settle(&resolver, side)`, `set_living_defaults` is `set_defaults`, `clear_living_props` is `clear_props`, and `living_props()` is the props themselves. The resolver gained `living_spec(name)`, `budget_left_ms()` and `set_frame_budget_ms(ms)`. `JITTER_RATE_MAX`, `ROW_RATE_MAX`, `LivingGenerator`, `LivingRig` and the `[living]` / `[wooze]` tables stay in `flint_procgen`. A scene, spec or script sees no difference: frames are byte-identical.
**What to do.** Nothing for a scene or script. A game binary that named one of the moved types adds `flint-living` beside `flint-procgen` and changes the path; `flint-player` builds without `living` no longer link the scheduler.

### An open spline's sweep keeps its width to the end, and `spline_data` has a frame there (#1016)
**What changed.** The last sample of an open spline had a zero `forward`, `right` and `up`, so every `spline_mesh` (and `sweep_v1`) on an open spline narrowed to a point over its last sample spacing, with a degenerate end cap. That sample now takes its direction from just before it: an open sweep keeps its full width and its end cap faces along the path. A script reading `spline_data` at the end of an open spline gets unit `forwards_*`, `rights_*` and `ups_*` instead of zeros. Closed splines are unchanged, and so is every other sample of an open one.
**What to do.** Nothing. Open-spline track pieces (FlintKart's crossroads walls, verges and shortcut) look different at their ends: square instead of a wedge.

### A scene declares its own fields, `[field.<name>]`, and scripts sample them with `field_sample` (#1014)
**What changed.** A scene can declare a field of named numbers by place: `[field.weather] keys = { wind = { min = 0, default = 1 }, fog = { min = 0, max = 1, default = 0 } }`, with `[[field.weather.zone]]` entries written as `[[wooze.zone]]` ones are (`name`, `shape`, `center`, `radius` or `size`, `falloff`, and any of the keys). Scripts read a key by place with `field_sample("weather", "wind", get_world_position(me))` (or `x, y, z`), every key and the zones there with `field_at`, and change it with `field_set`, `field_set_zone` and `field_remove_zone`, each with an optional seconds to ease over; a write is read back at once. They are the `field` module, enabled in a scene with a `[field]` table, in the player, `flint playtest` and `flint render --scripts`. `flint check` and `flint validate` report a wrong key or zone as `bad-field`, by field and key or zone; the player warns and plays without it. The schema is `[table.field]` (`flint schema field`). The wooze field is unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that scripted "windier here" from trigger volumes can declare a field instead.

### `flint schema <component>` shows its F3 panel, and `--format json` carries it as `panel` (#1020)
**What changed.** A component whose fields declare a [panel](../docs/book/src/guides/debug-panels.md#a-panel-from-a-schema) (#744) now prints one line under its header, `Panel "Dead calm": status phase, calm, next_in_s · trigger trigger_calm "Calm now", end_now`, and its JSON (one component or the whole export) has a `panel` object: `title` and `rows`, each with `field`, `role`, `label` and the keys that say more than the defaults (`labels`, `press`, `step`, `min`, `max`, `slider`, `presets`, `format`, `scale`, `unset`, `from`, `commit`). A component with no panel prints and exports as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. To see what a controller's F3 panel shows without opening it: `flint schema <component>`.

### A `style_mask` or `<style>_style` on a billboard sprite now styles it (#1011)
**What changed.** A billboard (`sprite` with `mode = "billboard"`, the default) that asks for a per-entity style (`style_mask`, or a style's own component such as `ascii_style`) is drawn into the style mask, cut out by its texture's alpha as the scene draws it, so only the sprite's shape takes the style. Before, the mask drew no billboards and a styled sprite stayed plain. An unstyled billboard in front of a styled entity still cuts through its style, as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene that put a style on a billboard and relied on it staying plain should take the style off.

### `flint script check` reports a `global::` read that would fail every frame: `unknown-global` (#1007)
**What changed.** `global::NAME` reaches a script's top-level `const`s, and nothing else. Between #997 and #1033 every such read failed in callbacks; #1033 fixed that, and this adds the check that tells the two apart before the game runs. A `global::NAME` read in a script with no top-level `const NAME` is now the error `unknown-global` in `flint script check`, `flint check` and `flint validate`, at its line and column. The hint names a top-level `let` of the same name, which `global::` never reaches, or the nearest constant (did you mean `KEYS`?). Modules aren't judged, since a module's `global::` refers to its own constants. Float's 16 scripts, with 41 `global::` reads, pass.
**What to do.** If the check now fails on a script, make the value a `const` if it never changes, or read it by its bare name in the callback. Until that's fixed, the read fails every time it runs.

### `is_sound_playing(handle)`: a script can ask whether a sound it started still plays (#1000)
**What changed.** The handle from `play_sound` / `play_sound_at` can now be asked about. `is_sound_playing(handle)` is `true` from the frame after the sound starts until it plays out or the frame after `stop_sound`, on the desktop and through the web's worklet alike. It is `false` for a sound that never started (a name with no file, no sound device, a one-shot placed out of earshot, the web before Begin), for a handle nobody was given, and always in a headless capture (`flint render --scripts`), which has no sound. The answer is the mixer's as the frame began, so the frame a sound is started or stopped still reads as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that kept its own timer to know when a voice line ended can ask instead: `if !is_sound_playing(line) { line = play_sound(next) }`.

### `global::NAME` inside a method call's arguments works; transition checks walk the script (#998)
**What changed.** A top-level constant read inside a method call's arguments, as in `keys.push(global::GLOW)`, failed with "Variable not found: global::GLOW" since #1012: the scan that finds a script's `global::` reads skipped those arguments (Rhai's AST walk doesn't enter them). It now enters them, nested calls included. Whether a script may call `complete_transition()` (which decides if the player waits for a script to end a scene transition) is now found by the same AST walk instead of searching the AST's `Debug` text, about 100 times faster at each load and reload. A function whose name only contains the text, such as `complete_transition_soon()`, no longer counts as the call, so a scene with no real `complete_transition()` no longer waits out the 10 s timeout.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `flint` and `flint-player` link an 8 MB stack on Windows however they're built (#990)
**What changed.** A Windows exe's main thread gets the stack the exe asks for, 1 MB unless told otherwise. The engine asked for 8 MB only in `.cargo/config.toml`, which cargo reads only when it runs inside the engine's checkout, so `cargo build --manifest-path engine/Cargo.toml` from a game's root linked 1 MB. The two binaries now ask for it in their build scripts (`/STACK:8388608` on MSVC, `-Wl,--stack,8388608` on MinGW), and a build from a game's root gets 8 MB. Measured in debug, a `flint init` scene needs 778 KB for `flint render --scripts` and 905 KB for `flint-player` to play it, so a game's deeper scripts could overflow 1 MB.
**What to do.** Nothing for the engine's binaries. A game binary of its own (one embedding `PlayerApp`) isn't reached by another package's build script: copy the short `build.rs` in guides/player-extensions.md ("A Game Binary") to give it the same stack.

### `FLINT_STYLE_SCISSOR=0` keeps ASCII's edge cells round its entities; `StyleFrame::styled_area` (#930)
**What changed.** With the per-entity style scissor off (`FLINT_STYLE_SCISSOR=0`, `SceneRenderer::set_style_scissor(false)`, the web's `?scissor=0`), per-entity ASCII's edge cell pass is still cut to the cells round its entities, as it always was with the scissor on. Drawn whole, it kept unstyled geometry's lines in the cells past them, and a moving prop's edge history (#477) then differed from the scissored frame's: two outline pixels, the frame after a jump. Now both are the same frame after frame. The default (scissor on) draws exactly as before; every golden is unchanged. A game's Rust style gets `StyleFrame::styled_area(pad)` (`styled_rect` with the scissor on or off) and `StyleFrame::scissor` (the renderer's switch) for state it carries between frames.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `draw_rect_outline` strokes outside its rect again, as before the egui 0.36 port (#989)
**What changed.** Since the egui 0.36 port (#945), a script's `draw_rect_outline` put half of its stroke inside the rect: a 4 px outline on a 100 x 40 box covered 8 to 112 across where it had covered 6 to 114, so an outlined box drew smaller and a thick outline crept over what was inside it. It is back outside the rect, where egui 0.30 drew it, measured against a wgpu 23 build pixel for pixel. The same goes for what a UI document outlines: a `rect` element with a `thickness`, and an element's `border_width`. Flint's own outlines (the scene tree's selection, the gradient editor's bar, the music guide and manifest map) are back outside too.

**What to do.** Nothing, for a HUD tuned before the port. A HUD tuned since it (on 0.36, from 14 November) whose outlines should sit where they sit now: move each outline in by half its thickness and shrink it by one thickness, or draw it as a filled frame.

### `engine/examples/` is gone: the examples are the flint-examples repository (#984)
**What changed.** The curated example scenes left the engine's tree for
[flint-examples](https://github.com/chrischaps/flint-examples), with their
history, the scenes at its root and the engine as a squashed subtree at
`engine/`, like a game; the showcase film's rig (`tools/showcase/`) went with
them. A game's `engine/` no longer carries them (45 MB less). `flint
examples` with no `--dir` now reads the project root's `index.toml`, so it
works in a flint-examples checkout; in a game, pass `--dir`. The engine's
schemas, including the room-builder layer `engine/schemas/examples`, stay.
**What to do.** A game that names a file under `engine/examples/` (a model,
a texture, a scene in a transition) copies it into its own project first;
`flint check <dir>` lists the misses. A sparse-checkout line
`'!/engine/examples/'` can go. To read or run an example, clone
flint-examples.

### `flint goldens` uploads each texture and mesh once a run, and the shimmer cases draw 30 frames (#961)
**What changed.** In one process, `flint goldens` keeps the models' and image files' textures and the meshes' vertex and index buffers on the GPU device for the next case, as it keeps pipelines since #911. They are keyed by their bytes, so a changed file still uploads fresh. On WARP a case on a scene with several 2048-texel textures took 3 to 13 s just to upload them. The run's last line now ends `textures: N uploaded, M reused; mesh buffers: N uploaded, M reused`, and `--fresh-pipelines` uploads every case's anew too. While the cache is on, `TextureCache::update_rgba` refuses a kept texture. The stylize suite's two shimmer cases draw 30 frames, not 60; their means per frame are the same at any count from 20 up.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that parses the summary line should allow the new parts.

### `cargo clippy` in the engine fails on a read past the vfs in every crate (#979)
**What changed.** The root `clippy.toml`'s rules (the vfs reads, `toml::Value::as_float`, the wgpu and egui seams) were warnings in most engine crates and errors only in the five that draw (#761). They are now `disallowed_methods = "deny"` in `[workspace.lints.clippy]`, and every engine crate takes it with `[lints] workspace = true`; the five drawing crates' own `[lints.clippy]` tables are gone. Native-only code (`cfg(not(target_arch = "wasm32"))`) is held too, which only the wasm check did before. `scripts/ci.sh` refuses a member without `[lints] workspace = true` or with a `[lints.*]` table of its own. Nothing in the engine called a listed method, so nothing was annotated.
**What to do.** Nothing for a game's own build or clippy: engine crates lint as members of the engine's workspace, not the game's. A game that patches engine code in `engine/` and runs `cargo clippy` there must route a new read through `flint_core::vfs`, or mark a deliberate one with `#[allow(clippy::disallowed_methods)] // <reason>`.

### `play_sound` finds a sound nobody preloaded, and `flint check` holds script sounds to that search (#975)
**What changed.** A sound name a script plays that nothing cached (no `audio_source.file`, not a file of an `audio/` the scene sees) is now looked for on its first play as an `audio_source.file` is: a path from the scene's folder or any folder above it up to the project root, then each `audio/`, then the engine's. So `play_sound("../sfx/door.ogg")` plays, where it used to warn "Sound not cached", and with `preload_audio = false` a script's sounds still play, loaded when first asked for (a large one misses that first play). `flint check` now holds a script's sound strings to that same search, so it reports what the player can't find: a sound beside the script, or in `models/` or another asset folder, used to pass and never played, and is now a `missing-file` error with the paths tried.
**What to do.** Run `flint check`. Move or rename any sound it reports, or write it as a path from the scene. A copy kept in `audio/` only so a script's written path would play can go, if the file it copies is inside the game: a web bundle packs the game's folder, not one outside it.

### The examples' `room` archetype lists `bounds` (#974)
**What changed.** `schemas/examples/archetypes/room.toml` said a room was a
"transform-only parent container" while the layer's `rooms_have_bounds`
constraint failed `flint check` on every room without `bounds`. The archetype
now lists `components = ["transform", "bounds"]` and calls a room the box of
its bounds, drawn as an outline. It gives no default box: an archetype's
`components` list is what `flint schema room` prints, and nothing at load
reads it, so scenes load and draw exactly as before (renders byte-identical).
`cargo test -p flint-constraint --test schema_files` now fails a
`required_component` constraint that asks an archetype for a component the
archetype doesn't list.
**What to do.** Nothing, if your rooms write their `bounds`. An entity that is
only a level's root (it holds walls, floors and lamps) should carry no
archetype, as the showcase levels' roots have since #804; a `room` without
`bounds` still fails `rooms_have_bounds`.

### ASCII's cell silhouettes are decided once a cell: the glyph shader compiles in about a second on FXC (#929)
**What changed.** Which snapping variant (`silhouette = "cell"`) holds a cell is now a small pass of its own, a texel a cell of each snapping grid, and the glyph pass reads one texel a grid where every pixel used to make 16 mask reads. The pictures are the same to the byte. On DX12 with FXC (`WGPU_DX12_COMPILER=fxc`, and every game that doesn't ship DXC) the glyph shader's two entry points compiled in 10.5 s in a debug build and 7.2 s in a release build; they now take 0.9 s and 1.6 s, so the first ASCII frame no longer stalls. A frame whose snapping grids would need more than 8192 rows of the new state draws the variants past them cut to the pixel and says so once (`rows of snap owner state`).
**What to do.** Nothing.

### Scripts can find a way round walls: `find_path` and `move_along_path` (#969)
**What changed.** A new `nav` script module. `find_path(fx, fy, fz, tx, ty, tz[, opts])` returns the way between two points as `[#{x, y, z}, ...]` on the floor, round walls and up ramps and steps, or `[]` when there is none. It works over the floors the scene's static colliders, terrain and spline roads make, baked into a grid a tile at a time as searches reach it. The agent is 0.8 m across, 1.8 m tall and climbs 0.35 m steps unless an opts map says otherwise (`radius`, `height`, `step`, `max_slope`, `cell`, `max_nodes`). `move_along_path(id, path, speed)` walks an entity along a path for the frame and returns `#{x, y, z, dir_x, dir_z, next, remaining, arrived}`. Doors, lifts and other agents are not on the grid yet. A script that declared its own `find_path` or `move_along_path` with these arities now shadows the engine's.
**What to do.** Nothing. To use it, see the book's Physics and Runtime page, "Navigation", and `examples/nav_lamplighter.scene.toml`.

### `flint asset resolve` reads every `asset` field your schemas declare (#968)
**What changed.** `resolve` used to read four fields (`model.asset`, `material.texture`, `sprite.texture`, `audio_source.file`) and five old top-level keys (`mesh`, `texture`, `material`, `audio`, `script`), and it only asked the asset catalog. Now it reads every field your loaded schemas declare as `type = "asset"`. That includes your game's own fields, scripts, terrain maps, particle textures, spline files, prefab entities and `[environment] skybox`. A reference counts as found when the catalog holds it or the engine's file search finds it, the same lookup `flint check` uses. So a scene that plays no longer reports 0 found. `--json` gains a `references` list. `audio_source.file` is now `type = "asset", kind = "audio"`, so `flint check` checks it like other assets. `resolve` takes `--schemas`. In Rust, `flint_asset_gen::batch::resolve_scene` and `scan_scene_assets` are replaced by `resolve_missing`, which takes a `SceneNeeds` (the count and the missing list). `flint_scene::asset_references` builds that list.
**What to do.** Nothing for scenes. Move an entity's top-level `mesh = "..."` into its component (`model.asset`). Batch strategies now exit 1 when a missing reference is one no provider makes, such as a script.

### The renderer keeps bind groups over any depth view; `new_depth_id` and `depth_id` are gone (#960)
**What changed.** Bind groups over a host's depth view, and over the shadow map, fog map, style edges, albedo and pencil's images, are now kept by the objects themselves (wgpu 30 compares its handles by identity), not by ids and sizes standing in for them. A bare `&TextureView` handed to `encode_to` / `render_to` is now kept from frame to frame like `RenderContext::depth_target()`'s, where it used to make its groups every frame. `flint_render::new_depth_id`, `RenderContext::depth_id`, `HeadlessContext::depth_id` and `DepthKey::Frame` are removed; `DepthTarget::new` takes the view alone. A Rust style's `StyleFrame::edges_key` / `albedo_key` are replaced by `bind_over` / `depth_bind_over` with `edges_or` / `albedo_or`. Pictures are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing for scenes and scripts. A game binary that made its own depth texture and took a `new_depth_id()` for it drops that call and passes the view; one with a Rust style that keyed a bind group on `edges_key()` passes `&[frame.edges_or(&blank)]` to `depth_bind_over` instead.

### Creases in the shared edges mark two pixels at a fold too, so pencil and ASCII crease lines are thinner (#952)
**What changed.** The shared edge pass (`edges.wgsl`, read by `pencil`, `ascii`, `contour`, `edges_view` and user styles with `inputs = ["edges"]`) rebuilds each surface normal from one neighbour on each axis. Since #743 it took the neighbour nearer in view distance, which finds the right side of a depth step but not of a fold, where the distance runs on unbroken: the steeper face's last pixel took a tangent across the fold, and a crease marked a band 3 to 4 px wide (a pillar's foot seen square on, a box's vertical corner). Each tangent now comes from the side whose two neighbours lie on the pixel's own plane, so a fold marks the two pixels astride it. Crease lines in pencil, ASCII and `edges_view` are thinner at folds, and a few ASCII crease glyphs on small curved props change. Silhouettes are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game with its own pencil or ASCII goldens may see crease pixels move by a pixel and should re-bless them after a look.

## 2026-11-19

### A scene's unknown `[post_process]` key no longer stops `set_look` and F4's Look (#896)
**What changed.** A scene whose `[post_process]` held a key that isn't a field (Float's scenes had `enabled = true` and `vignette = 0.18`) loaded with a `warning[unknown-field]`, then failed every look laid under its keys: the scene's own `look = "..."` (in `flint render` and the player alike), `set_look`, F4's Look picker, comic panels and `flint render --scripts` all printed `[post_process] has no field 'enabled'` and drew the frame without the look. When a look is laid under the scene's keys, the scene's keys now go on as the loader read them: an unknown key is left out, and a value past its tuned range (which only warns at load) is drawn as written. The load-time warning is still the only message about such a key, so `flint check` no longer calls such a scene's own look a `bad-look` error. A look file's own mistakes are refused as before, naming the look.
**What to do.** Nothing. To quiet the warning, remove the key (`flint check` names it); `vignette = x` is `vignette_enabled = true` with `vignette_intensity = x`.

### `blend_source = "field"`: a prop's style follows a screen field your scripts fill (#859)
**What changed.** `blend_source` on a style component (`ascii_style`, `pencil_style`, a user style's `<name>_style`) or a `style_mask` takes a third value, `"field"`. The entity's blend is then its `blend` times the **style field** at each pixel. The style field is a screen field that scripts fill each frame with the new `style_field` module: `field_disc(x, y, radius, soft[, strength])`, `field_ring(x, y, radius, width, soft[, strength])` and `field_cone(x, y, direction, spread, length, soft[, strength])`, in logical points, with angles in degrees clockwise from up. Like the draw calls, it is immediate mode: a frame that draws no shape has no field, and a prop following it draws as at blend 0. Each frame holds 16 shapes at most. A host that builds `flint_render::FrameInputs` by hand gets a new field, `style_field` (`&[]` by default). `"own"` and `"tear"` are unchanged, and a whole-frame style's table still takes only those two.
**What to do.** Nothing. To use it, see `examples/ascii_shockwave.scene.toml` and the book's "A blend that follows a screen field".

### `spline_is_gap` and `spline_gap_at` are gone: a spline's control points carry `attrs`, read with `spline_point_attr` and `spline_point_attr_span` (#855)
**What changed.** A `[[control_points]]` entry in a `.spline.toml` takes `attrs = { surface = "ice", speed = 0.6 }`: each value holds from that point to the next, and neighbours with equal values make one span (on a closed spline a span can run through the first point). `spline_point_attr(spline, name, t)` returns the value at `t`, `()` where it is unset; `spline_point_attr_span(spline, name, t)` returns `#{value, start_t, end_t}` or `()`. A point's `gap_after = true` reads as the attribute `gap_after`, `true` over each gap. The two gap functions are removed (a call to either is now an unknown function), and `spline_data`'s `gap_starts`, `gap_ends` and `gap_count` are replaced by `attrs.<name>.starts`, `.ends` and `.values`. `attrs` may not name `gap_after`. Gaps sweep and collide exactly as before.
**What to do.** A game with gap logic: `spline_is_gap(s, t)` becomes `spline_point_attr(s, "gap_after", t) == true`, and `spline_gap_at(s, t)` becomes `spline_point_attr_span(s, "gap_after", t)` (same `start_t` and `end_t`, `()` outside a gap). A script reading `gap_*` through `get_field` reads `attrs` instead.

### Scripts: `set_post(key, value)` sets any `[post_process]` key; the old post setters are deprecated (#853)
**What changed.** One function now sets any key of the scene's `[post_process]` table, by the scene's own name for it: `set_post("vignette_intensity", 0.4)`, `set_post("fog_color", [r, g, b])`, `set_post("fxaa", true)`, `set_post("ssao_samples", 16)`. A key that doesn't exist, or a value of the wrong kind, is a script error that lists the keys, and `flint script check` catches a misspelled literal key. The 13 single-purpose setters still work for this release and warn in `flint script check`:
`set_vignette(v)` → `set_post("vignette_intensity", v)` (it also set `vignette_enabled` to `v > 0.001`) · `set_bloom_intensity` → `bloom_intensity` · `set_exposure` → `exposure` · `set_chromatic_aberration` → `chromatic_aberration` · `set_radial_blur` → `radial_blur` · `set_ssao_intensity` → `ssao_intensity` · `set_fog_density` → `fog_density` · `set_fog_color(r, g, b)` → `set_post("fog_color", [r, g, b])` · `set_desaturation` → `desaturate` · `set_dof` → `dof_strength` · `set_dof_focus(d, r)` → `dof_focus_distance`, `dof_focus_range` · `set_tear` → `tear` · `set_tear_shape(s, iris)` → `tear_scale`, `tear_iris`. A Rust host: `ScriptCommand::SetFogColor` and `ScriptHost::set_fog_color` are gone (the colour rides in `SetPostProcess`), and `PostProcessOverrides` is a key map (`get`, `f32`, `iter`).
**What to do.** Replace each old call with its `set_post` form; `flint script check` lists them. If you call `set_vignette` in a scene whose `vignette_enabled` is false, add `set_post("vignette_enabled", true)`.

### A call into a script module the scene hasn't enabled is logged once a scene (#856)
**What changed.** Since #698 a call such as `tune_living`, `set_wooze` or `conducted_coherence` in a scene without the feature is a script error at its line. Since #693 a repeated error is logged at its 1st, 10th, 100th... time, so a shared HUD calling one of them in `on_update` still printed three lines in ten seconds and more as it ran. That error is now logged once for each call site in each scene: again only after a scene change or a reload of the script's file. It is still counted on every call, shown on the player's error card, listed in the end-of-run summary and `--report json`, and fails a run under `--strict-scripts`. Every other script error is logged as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that should make no error at all in such scenes can ask first: `if module_enabled("living") { tune_living(knobs) }`.

### The `debug` script module (`compare_living`, `commit_living`...) needs the `debug-hud` feature (#854)
**What changed.** The Living Props panel's script calls (`commit_living`, `compare_living`, `flip_living`, `split_living`, `stop_comparing_living`) are enabled only in a player built with its `debug-hud` feature, as well as in a scene with living props. `debug-hud` is on by default and in `flint`'s own player, so `flint play`, `flint render --scripts` and `--frame-step` captures run them as before. A game binary built without `debug-hud` gets a script error at the call: `compare_living: the debug module is not enabled here; it is enabled when the player is built with its debug-hud feature (on by default) and the scene has living props, ...`. The `living` and `wooze` modules are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing for a game that keeps the default features. A game that ships without `debug-hud` and calls these from a script should call them only in its dev build, or ask `module_enabled("debug")` first.

## 2026-11-18

### `form_v1` rows drift along their lines: `row_pace`, `row_pace_spread`, `[living] row_rate` (#576)
**What changed.** A `form_v1` spec with `card_layout = "rows"` takes `row_pace` (metres a second, default 0) and `row_pace_spread` (0 to 1, default 0.3): the words travel along their lines, slide out under one margin and back in under the other, each line at its own pace. They read a row clock that a new run-time knob, `row_rate` (0 to 8, unset 1), runs faster or slower: a scene's `[living] row_rate`, `[living.species.<spec>] row_rate`, a preset, the F3 panel's row rate slider, or `tune_living(#{ living: #{ row_rate: r } }[, seconds])`. A new rate moves no word on the frame it's set. Echoes show the lines a little before or after now. `flint render --report json` adds each prop's `row_time` and its `row_rate` value. At `row_pace = 0` nothing changes: no bones are added and every spec draws as before. Example: `examples/drifting_page.scene.toml`.
**What to do.** Nothing. To drift a page's writing, add `row_pace` to its `[params]`.

### `flint style check --json` gives each error in parts; style paths print with `/` (#567)
**What changed.** Under the global `--json`, each entry of `styles` now carries `loops`, `bounded_loops` and `texture_reads` (`null` for a style that doesn't compile), `warnings`, and `errors`: one object an error with `file`, `line`, `col`, `message`, the quoted `source` line and `hints`. `error`, the whole text, is still there for a style that fails. On Windows, every path in a user style's messages (the check, the load errors, the in-game error card) and in `flint style new`'s output prints with `/`: `styles/x.wgsl:38:15` where it said `styles\x.wgsl:38:15`, and `demo/styles/x.wgsl` where it said `demo/styles\x.wgsl`. Elsewhere nothing moves.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that read `error` keeps working; one that matched a `\` in a style's message on Windows should match `/`.

### The window's frame on request, and `--record` (#583)
**What changed.** A line with a `capture` key on `--expression-listen`, `{"capture": "look.png", "id": 7}` or `{"capture": true}`, writes the next frame the window presents (scene and HUD) to `--capture-dir` (default `captures/` at the project root) and answers on the same connection: `{"captured": "<absolute path>", "id", "width", "height", "t", "frame", "state"}`, `state` being the last expression state the scripts heard, or `{"capture_error": "<why>"}`. Such a line is not a state: scripts never hear it, and a state object that carries a `capture` key is now refused instead of delivered. `flint play --record DIR --record-fps N` (default 8) writes the window's frames as `frame_000001.png`... with `frames.jsonl` (game, wall and Unix time) and `frames.ffconcat` for ffmpeg. Neither waits on the GPU, so the frame clock is unchanged. Desktop only.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game's socket states use a top-level `capture` field: rename it.

### `--expression-listen` opens once the first frame is presented (#584)
**What changed.** The socket no longer listens from the moment the player starts. It binds at the start of the second frame, once the first has been presented, and says so in one log line under the new `listen` target, which the default filter and `--quiet` both keep: `[listen] expression: listening on 127.0.0.1:7777 from frame 2 (game time 0.017 s)`. Until then a connect is refused, so a client that has connected knows a state it sends is heard on the frame it arrives at, instead of states sent during startup (seconds, in a debug build) being drained together on the first frame. A malformed or non-loopback address still fails the run at start; a taken port now fails it when the socket opens, as an error under `listen`, and the player exits non-zero. The F3 Expression panel says "opens after the first frame" until it does.
**What to do.** A client that connects once and gives up on a refusal should retry until it connects, or wait for the `listen` line. A launcher that waited on a renderer log line plus a delay can drop both.

### A script-heavy game's trace covers its whole look-back, and the readers say when one doesn't (#1288)
**What changed.** While the recorder records, a script's calls of an engine function (`get_field()`, `clamp()`) are no longer one scope a call: each function is one *summed* record a scope that called it, laid from the first call for their total, with `calls` and `worst_us` in its args. A call that recorded a scope inside it stays a scope of its own. A ring that wraps inside the window is now named in the header (`covered_from_ms`, `cut_threads`) and in the run's `[trace] wrote` line, with the `--trace-seconds` that would hold the window. `flint profile top`, `frame`, `explain`, `diff` and `flint frame open` read only the frames the scopes cover, say where they begin, and count a summed record as its calls; an older trace is read the same way from its `wrapped` header.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tool of your own that reads trace files should treat an event with a `calls` arg as a leaf (it may overlap its siblings) standing for that many calls.

### `flint render --strip`: a `--time` span or list as one PNG contact sheet (#574)
**What changed.** `flint render <scene> --time 0..8 --strip --every 1 --columns 3 -o x.png` writes the span as one labelled PNG: a tile every `--every` seconds (default 1), the span's end included when a step lands on it, each tile labelled `t = 2 s`. A list (`--time 2,5,9.5 --strip`) makes a tile a moment. Tiles are 640 x 360 unless `--width`/`--height` say otherwise, `--columns` (alias `--cols`) sets how many go across, and `--label off` draws the tiles alone, 4 pixels apart, for a script to cut back out. Each tile is the pixels of `--time <t>` rendered alone at that size: the clocks run on the GIF's `--frame-step`, and each tile is drawn as a first frame (a style's history starts fresh). Nothing else a render writes changed.
**What to do.** Nothing. A gate script that renders each second as its own still and tiles them can call `--strip` instead.

### User style images: folders listed while the game runs, and `mips = true` (#558)
**What changed.** An image option with no `choices` (it offers the images in its default's folder) now lists that folder again while the player or the scene viewer runs: a PNG added there or deleted changes its choices with no save of the `.shader.toml`, and the game's settings for the style are kept. `StyleRegistry::reload_changed_for`'s `Reload` has a new field, `rechosen`, for these styles; `changed()` counts it. `[images.<name>]` and an image option take `mips = true`, which uploads a mip chain: `flint_image_<name>` and `flint_image_tiled_<name>` then read the level the screen asks for, and `flint_image_lod_<name>`, `flint_image_level_<name>` and `flint_image_tiled_level_<name>` are new. An image without `mips` draws exactly as before. `examples/styles/darkroom`'s paper has it.
**What to do.** Nothing. A host that builds `Reload` by hand adds `rechosen`. Give a paper or brush sheet that is drawn smaller than its texels `mips = true`; leave it off for a LUT.

### User styles warn about a derivative under a branch, which the browser refuses (#573)
**What changed.** `flint style check`, `flint render`, `flint check` and `flint bundle` now warn, at the line and column, about a `dpdx`, `dpdy`, `fwidth` or `textureSample` in a user style where the pixels of a quad may not all be: under an `if` or `switch` on a value that can differ per pixel, in a loop whose turns can, or after a `return` only some take. Natively such a style draws, but the browser's WebGPU refuses the whole module, so on the web it drew its input unchanged. A branch on `params` counts: it is a per-pixel `var<private>`. The style still loads and packs; nothing is refused.
**What to do.** Run `flint style check styles/` on your game. For each warning, take the derivative before the branch and use the result inside it, or put `@diagnostic(off, derivative_uniformity)` on the function when a quad's pixels always agree.

### A trace's `engine_commit` and a capture's `engine.commit` name the engine the binary was built from (#1290)
**What changed.** Both were the HEAD of the repository the player ran in, so a player run in a game's checkout named the game's commit as the engine's. They are now the commit the engine's sources were at when the binary was built, stamped at compile time by `flint-player`'s build script; `engine_dirty` / `engine.dirty` is whether that tree has uncommitted changes. A player a game builds from its `engine/` subtree names the game's commit (which pins the engine) with `engine_subdir` / `engine.subdir` = `engine/`. A build that couldn't tell writes no `engine_commit` (`commit: null`), and the views say `at an unknown commit`. The project's checkout is kept beside it: a trace's `project_commit` / `project_dirty`, a manifest's `project { commit, dirty }`. `flint frame replay` compares a capture's commit with the replaying binary's build. Older traces and captures still read.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tool that read `engine_commit` as the game's revision reads `project_commit` now.

### A user style's `<name>_style` component is checked: a misspelt or mistyped field says so (#556)
**What changed.** A user style that reads the mask gives entities a `<name>_style` component. That component had no schema file, so the engine dropped `hologram_style { glwo = 2 }` or `glow = "bright"` without a word. The engine now makes the component's schema from the style's `.shader.toml`: each `[params]` entry a number with its range, each option one of its choices, and `enabled`, `blend` and `blend_source`. `flint check` and `flint validate` report a misspelt field as `unknown-field` (a warning, with the nearest name as its hint), a wrong type as `bad-type` and a value past its range as `out-of-range`. `flint play`, `flint render` and `flint edit` log the same lines when they bind the scene. A game's `<name>_style` with no schema file is no longer an `unknown-component` error in `flint check`. A schema file you wrote with fields of your own is kept.
**What to do.** Nothing, beyond fixing any field the new lines name. A game that declared its user style's component open in `schemas/components/` to quiet `unknown-component` can keep that file: the loader in `flint play` still needs the name.

### `flint frame open` opens the profiler on the frame a file was saved for, and `hitch` means one frame everywhere (#1289)
**What changed.** A `--trace-on-hitch` trace opens on the hitch its header names (`saved on hitch at t=6.004 s`), not the worst hitch in it. A capture's Profiler tab opens on the frame the capture holds (the manifest's `frame.index`), or on its hitch when it was taken on one, so P switches between two views of one frame; it used to fall back to the longest frame, which a short trace might not even hold. `flint profile frame|explain <trace> hitch` and `--trace-frame hitch` now follow one rule: the hitch a `--trace-on-hitch` file was saved for, matched by its game time, else (for a trace saved on exit, a key or a capture) the worst hitch. Before, `flint profile` took the last hitch on the track, which for an exit trace with several hitches was a different frame.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that read `flint profile explain <exit trace> hitch` as "the last hitch" asks for that frame by number instead.

### `transition = "dissolve"` turns exactly `blend` of a prop's cells, outline slivers included (#554)
**What changed.** An ASCII prop that dissolves (`ascii_style { transition = "dissolve" }`) ranks each of its cells in the dissolve order and turns those ranked below `blend` (#398). Before, a cell counted as the prop's only when one of its 16 sample points landed on the prop, so a thin edge or an outline sliver between them took the unranked order, and a prop could turn a cell or two more or fewer than its share (a barrel of about 40 cells at blend 0.5 turned 47-56% of them over 17 placements). Now a cell counts when any of its pixels is the prop's, so the same barrel turns exactly half at every placement. A prop with `silhouette = "cell"` is unchanged: its cells are still the ones that cover `cell_share` of the samples. A dissolve part-way through can show a cell or two differently at its edge; blend 0 and 1 are the same.
**What to do.** Nothing. A golden of a dissolving prop may need re-blessing.

### The F2 overlay lists each style target's memory (#561)
**What changed.** Under its `Style targets: N (X MB, K full-size)` total, the player's F2 overlay now has a row for each texture the style chain holds: its name, size and MB, as `flint render --gpu-time` lists them. A user style's own targets show as `<style>.<target>` (and `.a`/`.b` for a history pair) beside the built-ins', so a `[[passes]]` style's cost can be read while it runs. Past 16 textures, the rest share one `+N more` row. Nothing is read while the overlay is closed or showing the graph alone.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### Pencil and watercolor: a paper image (and stroke strip) per entity (#547)
**What changed.** `paper_image` and `stroke_image` on a `pencil_style` entity, and `paper_image` on a `watercolor_style` entity, now draw with that entity's own file even when another entity, or the scene's `[post_process.styles.*]` table, names a different one. Before, a frame drew with one paper and one strip (the table's, else the first entity's), and every other entity naming a file fell back to the procedural paper with a warning. Up to four papers and four strips draw in one frame; a fifth falls back to procedural with one warning naming the entity (`style_drops` in `flint render --report json`). Entities naming the same file share it. A scene that names one image, or none, draws exactly as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene that worked round the old limit (one shared paper for every prop) can now give each prop its own.

### A user style's runaway loop no longer loses the device in the browser (#550)
**What changed.** `flint bundle` (and `flint export web`) now packs a loop-guarded copy of each user style pass that has a loop to guard: `styles/<style>.guarded.wgsl`, or `styles/<style>.<pass>.guarded.wgsl` for a style with `[[passes]]`, beside its `.shader.toml`. The web player builds that copy, as the native build has since #411, so a loop that never ends stops after 4096 turns and draws a wrong pixel instead of losing the GPU. A style without loops, or with only constant-bounded `for` loops (#424), packs nothing extra. The copy records a hash of the text it was made from, and the browser passes over a copy made from another version of the WGSL or the engine.
**What to do.** Bundle again to get the guard on the web. If your game already has a file at one of those names, rename it: `flint bundle` refuses to overwrite it.

### Per-entity watercolor draws only near its entities; a user style can say `reach = <points>` (#538)
**What changed.** Per-entity watercolor (a `watercolor_style` or `style_mask`, or a table with `scope = "entities"`) now runs its wash, blur and paint passes only inside its entities' screen bounds, grown by how far each reads; the frame is byte-identical. A `.shader.toml` that reads the mask may now declare `reach = <points>` (0 to 512): the promise that further than that from its entities `style` returns `p.color` unchanged. The framework then draws the style's last pass only inside that, and the style's own passthrough around it. A pixel the shader changes past its reach is cut back to the input there. The web page takes `?scissor=0` to draw the full passes, for measuring. An engine before this one refuses a `.shader.toml` with `reach` (an unknown key).
**What to do.** Nothing for watercolor. For a per-entity user style that passes through off its entities, add `reach` (`0` when it draws only on their own pixels, as `examples/styles/hologram` does; more when it draws a rim or reads neighbours' mask ids) to make it cheaper.

### Pencil `grid = "local"`: strokes that ride on moving props (#544)
**What changed.** `pencil_style` (and the pencil table, `--style pencil:grid=local`) takes a fourth `grid`, `local`. It lays the same strokes as `grid = "world"` on the surfaces, but in the styled root's own space, so they ride on a prop that rolls, slides or turns instead of the prop moving under them; children that move with the root ride too, and a prop held on twos (`motion_hold`) keeps its strokes between drawings. A skinned mesh still swims under them. On the whole frame's table, which has no root, `local` draws as `world`. Nothing else moves: every existing pencil picture is byte-identical. Pencil's variant row grew from 160 to 208 bytes, which only a game reading `flint_render::stylize::pencil::PencilRow` sees. Demo: `examples/pencil_rolling.scene.toml`.
**What to do.** Nothing. For a prop that moves through the world, set `grid = "local"` on its `pencil_style`.

## 2026-11-17

### Whole-frame ASCII's `silhouette = "cell"` snaps another style's entity to whole glyphs (#534)
**What changed.** With ASCII on for the whole frame and `silhouette = "cell"` in its table (`--style ascii:silhouette=cell`), the edge of an entity in another style (a `style_mask { style = "pencil" }` prop) no longer cuts glyph cells in half. Each cell along it is a whole glyph, when at least `cell_share` of it is not the entity's, or wholly the entity's, its other pixels showing the plain scene. This holds in either chain order. Before, the option snapped only ASCII's own per-entity props, and every other style's edge was cut pixel by pixel. With `silhouette = "pixel"` (the default) every frame is byte-identical to before. A Rust style can ask for the same cut by returning its grid from the new `StylePass::ownership_cells`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene whose ASCII table already says `silhouette = "cell"` now draws its other styles' props with stepped, whole-cell edges. Lower `cell_share` to give more edge cells to the glyphs.

### Living Props: `jitter_rate` sets how fast the boil ticks, live (#529)
**What changed.** A new knob, `jitter_rate`, runs a living prop's leaf-card and shade boil faster or slower than its spec's `jitter.step`: 2 ticks twice as often, 0.5 half, 0 holds it, up to 8. It layers like `softness`: `[living] jitter_rate`, `[living.species.<spec>] jitter_rate`, a `.wooze.toml` preset, the F3 panel's Clock section, `tune_living(#{ living: #{ jitter_rate: 2.0 } })` (eased with `tune_living(map, seconds)`), `clear_living(species, ["jitter_rate"])` and `--report json`'s values. A change never pops: each prop's jitter clock moves on at the new rate from where it stands, so every card holds until its next tick, and echoes follow. The pose clock (`step`) is untouched. Unset is 1, and a scene that never sets it renders byte for byte as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. To quicken a form's flicker with activity, tune `jitter_rate` rather than `jitter` (which is how far it moves, not how fast).

### `flint render --sweep living.echoes.spread=...`, `.alpha` and `.count` (#531)
**What changed.** `--sweep` takes the scene's `[living] echoes` fields as keys: `--sweep living.echoes.spread=0,0.9,1.8 --time 1.5` draws one labelled sheet with a column a spread, each column what a scene with that `[living] echoes` table draws, pixel for pixel (the scene's other `[living]` values and any species' own `echoes` apply as in the file). Values are checked as a spec's echoes are (`count` up to 16, `spread` 0 to 1.82, `alpha` 0 to 1) before anything loads, and a scene with no living props is refused. Two keys make a grid as with any sweep (`--sweep living.echoes.spread=... --sweep living.echoes.alpha=...`). Nothing a scene, spec or script says changes.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that writes one scene copy per echoes value and tiles the renders can use the one command instead.

### The style registry is a `StyleRegistry` a renderer or host owns (#742)
**What changed.** Styles are no longer registered for the process (ADR 0129). `stylize::register(&STYLE)` is `styles.register(&STYLE)` on a `StyleRegistry`; `stylize::find`, `registry()` and `names()` are its methods (`builtin(name)` / `builtins()` for the engine's own); `custom::load_dir`, `load_for`, `load_file`, `reload_changed_for`, `errors` and `source` are too, and `custom::drop_unseen` is gone (a scene's registry holds only the user styles it sees: `StyleRegistry::for_scene`). Readers take the registry: `post_process_config_from_def(def, &styles)`, `StyleSettings::from_tables`, `StyleSpec::parse`, `apply_style_spec(s)`, `post_style::*`, `entity_style::*`, `LookFade::new`, `paint_style_errors`. `SceneBinding` makes the scene's (`SceneBinding::over(scope, &base)` keeps a host's game styles); `bind_scene` hands it to the renderer (`styles()`, `styles_mut()`, `set_styles`). A user style edited while running is freed once rebuilt. A look naming a style the engine lacks is judged by the scene it is laid on.
**What to do.** A game with `PlayerExtension::styles`: nothing. One that called `stylize::register` itself: register into the registry it reads scenes with and hand that to its renderer (`app.core.styles` in an embedded player).

### `flint frame open` opens a scope trace: a profiler tab with the frame strip, a flame chart and scope statistics (#1099)
**What changed.** `flint frame open` takes a trace file (what `--trace`,
`--trace-on-hitch` or Shift+F3 wrote) as well as a capture. A trace opens on a
**Profiler** tab: every frame as a strip in the F2 graph's colours with
hitches marked, the chosen frames as a flame chart per thread with the script
callbacks and GPU passes beneath, and each scope's count, total, mean, p90 and
worst over them. A capture taken while recording, which holds `trace.json`,
opens with both tabs, and P switches between them. New flags under
**Profiler (a trace)**: `--profile` (open on that tab), `--trace-frame
N|A..B|hitch|worst`, `--scope NAME` (mark its calls and plot its time across
frames) and `--compare` (tint each bar by how much longer its path took than
in the median frame). `--screenshot` draws it headless. Captures and traces
are read as before; nothing is written.
**What to do.** Nothing. Open a hitch's trace with `flint frame open
traces/<scene>-hitch-t<time>.json`.

### Script profiling names the function inside a callback: `--perf`, traces, `take_function_times` (#1100)
**What changed.** While scripts are timed (`--perf`, F2, `--hitch-log`, `--trace-on-hitch`) or a trace is recording, each function a callback calls is timed as well. That covers engine functions by name (`raycast()`, all overloads together) and the script's own functions (`hud.rhai:draw_bar()`), each with its call count and total and worst time. In a trace they are scopes nested inside the callback's scope. `--perf`'s worst-scripts entries now end with the callback's slowest function: `stall.rhai:on_update 0.33/100.1 (hold() 100.0)`, or `(raycast() x400 3.1)`. A host reads the same numbers from `ScriptSystem::take_function_times()`. Closures, functions that read `this` and the functions of imported modules aren't timed on their own: their time is counted in their caller's. With timing off, scripts run as before. With it on, a call to the script's own function costs about 0.5 µs more in release, and a timed call gets twice Rhai's call-level limit, so a deep recursion fails at the same depth either way.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tool that parses the perf line's `worst scripts:` part should allow for the `(...)` that may now follow each entry.

### `flint profile` reads a scope trace from the command line (#1098)
**What changed.** A new command reads the trace `flint-player --trace`, `--trace-on-hitch` and Shift+F3 write, with no GPU, scene or schemas. `flint profile explain <trace> [frame]` says why a frame was slow in its first line, holding it against the trace's normal frames: `frame 525 took 107.28 ms (median 4.57): stall.rhai:on_update 100.09 ms against its median 0.032, ...`, or a scope that `ran only here`, or the phase when no scope covers the time. `flint profile frame <trace> <n|worst|hitch>` prints that frame's scope tree, `flint profile top <trace>` the scopes by total, self time, worst call or calls (`--thread`, `--frames a..b`), and `flint profile diff <a> <b>` two traces' per-scope mean and p90 deltas, marking the ones the frames' own spread can explain. Every view answers `--json`.
**What to do.** Nothing. After a hitch, `flint profile explain` on the file `--trace-on-hitch` wrote is the quickest way to its cause.

### `--trace`, `--trace-seconds`, `--trace-on-hitch` and Shift+F3 save the engine's scopes as a Perfetto trace (#1097)
**What changed.** The scope recorder records now. `flint play` and
`flint-player` take `--trace FILE` (the last 5 s of every `flint_core::scope!`,
written when the run ends), `--trace-seconds N` (how far back; 0 keeps the
whole session) and `--trace-on-hitch[=N]` (a trace eight frames after each of
the first N hitches past the start, beside `--trace`'s file or in `traces/` at
the project's root). The new `save_trace` debug key, Shift+F3, saves one
mid-play. The file is Chrome trace-event JSON that Perfetto opens: a track
per thread, plus frames, phases, GPU passes, hitches and frame notes. Each
script callback is a scope of its own while recording
(`scripts/hud.rhai:on_draw_ui`), and a frame capture taken while recording
holds `trace.json`. Every debug build has the recorder; a release build
needs the `trace` feature, and refuses the flags without it. On the web,
`?perf=1` shows a Save trace button in a build with the recorder.
**What to do.** Nothing. Add `traces/` to a game's `.gitignore`: a trace
names its scenes, scripts and assets.

### Pencil, contour and ASCII read one shared edge pass; a floor seen edge-on no longer inks a band (#743)
**What changed.** The `[post_process] edge_depth` / `edge_normal` pass (#301) is now where pencil's contours, contour's silhouettes and ASCII's line glyphs get their depth steps and creases. It keeps a step only where the depth really bends, so a floor seen edge-on no longer draws a dark band along the horizon in any pencil look (`graphite`, `blueprint`, `charcoal`...): the horizon is one line, and figures standing at it read again. Pencil's `depth` and `crease`, contour's `width` and `threshold`, and ASCII's `edge_depth` / `edge_normal` keep their meaning, measured against the shared edges; ASCII's direction follows the scene's thresholds. Pencil's `grazing` still adds its own stricter test. A user style gets `flint_unproject(inv_view_proj, uv, depth)`, `flint_unproject_h` and `flint_safe_w` in its prelude, the text the built-ins use; its other prelude helpers are unchanged. A Rust style can say `.needs_edges_when(f)` or `StyleNeed::EdgeSteps`, and `StylePass::edges_rect`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A look that leaned on the old horizon band reads lighter near the horizon now; there is no switch that brings the band back.

### Screenshots and opening F2 are `tooling` hitches, not the game's (#1129)
