An edit that can't be written anywhere is named in red in the status bar and keeps the title's `*`; "Saved!" means everything was written. Each save appends one JSON line per field (`entity`, `component`, `field`, `old`, `new`, `table`, `saved_at`, `scene`) to `.flint/viewer-edits.jsonl` under the project root, so an agent can read what a person changed.
**What to do.** Add `.flint/viewer-edits.jsonl` to your game's `.gitignore` (`flint init` does it for new projects). An agent working with a person in the viewer can read the file's tail after they save.

### A parent cycle is refused: `parent = "<itself>"`, `a` under `b` under `a`, and `set_parent` (#680)
**What changed.** An entity parented to itself, or a ring of entities parented to each other, used to load and then kill the player with a stack overflow and no message. Now `FlintWorld::set_parent` refuses the link that would close the loop with `FlintError::ParentCycle { child, parent, chain }` and stores nothing, so such a scene fails to load with a message naming the entities and the chain. `flint check` and `flint validate` report it before you play, as the new error code `parent-cycle`. A script's `set_parent` that would close a loop is a script error at its line. World matrices are walked with a loop, not recursion, so no depth of hierarchy overflows the stack, and every matrix is the same bits as before.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a scene of yours crashed on load: run `flint check` on it and fix the `parent` it names. A Rust host that matches every `FlintError` variant adds `ParentCycle`.

## 2026-11-15

### Looks cross-fade: `set_look(name, seconds)`, `clear_look(seconds)`, `look_faded` (#496)
**What changed.** `set_look` and `clear_look` take an optional length in seconds of game time and ease from the look drawn now to the new one: numbers and colours lerp, an effect one look turns on ramps its strength from 0, a style only one look has fades by its whole-frame `blend`, and options (a ramp, a palette) switch halfway. Only what the two looks differ in moves, and scripts' per-frame post overrides still win. On arrival every script's `on_event` hears `look_faded` with `#{ look }`. F4's Look picker has a Fade time; `flint render --scripts --time T` captures a fade at T. The one-argument forms are the cut they always were. For Rust hosts: `ScriptCommand::SetLook` has a `seconds` field, `ScriptHost::set_look` takes `(name, seconds)`, `HeadlessGame::take_look_request` returns a `LookRequest`, and `PlayerApp::fade_to_look(look, seconds)` is new.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game binary that implements `ScriptHost::set_look` or matches on `SetLook` adds the `seconds` field. See `examples/look_fade.scene.toml`.

### `flint render --reference-text`: ASCII frames scored by their letters (#495)
**What changed.** `flint render --reference ref.png --reference-text ref.txt` takes the reference's glyph grid (the file `--ascii-text` writes). When it's given, a frame the ASCII style drew is scored by mean colour, glyph coverage and glyph layout instead of #183's pixel histogram and layout. This applies to sweeps, `--report` and `--ai-refine`. Without it the score is as before. Every distance in `--report` and in the `--ai-refine` log now has `metric` (`pixels` or `glyphs`) and `terms` (each term's distance and points, which sum to `score`), and keeps `mean_colour`, `histogram` and `layout`. The refine log also has a top-level `metric`, and the terminal prints each score's terms. `--report` together with `--ascii-text` on a still no longer fails with "the ASCII style didn't draw this frame".
**What to do.** Nothing. To refine an ASCII look toward another ASCII render, write the reference with `--ascii-text ref.txt` and pass `--reference-text ref.txt`. A script that reads the report's `distance` keeps working.

### Render modes 1-6 are styles and a tear: `matrix`, `blood`, `drunk`, `tron`, `underwater`, `[post_process] tear`, the ocean's `tint` (#746)
**What changed.** The composite and ocean shaders no longer carry render modes. Each look is a display style with named params (`flint render --list-styles`). The tear is three `[post_process]` keys: `tear`, `tear_scale` (default 3) and `tear_iris`. Set them with `--tear` or Rhai `set_tear` / `set_tear_shape`, which hold until set again. A style shows only in the tear's holes when its table says `blend_source = "tear"`, the key props already use. The sea's blood and underside are the `ocean` component's `tint*` fields. `PostProcessConfig` lost `render_mode`, `mode_mix` and `mode_params`, and `flint_player::apply_look` takes a `RenderModeShim`. `--render-mode`, `set_render_mode` and `set_render_mode_params` are deprecated shims for one release: they still draw each mode, transient as before.
**What to do.** Move to the styles: mode 1 is `set_post_style("matrix", true)`, `set_post_style_param("matrix", "blend_source", "tear")` and `set_tear(mix)`. Drunk and underwater take `blend` (underwater's x..w are `depth`, `churn`, `daylight`, `glow`). For blood, also set the ocean's `tint`. Set the tear back to 0 when it ends.

### `global::NAME` reads a script's top-level `const` from callbacks again (#1012)
**What changed.** Since #692 (a script's top level runs once), a callback, or a function it called, that read a top-level constant as `global::NAME` failed with `Variable not found: global::NAME`, every call. Rhai records a constant for `global::` only as its `const` statement runs, and callbacks no longer ran the top level. Now each constant a script reads as `global::` is handed to every callback with the value its one top-level run made: the initializer (`const DOOR = get_entity("door");`) still runs once, and a hot reload's new constants are included. A bare `NAME` worked throughout and is unchanged. A script with no `global::` read costs nothing more per callback.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game whose engine is at #692 or later and whose scripts read `global::` (Float: time of day, birds, music, water, weather) pulls this to stop the errors.

### A callback that ends in a value no longer fails: "Output type incorrect: i64 (expecting ())" (#1031)
**What changed.** A Rhai function's last expression is its value. Since `play_sound` and `play_sound_at` started returning a handle (#699), an `on_update` that ended in `if t <= 0.0 { play_sound_at(..) }` returned that handle, and the engine, which called every callback expecting `()`, counted the call as a failure and warned with `Output type incorrect: i64 (expecting ())` each time the sound played. The same was true of any callback ending in a getter, a `random()` or a bare variable. The rest of the callback had already run, so the sound played and nothing was lost but the warning. Now every callback's return value is dropped and the call counts as a success.
**What to do.** Nothing. A trailing `;` never silenced it (Rhai's block keeps its last call's value); a `()` added as a callback's last line to silence it can come out.

### Per-entity ASCII outlines no longer drop a line glyph on a cell border (#493)
**What changed.** A line glyph along a per-entity ASCII entity's silhouette (`ascii_style`, or a `style_mask` naming `ascii`) is now drawn in a cell the entity shows. Before, when the silhouette ran along a cell border, the line could go to the cell just outside the entity, whose pixels pass the picture through, and no glyph was drawn there: a `.` or `:` where the barrel's top edge should be `─`, a one-cell gap in an outline. Of two cells that could take a line, the edge thin pass now gives it to the one the style draws, and a line lying wholly in an outside cell moves at most one cell inward. Whole-frame ASCII is unchanged byte for byte. Per-entity props gain a few line glyphs along their outlines; most of them show only with `outline = 0`, since the `outline` ink floor (#288) drew the silhouette in pixels already.
**What to do.** Nothing. Re-bless a golden of a per-entity ASCII scene if its outline cells moved.

### A script's `log()` prints once under `flint render --scripts` (#1013)
**What changed.** `log`, `log_warn` and `log_error` wrote every line twice in a headless capture (`flint render --scripts`), though once in the player. A capture looked up each command's name by carrying out a copy of it on a probe host, and the probe's `log` printed. The name now comes from a table, `flint_script::host::command_name`, which carries nothing out; it names each command as its `ScriptHost` default does, and `None` for `log`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A tool that counted a capture's script lines, or dropped every second one, now sees each once.

### `set_style_param` and a style component's colours say when a value is past its range (#492)
**What changed.** Scripts' `set_style_param`, `set_post_style_param`, `tween_style_param` and `tween_post_style_param` still clamp a number, `blend` or a colour channel to its range, and now log a warning naming it (`set_style_param: ascii on 'barrel': cell=400 is outside its range 4..32; clamped to 32`); a tween says it once, when it starts. A schema field of type `vec2`, `vec3`, `vec4` or `color` with `min`/`max` now checks each member, and `ascii_style.tint`, `pencil_style.paper`/`lead` and `watercolor_style.paper` take 0 to 1, so `tint = [3, 0, 0]` is an `out-of-range` error in `flint check` and `flint validate` and a warning at load. A user style's `<name>_style` field past its `[params]` range is the same error in `flint check`, and a warning when the scene is bound. The JSON Schema export puts a vector's range on each member.
**What to do.** Run `flint check` on your scenes: a colour channel above 1 or below 0 in one of those components is now reported; it draws as it did before. A game schema with `min`/`max` on a vector field now has them enforced.

### `[post_process]` keys are listed, range-checked and declared once; `flint render --list-effects` (#741)
**What changed.** Every built-in effect key (`exposure`, `bloom_*`, `ssao_*`, `fog_*`, `grade_*`, ...) is declared once in `flint_schema::post_effects`, with a default, the range its effect is tuned for and help, and the scene parse, `PostProcessConfig`, the schema, `--sweep post.<key>` and the F4 sections are made from it. `flint render --list-effects [--json]` lists them. A scene value past its range draws as before and warns (`post-range`, in the load, `flint validate` and `flint check`); in a look or a `--sweep post.<key>` it is refused, as a style value is. `bloom_soft_threshold` and `ssao_bias` are new scene keys (same defaults). `engine/schemas/scene/post_process.toml` no longer lists the effect keys: the registry adds them. `PostProcessConfig::fxaa_enabled` is now `fxaa`, the scene key's name.
**What to do.** Rust that sets `config.fxaa_enabled` sets `config.fxaa`. A game layer's own `post_process.toml` may drop its copies of the effect keys. Run `flint check` and settle any `post-range` warning.

### `spline_mesh` sweeps any `profile`, a bad spline file is named by line and column, and `sweep_v1` makes swept models (#750)
**What changed.** A `spline_mesh` takes `profile = [[right, up], ...]` in place of `width` and `height` (a tube, an I-beam, a gutter), with `profile_closed = false` for an open surface and `smooth = true` for round shading; `width`/`height` are needed only without one. A `.spline.toml` with a mistake is skipped with a warning naming file, line and column (it said "Failed to parse"), and `flint check` reports it as `bad-spline`. A `position` of other than three numbers, a `spacing` of 0 and a wrong type are now errors where they were skipped or read as defaults. A striped chunked mesh's stripes run on across chunks instead of restarting. The new procgen generator `sweep_v1` makes one model from a profile and an inline path. `flint_player::spline_gen` is gone: `flint_render::load_scene_splines`.
**What to do.** Nothing for a track that loaded: FlintKart's render byte-identically. Run `flint check` on a scene whose spline was silently missing.

### An archetype sets its placeholder box's colour: `[archetype.<name>.visual]` (#751)
**What changed.** An entity with a `bounds` box and nothing else to draw takes its colour from its archetype's new `visual` table (`color = [r, g, b]` or `[r, g, b, a]`, `wireframe = true|false`). The renderer no longer knows `room`, `door`, `furniture`, `character`, `wall`, `floor`, `ceiling` or `pillar` by name: their colours moved into their own schema files, unchanged, so a scene that uses them renders as before. An archetype with no `visual` draws grey, as a game's did already. A malformed `bounds` (a `min` or `max` that isn't three numbers) now draws a 1 m box instead of the archetype's old built-in size. `flint_render::ArchetypeVisual` and `SceneRenderer::set_archetype_visual` are gone; `flint_schema::ArchetypeVisual` and `SceneBinding::with_archetypes(&registry)` replace them.
**What to do.** Give your own archetypes a `[archetype.<name>.visual]` if their boxes should not be grey. A game binary that embeds `PlayerApp` needs nothing. Rust that binds a scene itself adds `.with_archetypes(&registry)` to its `SceneBinding`.

### A component's schema gives it an F3 panel: `panel = "status" | "tune" | "trigger"`; the Day / Time, Weather, Reality, Visitor and Dead Calm panels are gone (#744)
**What changed.** A field's schema can say what it is on its component's F3 panel: `panel = "status"` (read only, on the status line), `"tune"` (an editor that writes the live component, with Reset and Commit to File) or `"trigger"` (a button that writes once for the script to clear), or a table with `role` and `label`, `labels`, `format`, `scale`, `unset`, `from`, `min`/`max`, `step`, `slider`, `presets`, `commit`, `press`; `[component.<name>.panel] title` names it. Every entity carrying such a component gets a panel. The engine's five panels for `time_of_day`, `weather`, `reality`, `raft_visitor` and `dead_calm` are removed, with `TimeOfDayDebugPanel`, `WeatherDebugPanel`, `RealityDebugPanel`, `VisitorDebugPanel` and `DeadCalmDebugPanel`. `DebugPanel` has `sync(&mut PanelCtx)` and `weight()` (defaulted), and `assign_columns` takes weights. A mistake in a panel line is a `bad-panel` warning.
**What to do.** A game that used those panels writes `panel` lines in its schemas (the book's Debug Panels page, "A Panel From a Schema"); field-level lines load on an older engine too. A game binary calling `assign_columns` passes `panel.weight()` for each panel.

### `flint_core::step_clock` and `flint_core::field`: the wooze clock and field, by their general names (#747)
**What changed.** The hold-and-glide clock moved from `flint_wooze` to `flint_core::step_clock` (`warp`, `eased`, `tick`, `Cycle`, `StepClock`, `STEP_MIN`, `TICK_EARLY`), and motion on twos (`flint_ecs::motion_hold::tick`) and pencil's `boil` now step through the same `warp` the living props glide on. The zones, falloff and ramps of the wooze field are `flint_core::field::ParamField<N>` over a `FieldKeys<N>`; `WoozeField` is that field with four keys (`WoozeField::params()`), and `flint_wooze::WOOZE_KEYS` names them. The old paths are re-exported, scenes, `[wooze]` tables and scripts are unchanged, and the renders are byte-identical. ADR 0123.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game's Rust calls `Cycle::read`: it takes `(beats, glide, amount)` now, so pass `sample.glide, sample.amplitude` where you passed `&sample`. A game that wants its own field by place (fog, wind) can make a `static FieldKeys<N>` and a `ParamField::new`.

### `LivingProps::tune_*` is the one way to turn a Living Props knob; the F3 panel's knobs say what they do (#753)
**What changed.** The runtime behind living props moved from one 5,000-line `living_props.rs` into `flint_procgen::living_props::{layers, ring, prop, schedule, blend, ab}`, and the F3 panel's into `flint-debug-ui`'s `living_props_panel/`, with every knob described once in a table. The whole-config setters `LivingProps::set_echoes`, `set_echoes_of`, `set_stagger`, `set_stagger_of`, `set_variance` and `set_variance_of` are gone: `tune_echoes`, `tune_stagger`, `tune_variance` and `tune_softness` take a `LivingScope` and do the same. New: the `Layer` trait and `Layered<L>`, `ClockOverride::full`, `flint_procgen::living::Segment` (another name for `BranchSegment`), and `flint_debug_ui::{KnobDesc, KnobSection, KnobControl, KnobValue}`. `Knob::ALL` now lists the knobs in the panel's order (`step` first). Each knob's hover in the panel says what it does. Frames, presets and `--report json` are byte-identical.
**What to do.** A game binary calling `set_echoes(c)` calls `tune_echoes(LivingScope::All, EchoOverride::full(c))` (variance likewise with `VarianceOverride::full`, stagger with `tune_stagger(LivingScope::All, s)`). Scenes and scripts: nothing.

### `ocean_contact` foams every hull where it is drawn; `ocean.show_probe` is gone (#749)
**What changed.** Contact foam reads each `ocean_contact` entity's world transform, so a hull under a parent or posed with `rotation_quat` (physics write-back) foams where it is drawn, and its half-extents grow with world scale. Up to four foam at once, the nearest the camera past that, and each one left out is warned about once by name (it used to be the first found, at its local position and Euler yaw). A dynamic body's velocity comes from physics. A lone upright hull renders byte-identically: Float's scenes are unchanged. The `ocean` component's `show_probe` field is removed. The Ocean Debug panel's **Diagnostics → Wave probe** box now draws the parity cross itself, so no scene entity or script is needed.
**What to do.** If your scene sets `show_probe`, delete it (an unknown field now draws a warning), along with any buoy entity and script that read it. Use the panel's Wave probe instead. A second hull just needs its own `ocean_contact`.

### A script's `print` goes to the log, and `--perf` times every callback (#703)
**What changed.** Rhai's `print(x)` and `debug(x)` no longer write to stdout: they log at info under the target `script`, with the script's `file` and `entity` (`debug` adds the `line`), so `flint render --scripts --report json` keeps stdout for the report. The script system's reload and load lines and the player's transition and quit lines moved from stdout to the same log. `flint` and `flint-player` now show `script=info` by default, which also makes `log()` visible without `RUST_LOG`. The perf readout (`--perf`, `?perf=1`) times every callback, named `file.rhai:callback` (`hud.rhai:on_draw_ui`), not only `on_update`; `[load, on_init]` is now `[load]`, and `[events]` / `[expression]` are gone (their callbacks are listed by name). `ScriptSystem::set_update_timing` / `update_times` are now `set_timing` / `take_times`, which empties them.
**What to do.** A game binary with its own `tracing_subscriber` adds `script=info` to its filter to see what scripts print. A host that read `update_times()` calls `take_times()` after each batch of script calls.

### `SceneRenderer`'s per-frame fields and setters are private: `bind_scene` and `prepare` replace them (#739)
**What changed.** A host that embeds `flint_render::SceneRenderer` drives it through `bind_scene` (once a scene) and `prepare(device, queue, &world, &FrameInputs)` (once a frame) only. What each removed name became:
- `ocean_time` → `FrameInputs::time`, or `look_time` to hold it (`--style-time`).
- `grass_time`, `grass_wooze` → `grass_time`, `grass_wooze`; `style_dt` → `dt`.
- `pixels_per_point` → `pixels_per_point` (read back with `pixels_per_point()`).
- `camera_offset`, `ortho_height`, `aspect_ratio` → `view_2d: View2d` (`View2d::of(&camera)`).
- `set_boil` → `boil`; `set_shallow_wooze` → `shallow_wooze`; `set_world_lines` → `world_lines`.
- `update_particles_from` → `particles`; `update_grass_entities` → `grass_benders`.
- `update_from_world` → `prepare`.
- `scene_dir`, `set_asset_scope`, `set_terrain_entity` → `bind_scene` (`scene_dir()` reads it).

`update_particles` (a host's own draws, after `prepare`) and `set_motion_time` (before bone writes) stay. Pipeline and GPU-uniform types are crate-private; `flint_render::MAX_BONES` and `WORLD_LINE_DEPTH_SLACK` replace the `skinned_pipeline::` and `world_line_pipeline::` paths.
**What to do.** Nothing for a game on the engine's binaries. Rust that drove the renderer by hand: build one `FrameInputs` a frame, as `crates/flint-render/CLAUDE.md` ("Host protocol") shows.

### Script errors are counted once each, shown on screen, and `--strict-scripts` fails a run on them (#693)
**What changed.** A script that fails in a callback used to print the same warning every frame, with the scene's `source` and no entity. Now each failure (same file, callback, line and message) is logged once, `[script] scripts/hud.rhai:4:5: hud_controller, on_update: ...`, then counted, with a line at its 10th, 100th, 1000th time. A script that isn't found or doesn't compile counts too, and is loaded again when its file is saved. The player draws a card in the bottom-left corner naming each failing script (debug HUD builds). `flint-player --exit-after` and `flint render --scripts` end with `[script] N errors` and a line each, `flint render --report json` has a `script_errors` list, and `--strict-scripts` on `flint-player`, `flint play` and `flint render` exits 1 on any. A game binary reads them from `app.core.script.errors()`. Log lines that matched `on_update error (file.rhai)` now read as above.
**What to do.** Nothing. Add `--strict-scripts` to a headless check of your game so a script error fails it.

### `stop_sound` works; `play_sound` returns a handle, loops and fades (#699)
**What changed.** `stop_sound` used to do nothing. Now `play_sound` and
`play_sound_at` return a handle. `stop_sound(handle)` stops that sound, and
`stop_sound(name)` stops every sound a script started under that name. Both take
an optional fade in seconds. `set_sound_volume(handle, volume[, fade])` changes a
sound's volume, with an optional fade. The opts map takes `looping: true` and
`bus: "music"` (`loop` is a Rhai keyword, so the key is `looping`), and
`play_sound(name, opts)` is new. A scene change stops every loop a script
started. In Rust, `ScriptCommand::PlaySound` and `PlaySoundAt` now carry a
`handle` and a `play: SoundPlay`. `StopSound` carries a `SoundRef` and a fade,
and `SetSoundVolume` is new. So do the `ScriptHost` sound methods.
**What to do.** A script that called `stop_sound` and heard nothing will now
stop the sound. Check that it stops what you meant. A game binary that matches
these variants in `PlayerExtension::script_command`, or implements their
`ScriptHost` methods, needs the new fields.

### A misspelt callback is a load warning; a callback at the wrong count is no longer called (#700)
**What changed.** Loading a script now warns about an `fn` one edit from a callback that nothing in the script calls: `fn on_updte()` logs "`fn on_updte` is not a callback, and nothing in this script calls it (did you mean `on_update`?)". Before, it loaded and never ran, in silence. A callback defined with a parameter count the engine doesn't call it with (`fn on_update(dt)`, `fn on_sequence_cue(seq)`) is warned at load as before (and `on_sequence_cue` is now checked too) but is no longer called, where it used to fail with a script error every frame. The callbacks are one table, `flint_core::callbacks::CALLBACKS` (`Callback`, `CallbackSpec`); for extension code, `ScriptInstance`'s `has_on_*` fields are gone (use `defines(Callback::Update)`), and `flint_script::api::registry::CallbackDoc` is now `CallbackSpec`.
**What to do.** Look for the new warnings when a game loads, or run `flint script check`: each names the callback meant. Rename a helper that starts with `on_` and is near a callback's name if it is called only from another script.

### The egui 0.36 port covers a game's tests: `egui_wants_keyboard_input`, `ModifiersChanged`, `FontsView::definitions`, frames that grow by their stroke (#996)
**What changed.** [Upgrading the Engine](https://docs.chaps.dev/guides/upgrading-the-engine.html#egui-030-to-036) was checked against a game's test targets as well as its binary. The table gains three rows that rustc names no replacement for: `wants_keyboard_input`, `wants_pointer_input`, `is_using_pointer` and `is_pointer_over_area` to their 0.36 names, `RawInput::modifiers` to an `Event::ModifiersChanged` (which egui keeps until the next one), and `Fonts::lock` to `FontsView::definitions`. A new behaviour change: since egui 0.31 a `Frame` lays out `2 × stroke.width` more than it did, so a framed panel fitted to a width comes out 2 px wider. A new Tests section says why a plain `run_ui` panics in a debug test (`Dropped TexturesDelta`).
**What to do.** A game porting its egui code: read the new rows. Run its tests' egui frames through `flint_debug_ui::run_headless_frame`, with flint-debug-ui as a dev-dependency. For 0.30's frame size, give a frame with a 1 px stroke `.outer_margin(Margin::same(-1))`.

### A game binary's `Cargo.lock` from before wgpu 30 needs one `cargo update`; `flint check` warns `stale-cargo-lock` (#995)
**What changed.** Nothing in the engine. A game binary whose lock was written under wgpu 23 (any lock from before #945) fails on Windows inside wgpu-hal, with ten errors such as ``expected `ID3D12Device`, found a different `ID3D12Device` ``. When cargo moves that lock forward it gives gpu-allocator 0.28 a `windows` the lock already holds (0.58 or cpal's 0.54) while wgpu-hal 30 is on 0.62. `cargo update -p gpu-allocator` doesn't fix it. `flint check <game>` now warns `stale-cargo-lock` once cargo has moved the lock, with the command for that lock's versions.
**What to do.** In the game's workspace root, run `cargo update -p windows@0.54.0 -p windows@0.58.0` (every `windows` the lock holds except 0.62.2). It keeps the game's other locked versions. [Upgrading the Engine](https://docs.chaps.dev/guides/upgrading-the-engine.html#cargo) has the details.

### `flint edit` draws a scene's terrain, `[environment]`, shallow wooze and swaying grass (#739)
**What changed.** The scene viewer now binds a scene and prepares each frame the way the player and `flint render` do. On open and on each `--watch` reload it loads the scene's heightmap terrain (it drew none before, so a styled hillside was missing), masks a styled terrain, and sets the scene's `[environment]` levers (`shadows`, `default_lights`, ambient, wrap, Oren-Nayar, sheen), which it ignored. Shallow wooze meshes drift and terrain or woozy grass sways, on the viewer's own clock. An F4 lighting edit lasts until the next reload, which puts the scene's `[environment]` back. For hosts that embed the renderer: `SceneBinding::with_environment` hands `bind_scene` the scene's `[environment]`; `flint_scene::EnvironmentDef` is exported; the shallow wooze and woozy grass clocks are `flint_render::shallow_wooze::{ShallowWooze, GrassWooze}`.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene that looked right only in the viewer because it ignored `[environment]` now looks as it does in the player.

## 2026-11-14

### A script's top level runs once, with the world there: `let door = get_entity("door");` works (#692)
**What changed.** A script's top-level statements used to run at load with no world (a top-level `get_entity` gave `-1`, `self_entity()` gave `0`), and then again before every callback, so a top-level `log`, `play_sound` or `seed_random` ran once per callback per frame. Now the top level runs once, just before the scene's first `on_init` (or the first callback after a chunk loads the script), with the world lent and `self_entity()` the script's own entity. Callbacks no longer re-run it; only the top-level `import`s are evaluated again, from a cache. A hot reload runs the new source's top level once, with the world. For Rust hosts: `ScriptEngine::reload_script(world, id, ast)` and `ScriptSync::check_hot_reload(engine, world)` take the world.
**What to do.** Nothing for most games. A script that leaned on the re-run (a top-level counter or `log` meant to fire every frame) moves that into `on_update`. A host calling `reload_script` or `check_hot_reload` passes its world.

### `.font.toml`, `.atlas.toml` and `fonts/fonts.toml` warn about keys they ignore (#282)
**What changed.** These three formats used to skip any key they didn't know, so a misspelled `spacng` or a key written after the wrong `[table]` header changed nothing and said nothing. Each key they ignore is now a warning when the file loads. The warning names the file, the key and its table, and suggests the likeliest fix: "did you mean `spacing`?", "it is a key of [egui]", or, for a top-level key written below a header, "move it above the header". A single `[font]` in `fonts.toml`, where the format wants `[[font]]` entries, is reported, and so is an atlas region that isn't `[x, y, w, h]` in whole pixels (still skipped). Files load as before.
**What to do.** Nothing. If your game logs new warnings, fix the key each one names. A loader of your own can describe its format as a `flint_core::toml_util::Shape` and log what `check_keys` returns.

### `engine/` is 169 MB instead of 390 MB; `testModels/` and some example textures are gone or renamed (#759)
**What changed.** The engine tree a game carries as `engine/` lost its duplicates and its 4K textures (ADR 0122). `testModels/` is deleted: its models were copies of `examples/models/`, and `DoubleSidedCard.glb` is now `tests/fixtures/models/`. In `examples/`, `textures/wood_planks.png` is now `textures/wood_planks.jpg` (2048 texels, was 4096), `wooden_bench.glb` and `Lantern.glb` carry 2048-texel JPEG colour maps, `rolling_meadow/` uses `terrain/`'s four layer textures, and `worn_planks_diff_4k.jpg`, the Poliigon normal, roughness, displacement, AO and metallic maps, `mug2.glb` and `CubeWithTexture.glb` (read by no scene) are deleted. The book's PNGs are recompressed, same pixels. The engine's CI now fails a texture over 2048 texels or 3 MB, a file over 8 MB, or one binary tracked twice.
**What to do.** If a game names `engine/testModels/...`, `engine/examples/textures/wood_planks.png` or one of the deleted files, point it at the file above, or copy the old one into the game. To keep `engine/examples`, `engine/docs`, `engine/tests` and `engine/showcase_game` out of a game's working tree (166 MB to 41 MB), use the sparse checkout in the game-project guide.

### `SceneRenderer::bind_scene` and `prepare(&world, &FrameInputs)`: one way to drive the renderer (#739)
**What changed.** A host that embeds `flint_render::SceneRenderer` now has two entry points the player and `flint render` use. `SceneBinding::new(scope)` registers the scene's user styles (before its `[post_process]` is read); `bind_scene(device, queue, &world, &binding)` sets the asset scope, forgets the last scene (style history, bone buffers, lighting levers, terrain, grass) and loads the scene's terrain and grass, masking a styled terrain. Each frame, `prepare(device, queue, &world, &FrameInputs { time, dt, .. })` takes every per-frame input and reads the world: a field left at its default is off that frame, so a world line or boil no longer outlives the frame that set it. The old fields and setters (`ocean_time`, `grass_time`, `style_dt`, `pixels_per_point`, `set_boil`, `set_shallow_wooze`, `set_world_lines`, `set_terrain_entity`, `update_from_world`) still work for now; a later #739 change removes them.
**What to do.** Nothing yet. A game that drives the renderer itself can move to `bind_scene` and `prepare` now (`crates/flint-render/CLAUDE.md`, "Host protocol").

### The egui 0.30 to 0.36 port covers `Shadow`, `scope_builder`, `Tooltip`, the `Visuals` corner radii and egui_plot 0.37 (#983)
**What changed.** [Upgrading the Engine](https://docs.chaps.dev/guides/upgrading-the-engine.html#egui-030-to-036) had the renames Flint's own code needed. It now has the ones a game's client hit too, each 0.36 form compiled against egui 0.36.2: `Shadow`'s `[i8; 2]` offset and `u8` blur and spread, `allocate_new_ui` to `scope_builder`, `show_tooltip_at_pointer` to `Tooltip::always_open`, `Visuals`' `window_corner_radius`, `menu_corner_radius` and `corner_radius`, `painter.rect`'s `StrokeKind`, `ScrollArea::scroll_source`, `TextEdit::frame(Frame)`, `Sense`'s methods, and egui_plot 0.37's named items. It names the version of each companion crate (egui_plot 0.37.0 goes with egui 0.36), says a `ScrollArea` now drags only on a touch screen, and points to egui's CHANGELOG for the rest. A correction: `StrokeKind::Outside`, not `Middle`, is where egui 0.30 drew a rect's stroke.
**What to do.** A game porting its own egui code: read the table again. One that already passed `StrokeKind::Middle` for 0.30's look draws its rect strokes half a stroke inside it; pass `Outside` to get it back.

### A game binary's root `Cargo.toml` names the engine's `[patch.crates-io]`, `mach-dxcompiler-rs` included (#982)
**What changed.** cargo applies `[patch]` only from the root of the workspace it builds, so the static-DXC patch #947 added never reached a game with its own Rust binary: on Windows flint-render failed with five `E0425`s against crates.io's mach-dxcompiler-rs. flint-render now asks for exactly Flint's vendored crate (`=0.1.947`), which crates.io doesn't have. A game without the patch stops in cargo's resolver on every platform (``failed to select a version for the requirement `mach-dxcompiler-rs = "=0.1.947"` ``) and never downloads or links mach's prebuilt. `flint check <game>` warns `missing-cargo-patch` with the line to add.
**What to do.** In the game's workspace root `Cargo.toml`:
```toml
[patch.crates-io]
mach-dxcompiler-rs = { path = "engine/vendor/static-dxc" }
egui-winit = { path = "engine/vendor/egui-winit" }
```
If cargo then says the lockfile is `locked to 0.1.7+2026.10.26-141dd7f.1`, run `cargo update -p mach-dxcompiler-rs` once. A scenes-and-scripts game: nothing. Book: Upgrading the Engine, Cargo.

### `cargo clippy` names a read past the vfs; the wasm list moved (#761)
**What changed.** The engine's root `clippy.toml` now lists the vfs reads
(`std::fs::read`, `read_to_string`, `read_dir`, `File::open`, `Path::exists`,
`is_file`, `is_dir`, `image::open`, `gltf::import`) and `toml::Value::as_float`
beside the wgpu seams, so native `cargo clippy` in the engine warns on them in
every crate the player links and fails in the five that draw. The wasm-only
rules (the std clock calls and winit's cursor grab) are
`scripts/wasm-clippy/wasm-only.toml`; `scripts/wasm-clippy/clippy.toml` is
gone, and `scripts/wasm-check.sh` builds its list from the two.
**What to do.** A game script or CI step that set
`CLIPPY_CONF_DIR=engine/scripts/wasm-clippy` should run
`engine/scripts/wasm-check.sh` instead, or point at the file
`bash engine/scripts/clippy-conf.sh --wasm <dir>` writes. A game's own crates
read their own `clippy.toml`; to hold the engine's rules, copy the root list's
`runtime` group into it. Otherwise nothing.

### `engine/specs/oak_tree.procgen2.toml` is `oak_tree_deep.procgen.toml`; the engine root's stray files are gone; `.gitattributes` marks text and binaries (#760)
**What changed.** The deep L-system oak spec was renamed `specs/oak_tree_deep.procgen.toml`, and its `[meta] name` is now `oak_tree_deep`, so it no longer shares `oak_tree_01` with `specs/oak_tree.procgen.toml`. It also loses `branch_levels` and `crown_shape`, which an L-system tree never read, so it builds the same tree. The engine root no longer holds `kaleidoscope.procgen.toml`, `sprite2d_test.png` or `stone_wall_01_{color,normal,roughness}.png`, which were a seed variant of `specs/stone_wall.procgen.toml`, a test image and that spec's generated output. `.gitattributes` now sets `* text=auto`, LF for `*.sh`, CRLF for `*.bat` and `*.cmd`, and binary for images, models, sound and fonts. `.gitignore` now ignores `render.png`, `*.profraw` and `*.stackdump`. The engine has no tracked symlink left.
**What to do.** If anything names `engine/specs/oak_tree.procgen2.toml`, the `oak_tree_01` spec from it, or one of the removed root files, point it at `oak_tree_deep` or regenerate the textures with `flint gen engine/specs/stone_wall.procgen.toml`. Otherwise, nothing.

### `PlayerExtension::ui` takes the frame's root `&mut egui::Ui` (#959)
**What changed.** The game UI hook takes the root `egui::Ui` that egui 0.36's `Context::run_ui` hands out, the shape of eframe's `App::ui` (ADR 0121). Panels show on it (`egui::Panel::left("inv").show(ui, ..)`) and windows and areas from it (`egui::Window::new("Quests").show(ui, ..)`: a `Ui` derefs to its `Context`). An extension with `fn ui(&mut self, ctx: &egui::Context)` no longer compiles. `root_ui(&mut egui::Ui)`, added by #946 for panels, is deprecated; the player still calls it, and its default calls `ui`, so a game overriding it draws as before.
**What to do.** Change `ui`'s parameter to `ctx: &mut egui::Ui` (or `ui: &mut egui::Ui`). The body compiles as it was: `show(ctx, ..)`, `ctx.input(..)`, `ctx.clone()` and passing `ctx` as a `&egui::Context` go through the `Deref`. Rename a `fn root_ui` to `fn ui`; rustc doesn't warn about it. Book: Upgrading the Engine, "The game UI hook".

### A script's translucent HUD colours draw darker: egui 0.36's blend (#958)
**What changed.** Script `draw_*` colours, text `stroke` and `glow` halos, world text and HUD sprites now premultiply their alpha as egui 0.36 does, in display values, like the rest of Flint's UI. Until now they kept egui 0.30's, which brightened every translucent light colour. A 50% white over black drew 188 and now draws 128, and a 0.3 cyan stroke turns dull blue. Opaque colours are unchanged, and so are dark translucent ones, such as shadows and black panels. Glows at 0.9 barely move. The `.style.toml` default `glow_color` is now `[1, 1, 1, 0.73]` (it was 0.5), and `flint init`'s HUD hints were retuned.
**What to do.** For a light colour that should look as before, raise its alpha to alpha^(1/2.2): 0.25 to 0.53, 0.3 to 0.58, 0.5 to 0.73, 0.75 to 0.88, 0.9 to 0.95. Leave dark colours alone. A colour over a ground as bright as itself can't be fully restored: brighten it. Table and measurements: the book's Upgrading the Engine page, HUD colours.

### DXC is linked into Windows builds; `flint dxc install` and `FLINT_DXC_DIR` are gone (#947)
**What changed.** DX12's shader compiler is now built into the binary on x86_64 Windows with MSVC: Microsoft's DXC v1.9.2609, built from Microsoft's source by Flint's `static DXC` workflow. flint-render's new default feature `static-dxc` links it. No `dxcompiler.dll` or `dxil.dll` ships, and DX12 takes wgpu's `Dx12Compiler::Auto`. The adapter line reads `DXC (static, v1.9.2609)`. `WGPU_DX12_COMPILER` is read by wgpu now, so `fxc` still forces FXC, and `dxc` loads `dxcompiler.dll` by name. The first build on a machine downloads an 80 MB archive once into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\flint\static-dxc`. Offline, set `FLINT_STATIC_DXC_ARCHIVE` to a copy of it. Linking needs MSVC 14.44 (Visual Studio 2022 17.14) or newer.
**What to do.** Drop `flint dxc install` from your build scripts, and the DLLs and `dxc-LICENSE-*.txt` files from your Windows builds. Ship `flint dxc notices --to <build folder>` (`THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES-DXC.txt`) beside your game's executable instead.

### `flint_render::wait_for_gpu` and `FLINT_GPU_WAIT_TIMEOUT`; `SurfaceError::OutOfMemory` is gone (#948)
**What changed.** wgpu 23 gave up on every GPU wait after 60 s; wgpu 30 waits as long as the GPU takes. Flint's waits now go through `flint_render::wait_for_gpu(&device)`, which has no limit unless `FLINT_GPU_WAIT_TIMEOUT=<seconds>` is set. A wait that reaches the limit panics and names the variable, so a hung GPU fails a CI run quickly instead of running into the job's time limit. `flint_render::SurfaceError` loses `OutOfMemory`, which wgpu 30 never reports. flint-render's GPU tests share their device side by side again (`FLINT_GPU_TEST_PARALLEL=0` puts them back in turn).
**What to do.** Nothing for scenes, scripts and styles. In your own Rust, remove any `SurfaceError::OutOfMemory` match arm, and replace `device.poll(wgpu::PollType::wait_indefinitely())` with `flint_render::wait_for_gpu(&device)` if you want the limit. Set `FLINT_GPU_WAIT_TIMEOUT` in CI jobs that render.

### wgpu 30 and egui 0.36: the port for a game's Rust, `PlayerExtension::root_ui` for panels, naga's new error wording (#946)
**What changed.** The book has the port from wgpu 23 and egui 0.30 ([Upgrading the Engine](https://docs.chaps.dev/guides/upgrading-the-engine.html)). egui 0.36 shows panels on a `Ui`, so `PlayerExtension` gains `root_ui(&mut egui::Ui)`, the frame's root `Ui`; its default calls `ui`, so existing extensions are unchanged. User style errors read in naga 30's words (``identifier: `x` `` for `'x'`), with the same file, line and hints: the `[images.<name>]` hint now follows the identifier, not the wording. `pack2x16float`, `unpack2x16float` and `quantizeToF16` pass `flint style check`. egui sends modifier keys (`Key::ShiftLeft`) as key events.
**What to do.** Scenes, scripts and user styles: nothing. A game's own Rust: follow the page. Move `SidePanel` / `TopBottomPanel` code to `egui::Panel` in `root_ui`, add `@interpolate(flat)` to integer varyings, and know that a `stylize::kit` style is trusted code with loop bounding off, so its loops must end (`FLINT_SHADER_CHECKS=all` turns bounding on).

### Flint is on wgpu 30 and egui 0.36: a game's own Rust and egui code moves with it (#945)
**What changed.** The engine builds on wgpu 30.0.2 and egui 0.36.2 (egui-snarl 0.12), from wgpu 23 and egui 0.30. Scenes, scripts, styles and user-style WGSL are unchanged; script HUD colours keep egui 0.30's premultiply, and text is a pixel different here and there under egui's new rasteriser. Flint's own shaders skip naga's loop bounding, which keeps ASCII as fast as before (`FLINT_SHADER_CHECKS=all` turns it back on). A Kuwahara `radius` above 32 draws at 32. `run_headless_frame`'s closure takes the root `egui::Ui`; `CurveEditor` and `GradientEditor` ids must be `Debug`.
**What to do.** A game whose own Rust calls wgpu or egui (an egui `PlayerExtension`, a `stylize::kit` style, its own window) ports to wgpu 30 and egui 0.36; #946 brings the migration note. A subtree game that builds for the web adds `egui-winit = { path = "engine/vendor/egui-winit" }` under `[patch.crates-io]` in its workspace root.

### `flint_render::AcquireFrame`, `TrustedShader` and `SurfaceError`; wireframe lines unbiased on DX12 (#944)
**What changed.** Ahead of the wgpu 30 upgrade (#950), Flint's windows take their frame with `surface.acquire()` (`flint_render::AcquireFrame`), which returns `flint_render::SurfaceError` instead of `wgpu::SurfaceError`, and `SceneRenderer::render` now returns that type too. Flint's own WGSL modules are made with `device.create_trusted_shader_module` (`flint_render::TrustedShader`); user styles are unchanged. The overlay, skeleton and skinned wireframe line pipelines no longer carry a depth bias, which wgpu 29 rejects on lines. Vulkan applies no bias to lines and the web build never set one; on DX12 with an NVIDIA GPU the frames were byte-identical too, but another DX12 driver may lose a few pixels of a line to the surface it traces (Metal was not checked). `flint_debug_ui::run_headless_frame` runs an egui frame for a test and clears its texture updates.
**What to do.** If your code matches on `SceneRenderer::render`'s error, match `flint_render::SurfaceError` instead of `wgpu::SurfaceError`. A game with its own window can take frames with `AcquireFrame`, so the wgpu 30 bump won't touch that code.

### ASCII creases mark two pixels, not a band, so shallow silhouettes keep their outline (#484)
**What changed.** The `ascii` style's edge pass rebuilt each surface normal from both neighbours, so a normal beside a depth step belonged to neither surface. Where a step was too shallow to count as a silhouette (the ground just behind a pillar's foot), the crease Sobel marked a band 4 px wide, its cells held more than `edge_max` of edge and were drawn as texture, and the outline broke. Each normal now comes from the neighbour on each axis nearer its own depth, so a crease marks the two pixels astride it. Outlines that went missing near contact with the ground, or along a curved silhouette over a nearby floor, now draw; a few interior crease glyphs on small curved props change. The shared `edges` input (`edges_view`, user styles' `inputs = ["edges"]`) is unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game with its own ASCII goldens may see a few edge cells move and should re-bless them after a look.

## 2026-11-13

### `flint goldens` sequence cases hold shimmer to a budget; `flint render --last-frame` (#478)
**What changed.** A `[[case]]` in a `*.goldens.toml` may give `frames` (with `orbit` or `camera_path`, and `frame_dt`) and `max_reversals` (optionally `max_flips`). Such a case has no golden: it draws the sequence on one renderer, counts the ASCII style's glyph flips and A-B-A reversals from its own grid, and fails when the mean a frame passes its budget. Its `report.json` entry gains a `sequence` object, and the contact sheet charts every frame. A sequence case refuses golden keys and sequence flags in `args`, and a sequence key without `frames` is refused. `flint render --frames N --last-frame` writes only the last frame, to `--output` itself. Existing manifests and renders are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. To guard a temporal style in your own suite, add a sequence case with a budget measured well under what a broken history reads.

### A glyph set's TTF needs only the ASCII it has; eleven new sets in `examples/fonts/` (#935)
**What changed.** A `*.glyphs.toml` naming a TTF or OTF used to fail unless the font had every printable ASCII character, which ruled out emoji, cuneiform and other fonts with no Latin letters. The atlas now takes the ASCII the font has; the glyphs the set's ramps and `[edges]` name must still all be there, and a missing one is still an error naming it. A set whose font has every ASCII glyph loads exactly as before. The examples gain eleven sets with OFL fonts and their licences beside them (`examples/fonts/ofl/`): `phosphor`, `typewriter`, `poster`, `geometric`, `stars`, `kana`, `brush`, `hangul`, `cuneiform`, `moon` and `runes` (book: concepts/glyph-atlas.md, "Sets in the examples").
**What to do.** Nothing. To use one in a game, copy its `.glyphs.toml` and its `ofl/<Family>/` folder (font and `OFL.txt`) into the game's `fonts/`. A font of another script wants `cell = 14` or more to read as letters.

### `flint goldens` keeps its cases' pipelines; `kit::shader` returns a `kit::Shader` (#911)
**What changed.** In one `flint goldens` process each case takes the
pipelines an earlier case built on the device (scene, post-process and
built-in style pipelines, keyed by format, sample count and WGSL text), so a
suite runs several times faster; the summary prints how many were built and
reused, and `--fresh-pipelines` builds them anew. `flint render` now leaves
out a user style an earlier render in the process loaded from a folder the
scene can't see (#426). For a game's Rust style: `stylize::kit::shader`
returns a `kit::Shader`, which derefs to the `wgpu::ShaderModule`, and the
`kit::fullscreen_pipeline*` functions return whatever their result is stored
as: a `wgpu::RenderPipeline` as before, or an `Arc<wgpu::RenderPipeline>`
that the cache may share.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a style stores `kit::shader(..)` in a
`wgpu::ShaderModule` field: change the field to `kit::Shader`, or call
`.into_module()`.

### The `ascii` style's line glyphs hold still in motion (#477)
**What changed.** With `temporal = "on"` (the default) the edge glyphs (`edges = 1`) now keep a history of their own, as the tone glyphs have since #277. A cell keeps part of last frame's edge length, direction and place (the square root of `smoothing`), and keeps its decisions (a line or not, which line, which of two cells a line near their border belongs to, which corner) until the edge has clearly moved past them, by `hysteresis`. On the motion bench (`flint render examples/ascii_motion.scene.toml --frames 181 --orbit 6.8755 --flip-stats`) glyph reversals fell from 38.2 a frame to 0.9, and flips from 210 to 102. In a fast pan a marginal line waits until it clears the threshold band: about 6% fewer line cells at 1.2 rad/s. A single render, `temporal = "off"` and `hysteresis = 0` with `smoothing = 0` draw edges as before, byte for byte.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game that tuned `hysteresis` or `smoothing` for tone should know they now steady the edges too; `edges = 0` still turns line glyphs off.

### A zoom-only camera cut resets style history, and so do the viewer's jumps (#474)
**What changed.** The player's camera-cut detector, which resets the history
of styles that remember frames (ASCII's steady glyphs), now also watches
zoom: an FOV or orthographic-height step of more than 1.25x in one frame,
four times faster than the frame before, is a cut, and so is any switch
between perspective and orthographic. A punch-in no longer carries the wide
shot's glyphs into the close one (2,974 of 14,400 cells at 2x on
`examples/ascii_cuts.scene.toml`, now 0). `flint edit` now resets style
history at Space, a projection switch and a hot reload: before, a fifth of
the viewport kept the old view's glyphs for as long as you looked.
**What to do.** Nothing. A script that tweens FOV smoothly keeps its
history. One that steps the FOV by more than 1.25x at once as an effect, not
a cut, and wants the glyphs held calls `set_camera_cut_detection(false)`.

### `flint render --watch` writes the PNG again on every save (#432)
**What changed.** `flint render <scene> -o shot.png --watch` keeps running after its first render and writes the same output (a still, or a `--sweep` sheet) again whenever the scene's folder, its `styles/` folders, its schemas or a file a flag names (`--look`, `--variants`, `--reference`, `--camera-path`, `--expression-timeline`) changes. A save of a user style's `.wgsl` is drawn on the renderer that drew the last frame, about 0.15 s from save to PNG on a small scene, where a fresh `flint render` takes over a second; other saves load the scene again on the same GPU. Each render prints `watch: render <n> in <s> s (...)` and then every user style's error. Hosts that draw one frame a save can call `flint_render::stylize::custom::poll_files_now()` so a user style's pass looks at its files at the next draw instead of after 15.
**What to do.** Nothing. To tune a style headless, add `--watch` and save; Ctrl-C stops it.

### DX12 compiles shaders with DXC, with FXC as the fallback (#912)
**What changed.** On DX12, Flint now compiles shaders with DXC, which is about
5x faster than FXC on the ASCII style. DXC needs `dxcompiler.dll` and
`dxil.dll` beside the executable, or in the folder `FLINT_DXC_DIR` names. A
copy anywhere else, `PATH` included, is never loaded. Without the two DLLs,
DX12 uses FXC as before and prints one warning. Every adapter line now names
the compiler: `flint render`'s new `GPU:` line, `flint goldens`' `adapter:`
line, F3, and `--perf`. `WGPU_DX12_COMPILER=fxc` forces FXC.
**What to do.** Run `flint dxc install` after a build. It copies the pinned
release's DLLs beside `flint.exe` and the test binaries. To ship DXC in your
game, run `flint dxc install --to <your build folder>`, which copies two files
plus their licence files. Read the licence terms in the book's GPU Selection
section first. Vulkan and the web are unchanged.

### The `ascii` style compiles in seconds on DX12 again (#910)
**What changed.** Since #654, a game drawing the `ascii` style on DX12 waited about a minute in a release build (25 s in a debug one) the first time ASCII drew: FXC, DX12's shader compiler, inlined the per-pixel corner test about 45 times into the glyph shader. Each edge cell's glyph is now decided once per cell, in two small passes whose texel the glyph pass and the edge debug view (`--debug-mode edges`) read. Under FXC the glyph shader now compiles in about 6 s (9 s before the corners), and the two new passes in under a second; `flint render --style ascii` on DX12 went from 30 s to 11 s on an RTX 4070. Frames are byte-identical: the same glyphs, corners and colours.
**What to do.** Nothing. A user style (`*.wgsl`) that calls a neighbour-reading helper per pixel can hit the same wall on DX12: decide per cell in a pass of its own.

### `flint edit --watch` no longer reloads the scene for a style's WGSL (#429)
**What changed.** Saving a user style's `.wgsl` or `.shader.toml` in a
`styles/` folder the scene searches no longer re-parses and rebuilds the
whole scene in the viewer. The shader rebuilds in place, as before, and the
world (selection, camera, skinned meshes, unsaved entity edits) is left
alone. A changed `.shader.toml` still reloads the scene once, a few times a
second at most, because the scene's `[post_process.styles.<name>]` has to be
read again against its new parameters. Any other file under the scene's
folder, a `.wgsl` outside `styles/` included, reloads the scene as it did.
**What to do.** Nothing.

### `flint edit`'s panels blend as the player's do; `RenderContext::hud_view` (#428)
**What changed.** The scene viewer (`flint edit <scene>`) drew its egui into the swapchain's sRGB view, so egui-wgpu wrote linear colour and the GPU blended translucent panels in linear light. A dark translucent ground came out grey: the user-style error card read lighter there than in `flint play`. The viewer now draws egui into the plain (non-sRGB) view, as the player always has, so every translucent panel in the viewer (the error card, F4, the view gizmo's disc, the spline editor's polygon) reads darker and the same as in the player. Opaque panels and the 3D scene are unchanged. `RenderContext::hud_view(&texture)` is new: the view an egui pass draws into, in `hud_format`, the format to build the `egui_wgpu::Renderer` for.
**What to do.** Nothing for a game. A game binary with its own egui pass on a `RenderContext` should draw into `hud_view` with a renderer built for `hud_format`, or its translucent colours read too light.

### A scene no longer draws a user style only an earlier scene's folder had (#426)
**What changed.** User styles stay registered for the whole run, so after a transition a scene's `[post_process.styles.<name>]` could reach a `<name>.shader.toml` that only the previous scene's `styles/` folder held, and look different from the same scene loaded first or drawn by `flint render`. Now the player leaves such a table out, with a warning naming the file, and `flint check` of a folder reports it for that scene whatever order it meets the scenes in. Of two styles of one name, the one nearest the scene still wins (#781). The rule is ADR 0112's #426 addendum; `flint_render::stylize::custom::drop_unseen` is the check for a game binary that builds its own config.
**What to do.** If a scene relied on a style from another scene's folder, copy the `.shader.toml` and its WGSL into a `styles/` folder that scene searches (`flint asset where styles <name> --scene <scene>` lists them). A copy added while the scene runs is drawn at the next reload.

### A user style whose `.shader.toml` doesn't load is on the error card (#425)
**What changed.** `flint_render::stylize::custom::errors()` now also lists each `styles/*.shader.toml` that registered nothing (a TOML typo, a `name` that isn't the file's, an unknown stage or input, a default outside its range), as `(style, message)` with the style named by the file and the message starting with its path, ahead of the loaded styles' errors. The player's and viewer's card shows it as "style 'x' doesn't load" within a poll of the save, and drops it when the file loads or is deleted; before, it went to the log only. `custom::load_errors()` gives just these. A host that read `errors()` as "styles that are registered" now also gets names `stylize::find` doesn't know.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless your own UI looks each name from `errors()` up in the registry: skip the ones it doesn't find, or call `load_errors()` for them.

### User styles: a `for` loop with constant bounds is built as written (#424)
**What changed.** Since #411 Flint has rewritten every loop in a user style's WGSL with an iteration guard (at most 4096 turns a run) and built naga's rewrite of the module. A loop the IR proves ends within that limit is now left alone: a counter that starts at a constant, is compared with a constant (a literal or a module `const`), is stepped by a constant and is written nowhere else, as in `for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++)` over noise octaves or `for (var x = -4; x <= 4; x++)` over kernel taps. A style whose loops are all like that builds its own composed text again, with no rewrite. Every other loop (`loop {}`, a bound read from a parameter or a texture, a counter the body changes) is still guarded. The loops that change ended within the limit anyway, so they draw the same. `custom::Report` has a new `bounded` count.
**What to do.** Nothing.

## 2026-11-12

### `[panels]`: several cameras and looks in one frame, laid out as a comic page (#880)
**What changed.** A scene's new `[panels]` table lays the frame out as panels. Each `[panels.panel.<name>]` has a `rect` (shares of the page), a camera (`position`, `target`, `fov`, or `follow` an entity) and its own `look` / `post_process`. Each panel is drawn at its own size with the full post chain, then laid on a gutter inside an inked border, with the HUD still over the top. `flint render` and the player draw panels. Scripts cut between layouts with `set_panels(map)`, `set_panel(name, map)`, `remove_panel(name)` and `clear_panels()`. `Camera` is now `Clone`. A host that draws frames itself calls `SceneRenderer::encode_frame_to` / `render_frame_to` (they take the world), and `set_panels(layout, def)` at each scene load. N panels are N GPU submissions, and a scene without `[panels]` draws exactly as before. `flint_render::panels` is the API. Demo: `examples/comic_panels.scene.toml`.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless your game embeds the renderer and wants panels. Then replace `encode_to` with `encode_frame_to` and call `set_panels` at scene load.

### Changing MSAA at run time no longer breaks woozy meshes, lighten echoes or soft edges (#735)
**What changed.** Turning MSAA on or off while a scene runs (`--quality msaa=4`,
the web's `?quality=`, a script's `set_quality`) used to leave four pipelines
at the old sample count: shallow wooze's (`shallow_wooze`, plain and skinned),
a living prop's `echo_blend = "lighten"` echo, and a soft-edged skinned part
(`set_entity_part_softness`). The next frame was a wgpu validation error, a
panic natively. Every scene pipeline is now rebuilt together, and frames are
otherwise unchanged. For code that builds on `flint_render` directly:
`RenderPipeline::new` takes a `SceneLayouts`, which now holds the three bind
group layouts and the material sampler, and `ParticlePipeline`'s buffers moved
to `ParticleBuffers`.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless you call those two types yourself: then make a
`SceneLayouts::new(device)` once and pass it in.

### Pencil `tone = "light"` leaves the sky as paper (#890)
**What changed.** Under `[post_process.styles.pencil] tone = "light"` (or `pencil_style { tone = "light" }`), a pixel where nothing was drawn (the sky, depth cleared to 1) now has tone 1, blank paper, whatever the sky's colour and whatever `brightness` and `contrast` say. Before, the sky kept the picture's own luminance, so a dark sky was hatched all over; the `rotoscope` look, which takes its tone from the light, did that on any scene with a dark sky. Terrain, grass and water are drawn surfaces and keep the picture's luminance as before. `tone = "picture"` is unchanged and still hatches a dark sky.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a look wanted a hatched sky under `tone = "light"`: switch it to `tone = "picture"`.

### `material.color` colours a model entity too (#884)
**What changed.** `[entities.x.material] color = [r, g, b]` (or `[r, g, b, a]`) used to colour only an entity drawn from its bounds; on an entity with a `model` it did nothing, and `flint check` said nothing either. Now it replaces the model's base colour, the way `base_color_r/g/b` does, and the GLB's texture is still multiplied in. Expanded GLB child nodes take their model root's `color`. A model under a bounds-drawn parent does not take that parent's box colour. What wins, first to last: `material_overrides` for the named glTF material, `base_color_r/g/b` (what `set_material_color` writes, so a script still recolours a model the scene gave a `color`), `color`, the GLB's own. A scene that carried a `color` on a model and got the GLB's own colour will now render in that `color`.
**What to do.** Render any scene that has `color` on a model entity and check it looks the way you want. If you want the GLB's own colour back, remove the key.

### Pencil's `clutter` keeps a grass meadow from inking as a black band; `rotoscope` turns it on (#883)
**What changed.** Pencil takes `clutter` (0..1, default 0) in `[post_process.styles.pencil]`, in `pencil_style` and on `--style pencil:clutter=1`. At 1 a contour fades where the lines round it already cover most of a disc two outline widths across and of one three times that: a field of small silhouettes such as a `grass_ground` meadow, whose blades had merged into one black band from the near edge to the horizon. The meadow draws as a sheet with its near edge and horizon line; ordinary outlines keep theirs. At 0 every line is drawn as before, byte for byte. The `rotoscope` look now sets `clutter = 1`, so its drawings change slightly on thin features (a small figure's limbs lighten, about 1% of pixels on the props bench).
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a rotoscope scene relied on the old fill: set `clutter = 0` in its pencil table to get it back. For a meadow under another pencil look, add `clutter = 1`.

### Pencil `transition = "draw"`: a drawing drawn on, stroke by stroke (#879)
**What changed.** The pencil style takes a new option, `transition = "fade" | "draw"`, in `[post_process.styles.pencil]`, on `pencil_style` entities and as `--style pencil:transition=draw`. `fade`, the default, is the old cross-fade: at blend 0.5 the whole drawing is there at half strength. Under `draw`, a blend between 0 and 1 is a front. The outlines grow along their length up to it, the hatching follows a stroke at a time, and the paper's wash comes last. Where the front hasn't reached, nothing of the drawing shows. It works with an entity's `blend`, `tween_style_param`, `blend_source = "tear"` and the table's whole-frame `blend`. At blend 1 it draws the same pixels as `fade`. Rust styles get the hook pencil uses: `StyleDescriptor::draws_blend(fn)`, which takes a blend away from the stage's mix and the framework's cross-fade.
**What to do.** Nothing. To draw a prop in, give it `transition = "draw"` at `blend = 0` and tween its blend to 1 (`examples/pencil_draw_on.scene.toml`).

### A reveal's tear can read as torn paper: `reveal_rim`, `reveal_rim_color`, `reveal_shadow` (#876)
**What changed.** Every style's reveal takes three more keys. `reveal_rim` (points, default 0) lays a band of bare paper on the style's side of the line, ragged and frayed as a torn sheet's white core is; `reveal_rim_color` sets its colour (unset: the style's `paper` colour lifted halfway to white). `reveal_shadow` (0 to 1, default 0) darkens the live side under the torn edge, longest below and to the right. All three work in `[post_process.styles.<name>]`, `--style pencil:reveal=0.5,reveal_rim=3,reveal_shadow=0.4`, `--sweep`, `set_post_style_param` and `tween_post_style_param`. At their defaults a reveal draws exactly as before. The three names are now reserved: a user style may not name a parameter or option `reveal_rim`, `reveal_rim_color` or `reveal_shadow`.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a user style of yours uses one of those names; rename it.

### A held entity's lights and emitters step with it (`motion_hold`) (#873)
**What changed.** Under `motion_hold` (a component on the entity or an ancestor, or `[post_process] motion_hold` / `--motion-hold`), a point or spot light the held entity carries now shines from where the entity was drawn when its tick began, so its pool of light steps with the drawing instead of gliding. A `particle_emitter` or `particle_effect` on a held entity emits from the held drawing: particles are born only there, not along the jump to the next drawing, so a moving emitter's trail comes out as beads. Emission over distance still counts the whole way, `inherit_velocity` still sees the smooth motion, and live particles still move smoothly. Directional lights are unchanged. A game binary that steps its own `ParticleSync` holds emitters only once it calls `ParticleSync::set_motion_time(t)` (and `set_motion_hold(rate)` for a whole-frame rate) with the clock it gives `SceneRenderer::set_motion_time`; `flint-player` does both. `flint_render::motion_hold::{tick, held_rates, ...}` are re-exports of `flint_ecs::motion_hold` now.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a scene wanted a held character's torch to glide: put `motion_hold { rate = 0 }` on the light or emitter entity.

### The `rotoscope` look shades by light and holds motion on twos (#887)
**What changed.** `--look rotoscope` now sets pencil's `tone = "light"` with `brightness = 0` (it was `tone = "picture"` at brightness 0.5), so a dark floor or costume in the sun stays blank paper and only shadows are hatched, on any scene. It also sets `[post_process] motion_hold = 12`: poses and positions are drawn on the same 12 ticks a second as the lines' boil, so animation under the look is on twos at 24 fps. Stills at time 0 are unchanged apart from the shading; physics and scripts still move smoothly.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene that wants smooth motion under the look sets `motion_hold = 0` in its own `[post_process]` (the scene's keys win over a look it names) or passes `--motion-hold 0`. To get the old shading, set `tone = "picture"` and `brightness = 0.5` in `[post_process.styles.pencil]`.

### F4 → Styles has a Reveal group for every style (#875)
**What changed.** Each style's section in F4 (and the player's F3 Styles panel) now has a **Reveal** group: a checkbox that switches the reveal on at `reveal = 1` (off takes the keys out and keeps them for the next time it goes on), the amount, a shape combo, the ink's colour picker and a slider for each `reveal_*` number, with the ranges and help of `--list-styles`. Copy TOML gives the keys back. **Commit to scene** now owns `blend` and every reveal key in the style's table, so one at its default or switched off in F4 is removed from the file. Before, a `blend` or `reveal` set in the file stayed there after a commit that no longer had it. Rust: `StyleSettings::replace_reveal(name, reveal)`, and `StyleDescriptor::table_keys()` begins with `RESERVED_KEYS`.
**What to do.** Nothing. Commit to scene now removes a `blend = 1.0` or a reveal key your table holds when F4 says the style has none.

### `flint edit` holds motion on twos: `--motion-hold`, and the previewer's Hold switch (#871)
**What changed.** The editors draw `motion_hold` now; until this change they ignored it. The model previewer (`flint edit model.glb`) holds the pose on ticks of the clip's own time, the one its timeline shows, so playing and scrubbing step through the same drawings. Turn it on with the Animation panel's **Hold** switch and rate, or with `--motion-hold 12`. A layer dial, a posed bone or a clip switch shows at once. The scene viewer (`flint edit scene.toml`) holds what a scene's `motion_hold` components and `[post_process] motion_hold` hold, on its own wall clock, and `--motion-hold` writes over the scene's rate. The viewer plays no animation and runs no physics, so there only a gizmo drag of a held entity steps. `SceneRenderer::release_motion_hold(entity)` lets one hand-made pose through a hold.
**What to do.** Nothing. A scene with no hold looks and costs the same in both editors.

### A glTF animation clip starts at its first key (#872)
**What changed.** Skeletal and node clips loaded from glTF now play from their earliest key. Blender writes frame N at N / fps, so a clip on frames 1 to 250 (every Mixamo clip through Blender) began at 1/30 s, and the engine held its first pose until then: frame 0 and frame 1 of a `--frame-step` capture or a `flint render --anim-time 0 --time` span drew the same pose while physics moved, and each loop paused for a frame on its seam. Every key now moves back by the clip's first one, so a clip's `duration` (and `AnimationSystem::get_clip_duration`, the previewer's scrubber, a non-looping clip's `on_animation_end`) is shorter by that lead-in, and a pose at time t is the one the file keys at t plus the lead-in. Clips already starting at 0 are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a clip relied on waiting before its first key: key a still pose at 0 to keep the wait.

### Pencil `tone = "light"`: hatch the shadows, not the dark paint (#869)
**What changed.** The pencil style takes a new option, `tone = "picture" | "light"`, in `[post_process.styles.pencil]`, on `pencil_style` entities and as `--style pencil:tone=light`. `picture`, the default, is the old behaviour: hatching follows the picture's luminance, so a dark material is hatched like a shadow. `light` divides the picture by each opaque mesh's own colour, so a brown floor in the sun stays blank and the cast shadows are hatched. One `brightness` (0 on the benches) then holds across scenes. While a row asks for it, the renderer draws a new "style albedo" pass after the scene, about 0.03 ms at 1080p. Terrain, grass, water, particles and shallow-wooze draws aren't in it, and keep the picture's tone. A Rust style can read the albedo with `.uses_albedo(..)` and `StyleFrame::albedo`. Existing renders and the `rotoscope` look are unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing. To shade by light, set `tone = "light"`. A `brightness` raised to lift dark paint can come back toward 0: `rotoscope`'s 0.5 still hatches the shadows under `light`, only lighter.

### A reveal mask for whole-frame styles (#406)

Every style's `[post_process.styles.<name>]` table takes a **reveal**: the style shows only inside a screen-space region, the frame it read shows outside, and a drawn line runs along the edge between them. `reveal = 0..1` grows the region from nothing to the whole frame; `reveal_shape` is `tear` (a ragged hole ripping open), `wipe`, `iris` or `panel` (a comic-panel rectangle); `reveal_x`, `reveal_y`, `reveal_angle`, `reveal_ragged`, `reveal_scale`, `reveal_edge` (points), `reveal_ink`, `reveal_boil` and `reveal_seed` tune it. `--style pencil:reveal=0.5` and `--sweep pencil.reveal=0,0.5,1` take it too, and `--style-time` steps the edge's boil deterministically.

New script call: `tween_post_style_param(style, param, target, seconds[, easing])` eases a whole-frame style's number (`reveal`, `blend`, a `reveal_*` key or a parameter) and fires `post_style_tween_done` with `#{ style, param }`. One call tears a live scene into a style: see `examples/reveal_tear.scene.toml`.

What to do: nothing, unless a user style names a parameter `reveal` or `reveal_*`. Those names are now reserved, and such a style fails to load; rename the parameter.

### Motion on twos: a `motion_hold` component, `[post_process] motion_hold` and `--motion-hold` (#405)
**What changed.** `[entities.x.motion_hold] rate = 12` draws an entity and its descendants on ticks, 12 a second: its drawn position and a skinned model's pose change only on each tick and hold in between, the way rotoscoped film holds its drawings. Only the drawing is held; physics, IK and scripts still see smooth motion. A style's `boil` steps on the same ticks, so `boil = 12` redraws pencil lines in the frames the pose moves. `[post_process] motion_hold = 12`, or `--motion-hold 12` on `flint render`, `flint play` and `flint-player`, holds the whole frame, the post time styles read included. Scripts: `set_motion_hold(id, rate)`, `clear_motion_hold(id)`, `get_motion_hold(id)`. A game embedding `SceneRenderer` with its own loop calls `set_motion_time(seconds)` each frame before uploading bones, or nothing is held.
**What to do.** Nothing; scenes without the component draw as before. Example: `examples/motion_hold.scene.toml`; book: concepts/stylize-passes.md, "Motion on twos".

### A `rotoscope` look, pencil's `grazing` key, and no more outline round a pencil frame (#404)
**What changed.** `--look rotoscope` (or `[post_process] look = "rotoscope"`) is a ninth built-in look: pencil lines on a blank white sheet, one layer of hatching only in the darks, boiling lines in the player. The pencil style takes `grazing` (0..1, default 0; also on `pencil_style`): at 1 a floor seen edge-on draws one horizon line instead of a thick dark band. Every pencil look used to ink a line round the edge of the frame; it no longer does, so a pencil render moves by a few pixels at its border and nowhere else. `layers = 0` is now cheaper (it skips the stroke-direction work), with the same picture.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless a game pins a pencil render in its own goldens: re-bless it after checking that only the border moved. To keep a sheet blank under a floor, add `grazing = 1.0` to its `[post_process.styles.pencil]`.

### `flint edit <scene>` honours `--width`, `--height` and `--no-grid` (#423)
**What changed.** The scene viewer, and the track editor `--spline` opens, ignored these three flags: every scene opened a 1600 x 900 window with the editor grid drawn. They now size the window and turn the grid off, as they already did in the model, procgen, texture, terrain and particle editors. Without them the viewer still opens at 1600 x 900 with the grid. A game binary or tool that calls `flint_viewer::app::run` or `run_editor` itself passes one more argument, a `flint_viewer::app::ViewerWindow` (`ViewerWindow::default()` keeps the old window).
**What to do.** Nothing, unless you call `flint_viewer::app::run` / `run_editor` from Rust: add `Default::default()` as the last argument. To capture a styled scene that reads depth without the grid in it, pass `--no-grid`.

### `PlayerExtension` hears the frame's stages and the scene, and brings its own script module (#754)
**What changed.** A game's extension gets the hooks the engine's own
subsystems have: `update(stage, ctx)` at each `flint_player::Stage` of the
frame (after the engine's subsystems), `scene_loaded(ctx)` and
`scene_unloading(ctx)`, `script_command(command, ctx)` to take a
command before the engine applies it, and `script_modules()` to declare a
script module of its own, enabled for the whole run.
`ExtensionCtx::set_module_state(value)` hands that module what its
functions read. Every new method defaults to nothing, so an extension
compiles as it did. They run only in the player: `flint render --scripts`
links no game's Rust.
**What to do.** Nothing. A game whose Rust registered raw functions can move
them into a module of its own (the player-extensions guide shows one), and
work that belongs after physics or after the scripts' UI can move from
`pre_update` to `update` at that stage.

### AI style guides are `styles/<name>.styleguide.toml`, not `.style.toml` (#420)
**What changed.** The style guides `flint asset generate --style`, `flint asset validate --style`, `[generation] style` and `flint gen --validate --style-guide` read now use the suffix `.styleguide.toml`. `.style.toml` stays what it also was: a UI class file beside a `.ui.toml` layout, which is unchanged. The engine's `styles/medieval_tavern.style.toml` is now `styles/medieval_tavern.styleguide.toml`. A guide still named `<name>.style.toml` loads, with a warning naming the file to rename it to; when both names exist the new one wins. A later release stops reading the old name.
**What to do.** Rename your own guides: `git mv styles/<name>.style.toml styles/<name>.styleguide.toml` (and under `.flint/styles/`). Scripts or CI that pass `--style-guide styles/medieval_tavern.style.toml` take the new path. UI `.style.toml` files need nothing.

### A game binary can add a Rust style: `stylize::register` and `PlayerExtension::styles` (#419)
**What changed.** `flint_render::stylize::register(&'static StyleDescriptor)`
adds a style written in the game's own crate, and a player extension's new
`styles()` hook hands descriptors to `add_extension`, which registers them. A
registered style is listed in F4 (tagged `game`), read from
`[post_process.styles.<name>]` and `--style`, drawn by the player, and listed
with `"source": "game"`. A taken name (a built-in, a loaded `.shader.toml`
style, another game style) or a taken `component` is refused. A user
`.shader.toml` style can no longer take a game style's name, and a hot
reload never replaces one. `PlayerExtension` gained a method with a default,
so existing extensions build unchanged.
**What to do.** Nothing, unless you want a Rust style: build a `static`
descriptor with `StyleDescriptor::new` and return it from `styles()`, adding
the extension before the event loop starts. Example:
`crates/flint-player/examples/cyanotype/`.

### `flint_core::wooze`, `ocean`, `spline` and `ray_mesh` moved to two crates (#754)
**What changed.** The wooze field is the crate `flint-wooze`, and the ocean's
wave spectrum, splines and ray meshes are `flint-geometry`, so an edit to
them no longer rebuilds every crate above the core. The paths are
`flint_core::wooze::X` to `flint_wooze::X`; `flint_core::ocean`,
`flint_core::spline` and `flint_core::ray_mesh` to `flint_geometry::ocean`,
`flint_geometry::spline` and `flint_geometry::ray_mesh`. Seven component
names moved with them: `OCEAN`, `OCEAN_CONTACT`, `WATER_BODY`, `SPLINE`,
`SPLINE_DATA`, `SPLINE_MESH` and `SPLINE_CHUNK` are in
`flint_geometry::components`, and `SHALLOW_WOOZE` in
`flint_wooze::components`. flint-core keeps no re-export: it can't depend
on a crate above it. `flint_player::{RayHit, RayMesh, RayScene}` are
unchanged. Scene files and scripts are unaffected.
**What to do.** In a game's Rust, replace the paths above and add
`flint-wooze` or `flint-geometry` to its `[dependencies]`.

### `FLINT_STYLE_SHADER_DIR` hot-reloads the built-in styles' WGSL (#418)
**What changed.** The built-in styles' shaders are still compiled in, and a shipping build reads nothing else. With `FLINT_STYLE_SHADER_DIR` set to the engine's `crates/flint-render/src/stylize` (from a game: `engine/crates/flint-render/src/stylize`), natively, the viewer, the player and `flint render` read each built-in style's `.wgsl` files from that folder instead, shared ones such as `ascii_common.wgsl` and `../palette.wgsl` included. They build again each style whose file changed, about half a second after a save (470-672 ms over nine edits in `flint edit examples/watercolor.scene.toml`). A file naga rejects shows a `file:line:col` error in the style error card, and the last good shader keeps drawing. So does a shader wgpu refuses (a renamed entry point). The variable does nothing on the web.
**What to do.** Nothing. To tune a built-in look by eye, set the variable and edit the shader while the scene is open. Unset it when you're done: a warning at start-up says while it's on.

### flint-player's `music`, `procgen` and `living` features (#754)
**What changed.** The music, procgen at run time and living props are Cargo
features of `flint-player`, on by default, as `debug-hud` and `rhythm` are.
`music` plays a `music_session`; `rhythm` now turns it on. `procgen` makes a
`model.asset` or texture from a `*.procgen.toml` spec and gives extensions
`register_generators`; `living` (which needs `procgen`) runs living props,
